Solvable models in the KPZ class: approach through periodic and free boundary Schur measures
Takashi Imamura, Matteo Mucciconi, Tomohiro Sasamoto

Abstract
. We explore probabilistic consequences of correspondences between q q -Whittaker measures and periodic and free boundary Schur measures established by the authors in the recent paper [arXiv:2106.11922]. The result is a comprehensive theory of solvability of stochastic models in the KPZ class where exact formulas descend from mapping to explicit determinantal and pfaffian point processes. We discover new variants of known results as determinantal formulas for the current distribution of the ASEP on the line and new results such as Fredholm pfaffian formulas for the distribution of the point-to-point partition function of the Log Gamma polymer model in half space. In the latter case, scaling limits and asymptotic analysis allow to establish Baik-Rains phase transition for height function of the KPZ equation on the half line at the origin.



1.
Introduction

1.1.
One dimensional KPZ equation and stochastic heat equation

The Kardar-Parisi-Zhang (KPZ) equation
[
KPZ86
]
is the default model of growth of random surfaces and in one dimension it reads

(1.1)

\partial_{t}\mathcal{H}=\frac{1}{2}\partial_{x}^{2}\mathcal{H}+\frac{1}{2}\left(\partial_{x}\mathcal{H}\right)^{2}+\dot{\mathscr{W}},

where
\mathcal{H}=\mathcal{H}(x,t),x\in\mathbb{R},t>0,
is the height function and
\dot{\mathscr{W}}
is the space-time gaussian white noise with covariance
\mathbb{E}\left(\dot{\mathscr{W}}(x,t)\dot{\mathscr{W}}(y,s)\right)=\delta_{x-y}\delta_{t-s}
. This equation is notoriously ill posed due to the non linear term
\left(\partial_{x}\mathcal{H}\right)^{2}
and making sense of the solution of (
1.1
) has been one of the recent major accomplishments in the theory of stochastic analysis
[
BG97
,
Hai14
,
GIP15
]
. In this paper
we consider the Cole-Hopf solutions of the KPZ equation defined as

(1.2)

\mathcal{H}(x,t)=\log\mathscr{Z}(x,t),

where
\mathscr{Z}
is a
mild solution
of the stochastic heat equation with multiplicative noise (abbreviated as “mSHE” in the text)

(1.3)

\partial_{t}\mathscr{Z}=\frac{1}{2}\partial_{x}^{2}\mathscr{Z}+\mathscr{Z}\dot{\mathscr{W}}.

The correct prescription to interpret the noise term in the right hand side is discussed
[
BC95
]
, whereas for an introduction to mild solutions see
[
BC95
,
Qua13
]
and references therein.
Throughout the text we will mostly discuss the solution of the mSHE
\mathscr{Z}
rather than
\mathcal{H}
, as this allows to phrase our statements rigorously. In all cases the use of the Cole-Hopf transformation (
1.2
) will be justified.

Interest in the KPZ equation has, in the last decades, been motivated also by the fact that certain geometries and initial conditions allow to write (and sometimes prove) exact formulas for the distribution of the height function
\mathcal{H}

[
ACQ11
,
CLDR10
,
Dot10
,
SS10
,
LDC12
,
GLD12
,
IS13
,
BCFV15
,
BBC16
,
BBCW18
,
KLD20
,
BKLD20
,
DNKLDT20
,
BLD21
]
. In this paper we deal with two such choices of solvable geometries and initial data, which we introduce next.

1.2.
The KPZ equation in full space with narrow wedge initial conditions

The proper formulation of the problem of this case is given in terms of the mSHE which reads

(1.4)

\begin{cases}\partial_{t}\mathscr{Z}=\frac{1}{2}\partial_{x}^{2}\mathscr{Z}+\mathscr{Z}\dot{\mathscr{W}},\qquad x\in\mathbb{R},t\in\mathbb{R}_{+},\\
\mathscr{Z}(x,0)=\delta_{0}(x),\end{cases}

where
\delta_{0}
is the Dirac delta function.
Heuristically, for the function
\mathcal{H}
, the initial data in (
1.4
) represents a situation where the random KPZ growth is started from a wedge
\mathcal{H}(x,0)``="-|x|/\varepsilon
, with
\varepsilon
infinitesimally small, which justifies the name. Under delta initial conditions the function
\mathscr{Z}
can be interpreted as the point-to-point partition function of the
continuum directed random polymer

[
ACQ11
,
AKQ14
]
, which is a model of a polymer in 1+1 dimension propagating through a space-time in uncorrelated random environment. This is evident expressing the solution of (
1.4
) by the Feyman-Kac formula

(1.5)

\mathscr{Z}(x,t)=\mathbb{E}\left[:\exp:\left\{\int_{0}^{t}\dot{\mathscr{W}}(\mathscr{B}(s),s)\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}s\right\}\bigg|\mathscr{B}(0)=0,\,\mathscr{B}(t)=x\right],

where
:\exp:
indicates the Wick ordered exponential and the expectation is taken only over the brownian bridge
\mathscr{B}
. In this way the integral in (
1.5
) has the natural interpretation of the random energy of the polymer
\mathscr{B}
.

The first exact solution of the mSHE (
1.4
) became available in 2010 after the breakthrough result of four different groups
[
ACQ11
,
Dot10
,
CLDR10
,
SS10
]
. Famously, this solution expresses the Laplace transform of the probability distribution of
\mathscr{Z}
as a Fredholm determinant.

Theorem 1.1
.

Consider
\mathscr{Z}(x,t)
the solution of the stochastic heat equation (
1.4
). Then, we have

(1.6)

\mathbb{E}\left[e^{-e^{-s+\log\mathscr{Z}(0,t)+t/24}}\right]=\det(1-\mathscr{K}_{\mathrm{mSHE}})_{\mathbb{L}^{2}(s,+\infty)},

where
\mathscr{K}_{\mathrm{mSHE}}
is the linear operator with integral kernel

(1.7)

\mathscr{K}_{\mathrm{mSHE}}(x,y)=\int_{\mathrm{i}\mathbb{R}-d}\frac{\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}Z}{2\pi\mathrm{i}}\int_{\mathrm{i}\mathbb{R}+d}\frac{\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}W}{2\pi\mathrm{i}}e^{-\frac{t}{2}\left(\frac{Z^{3}}{3}-\frac{W^{3}}{3}\right)+Zx-Wy}\frac{\pi}{\sin[\pi(W-Z)]}

and for the integration contours we choose
d\in(0,1/2)
.

Remark 1.2
.

The kernel
\mathscr{K}_{\mathrm{mSHE}}

can be written as a “positive temperature variant” of the Airy kernel,

(1.8)

\mathscr{K}_{\mathrm{mSHE}}(x,y)=\int_{\mathbb{R}}\frac{{\rm Ai}((x+\xi)/\gamma_{t}){\rm Ai}((y+\xi)/\gamma_{t})}{1+e^{-\xi}}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\xi,

with
\gamma_{t}=(t/2)^{1/3}
.

From a physical standpoint the explicit solution of (
1.4
) is relevant as it has allowed to rigorously establish the large time asymptotic limit

(1.9)

\mathcal{H}(0,t)\approx-\frac{t}{24}+\frac{t^{1/3}}{2^{1/3}}\chi_{\mathrm{GUE}},

where
\chi_{\mathrm{GUE}}
follows the GUE Tracy-Widom distrubution
[
TW94
]
. Historically, the anomalous scaling exponent
1/3
had been predicted in
[
KPZ86
,
HH85
]
, whereas more precise conjectures about the universal fluctuations
for a large class of models (KPZ universality class)
were formulated
[
PS02
]
after the works
[
BDJ99
,
Joh00
]
. For the case of the KPZ equation (
1.9
) confirms the predictions from the KPZ scaling theory.

In this paper we will present a derivation of the solution of
theorem

1.1
which is conceptually different from ideas used in literature since 2010. The advantage of our techniques, compared to pre-existing methods, is that they explain the emergence of Fredholm determinants, by establishing an a priori correspondence between the height function and the edge statistics of a free fermion at positive temperature.
Moreover, methods developed in this paper can be applied not only to the study of the mSHE/KPZ equation, but also to a class of discrete models in the KPZ class. These are the ASEP, the
q
-PushTASEP, or the Log Gamma polymer and we will discuss this aspect more in
section

1.4
. More remarkably our methods can be naturally adapted to the more challenging task of treating the mSHE/KPZ equation in half space, as we discuss in the next subsection. See also
section

1.6
for more detailed account about novelty of our work.

1.3.
The KPZ equation in half space with narrow wedge initial conditions.

In this case the problem is phrased in terms of the mSHE as

(1.10)

\begin{cases}\partial_{t}\mathscr{Z}=\frac{1}{2}\partial_{x}^{2}\mathscr{Z}+\mathscr{Z}\dot{\mathscr{W}},\qquad x\in\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0},t\in\mathbb{R}_{+},\\
\mathscr{Z}(x,0)=\delta_{0}(x),\qquad\left(\partial_{x}-\omega\right)\mathscr{Z}(x,t)\bigr|_{x=0}=0.\end{cases}

The study of the well-posedness of (
1.10
) was done in
[
Wu20
,
Par19b
]
and in particular a mild solution of (
1.10
) exists, is unique and it is almost surely positive. This justifies the definition of
\mathcal{H}^{\mathrm{hs}}=\log\mathscr{Z}^{\mathrm{hs}}
as a solution of the KPZ equation in half space with initial conditions consisting in a narrow wedge peaked at the origin. The Robin boundary conditions of (
1.10
) become, for the height function
\mathcal{H}^{\mathrm{hs}}
the Neumann boundary conditions

(1.11)

\partial_{x}\mathcal{H}^{\mathrm{hs}}(x,t)\big|_{x=0}=\omega.

A physical realization of such boundary condition was proposed in
[
IT18
]
.
As in the full space case the solution
\mathscr{Z}^{\mathrm{hs}}
of (
1.10
) can also be interpreted as a point-to-point partition function of the continuum random polymer propagating in the half line
[0,+\infty)
with an interaction produced by a wall at the origin. Again, this is evident from the Feynman-Kac representation

(1.12)

\mathscr{Z}^{\mathrm{hs}}(x,t)\\
=\mathbb{E}\left[:\exp:\left\{\int_{0}^{t}\left(\dot{\mathscr{W}}(\mathscr{B}^{\mathrm{hs}}(s),s)-\omega\delta_{0}(\mathscr{B}^{\mathrm{hs}}(s))\right)\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}s\right\}\bigg|\mathscr{B}^{\mathrm{hs}}(0)=0,\,\mathscr{B}^{\mathrm{hs}}(t)=x\right],

where the expectation is taken over the reflected Brownian bridge
\mathscr{B}^{\mathrm{hs}}
, i.e. a Brownian bridge conditioned to remain nonnegative at all times. From (
1.12
) we observe that the interaction with the wall, modulated by the parameter
\omega
, is attractive when
\omega<0
and repulsive for
\omega>0
.

1.3.1.
Fredholm pfaffian solution.

The truly novel part of our results consists in the derivation of Fredholm pfaffian formulas for solvable models related to the half space KPZ equation. The typical approach to this problem has been that of leveraging integrable strucures of the continuum directed polymer model, which will be discussed in the next subsection, to obtain exact formulas for the partition funciton. This route involves the use of complicated and mathematically non-rigorous Bethe ansatz computations that only recently have led to manageable, although conjectural, pfaffian formulas; see
[
GLD12
,
BBC16
,
KLD20
,
DNKLDT20
]
.
Alternatively, in the more general case of the Log Gamma polymer model, exact formulas for the distribution of the partition function have been found through combinatorial techniques in
[
OSZ14
]
or in
[
BBC20
]
using eigenrelations of Macdonald polynomials. These last results, although mathematically rigorous have the disadvantage to be hard to manage and did not lead to a proof of asymptotic results. The only mathematically rigorous derivation of a pfaffian solution of (
1.10
), prior to this paper, was given in
[
BBCW18
]
, where the authors managed to study the case
\omega=-\frac{1}{2}
, through a matching between the Laplace transform of the KPZ equation and a nonlocal average of a pfaffian point process.
In this paper we overcome these technical difficulties by taking a different point of view and establishing a direct and a priori corresondence between KPZ models in half space and explicit pfaffian point processes. This allows us to study the full range
\omega\in\mathbb{R}
.

For the next theorem we recall the standard notation

(1.13)

J=\left(\begin{matrix}0&1\\
-1&0\end{matrix}\right),

and we use the convention that
J(x,y)=\mathbf{1}_{x=y}J
.

Theorem 1.3
.

Consider
\mathscr{Z}^{\mathrm{hs}}(x,t)
the solution of the half space stochastic heat equation (
1.10
) with initial conditions
\mathscr{Z}^{\mathrm{hs}}(x,0)=\delta_{x}
and boundary parameter
\omega>-1/2
. Then, we have

(1.14)

\mathbb{E}\left[e^{-e^{-s+\log\mathscr{Z}^{\mathrm{hs}}(0,t)+t/24}}\right]=\mathrm{Pf}\left[J-\mathscr{L}_{\mathrm{mSHE}}\right]_{\mathbb{L}^{2}(s,+\infty)},

where
\mathscr{L}_{\mathrm{mSHE}}
is the
2\times 2
matrix linear operator with integral kernel

(1.15)

\mathscr{L}_{\mathrm{mSHE}}(X,Y)=\left(\begin{matrix}\mathscr{K}^{\mathrm{hs}}(X,Y)&-\partial_{y}\mathscr{K}^{\mathrm{hs}}(X,Y)\\
-\partial_{x}\mathscr{K}^{\mathrm{hs}}(X,Y)&\partial_{x}\partial_{y}\mathscr{K}^{\mathrm{hs}}(X,Y)\end{matrix}\right),

with

(1.16)

\begin{split}\mathscr{K}^{\mathrm{hs}}(X,Y)=\int_{\mathrm{i}\mathbb{R}+d}\frac{\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}Z}{2\pi\mathrm{i}}\int_{\mathrm{i}\mathbb{R}+d}\frac{\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}W}{2\pi\mathrm{i}}&e^{\frac{t}{2}\left(\frac{Z^{3}}{3}+\frac{W^{3}}{3}\right)-ZX-WY}\frac{\Gamma(\frac{1}{2}+\omega-Z)}{\Gamma(\frac{1}{2}+\omega+Z)}\frac{\Gamma(\frac{1}{2}+\omega-W)}{\Gamma(\frac{1}{2}+\omega+W)}\\
&\times\Gamma(2Z)\Gamma(2W)\frac{\sin[\pi(Z-W)]}{\sin[\pi(Z+W)]}\end{split}

and we assume
0<d<\min(1/2,1/2+\omega)
.

A few remarks are in order.

Remark 1.4
.

In
section

7.3
we report equivalent expressions for the Fredholm pfaffian in the right hand side of (
1.14
). These had been previously derived in
[
KLD20
]
, although through non rigorous Bethe ansatz computations.

Remark 1.5
.

In
section

5
we will present pfaffian formulas analogous to that of (
1.14
) characterizing the distribution of the rightmost particle in a
q
-PushTASEP with particle creation and the free energy of the Log Gamma polymer model in half space. Unlike for the case of the mSHE, pfaffian formulas for these more general models had not appeared before in literature, not even at a conjectural level.

Remark 1.6
.

A theorem by Parekh
[
Par19a
]
relates the solution of the half space mSHE (
1.10
), which we denote by
\mathscr{Z}^{\mathrm{hs}}
and the solution of the half space mSHE with certain Brownian initial conditions and Dirichlet boundary conditions

(1.17)

\begin{cases}\partial_{t}\mathscr{Z}=\frac{1}{2}\partial_{x}^{2}\mathscr{Z}+\mathscr{Z}\dot{\mathscr{W}},\qquad x\in\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0},t\in\mathbb{R}_{+},\\
\mathscr{Z}(x,0)=e^{\mathscr{B}(x)-(\omega+\frac{1}{2})x},\qquad\mathscr{Z}(0,t)=0,\end{cases}

where
\mathscr{B}(x)
is a standard Brownian motion. Denoting by
\bar{\mathscr{Z}}^{\mathrm{hs}}
the solution of (
1.17
), then
[
Par19a
, Theorem 1.1]
states the equivalence in distribution

(1.18)

\mathscr{Z}^{\mathrm{hs}}(0,t)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathcal{D}}}{{=}}\lim_{x\to 0}\frac{1}{x}\bar{\mathscr{Z}}^{\mathrm{hs}}(x,t).

By this equality,
theorem

1.3
also provides an explicit formula for the probability distribution of
\bar{\mathscr{Z}}^{\mathrm{hs}}(x,t)
around
x=0
, although we are not going to state such results in this paper.

Although expression (
1.14
), as stated, only holds for
\omega>-1/2
, Fredholm pfaffian expressions for the expectation
\mathbb{E}\left[e^{-e^{-s+\log\mathscr{Z}^{\mathrm{hs}}(0,t)+t/24}}\right]
are available for any
\omega\in\mathbb{R}
. They can be derived by analytic continuation, in the parameter
\omega
, although such procedure, especially for
\omega<-1/2
, turns out to be not completely trivial. Such results are presented in
theorem

7.3
.

1.3.2.
Limiting distribution and phase transition.

Characterizing the large scale behavior of the free energy of directed polymers in presence of a wall has been a problem considered in the physics literature since the work of Kardar
[
Kar85
]
. Varying the value of
\omega
, the partition function of the polymer is expected to undergo a phase transition, which needs to be characterized. This an example of the so called “depinning transition”; see
[
PS02
, Section 7.3-7.4]
. Non-rigorous explicit computations
[
KLD20
,
BBC20
,
DNKLDT20
]
have allowed to conjecture the large time behavior for the free energy of the polymer model, which stated in terms of the height function
\mathcal{H}^{\mathrm{hs}}
reads

(1.19)

\mathcal{H}^{\mathrm{hs}}(0,t)\approx-f_{\omega}t+\sigma_{\omega}t^{\zeta(\omega)}\chi_{\omega},

where
f_{\omega},\sigma_{\omega}
are explicit constants depending on
\omega
and

(1.20)

\zeta(\omega)=\begin{cases}\frac{1}{3}\qquad&\text{if }\omega\geq-\frac{1}{2},\\
\frac{1}{2}\qquad&\text{if }\omega<-\frac{1}{2},\end{cases}\qquad\text{and}\qquad\chi_{\omega}=\begin{cases}\chi_{\mathrm{GSE}}\qquad&\text{if }\omega>-\frac{1}{2},\\
\chi_{\mathrm{GOE}}\qquad&\text{if }\omega=-\frac{1}{2},\\
\mathcal{N}(0,1)\qquad&\text{if }\omega<-\frac{1}{2}.\end{cases}

Above
\chi_{\mathrm{GSE}},\chi_{\mathrm{GOE}}
follow respectively the GSE and the GOE Tracy-Widom distributions
[
TW96
]
, while
\mathcal{N}(0,1)
is a standard Gaussian random variable. As mentioned above, the only rigorous previous study of this problem was carried out in
[
BBCW18
]
where the case
\omega=-\frac{1}{2}
was confirmed.

In the next theorem we establish the phase transition (
1.19
), (
1.20
). Scaling
\omega
properly around the critical value
-\frac{1}{2}
as a function of another parameter
\xi
we find that
\log\mathscr{Z}^{\mathrm{hs}}(0,t)
possesses fluctuations described by a crossover distribution
\mathrm{F}_{\mathrm{cross}}(\,\cdot\,|\xi)
. This interpolates between the GOE Tracy-Widom distribution, which is recovered setting
\xi=0
and the GSE Tracy-Widom distribution, which is obtained as the limit of
F_{\mathrm{cross}}(\,\cdot\,|\xi)
when
\xi\to+\infty
. The explicit expression of
F_{\mathrm{cross}}(\,\cdot\,|\xi)
is given in
section

6.1
, where we also prove, in
corollary

6.8
, that it corresponds to a distribution described first by Baik and Rains in
[
BR01c
]
.

Theorem 1.7
.

Let
\mathscr{Z}^{\mathrm{hs}}(x,t)
be the solution of the stochastic heat equation in half space (
1.10
). Then, the following limits hold:

•

if
\omega\geq-1/2
, rescaling
\omega=-1/2+\frac{\xi}{2^{-1/3}t^{1/3}}
we have

(1.21)

\lim_{t\to+\infty}\mathbb{P}\left[\frac{\log\mathscr{Z}^{\mathrm{hs}}(0,t)+t/24}{2^{-1/3}t^{1/3}}\leq r\right]=F_{\mathrm{cross}}(r;\xi),

where
F_{\mathrm{cross}}
is the Baik-Rains crossover distribution of
definition

6.3
;

•

if
\omega<-1/2
, we have

(1.22)

\lim_{t\to+\infty}\mathbb{P}\left[\frac{\log\mathscr{Z}^{\mathrm{hs}}(0,t)+f_{\omega}t}{\sigma_{\omega}t^{1/2}}\leq r\right]=\int_{-\infty}^{r}\frac{e^{-u^{2}/2}}{\sqrt{2\pi}}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}u,

where
f_{\omega}=\frac{1}{6}+\frac{\omega}{2}+\frac{\omega^{2}}{2}
and
\sigma_{\omega}=\sqrt{-\omega-\frac{1}{2}}
.

1.4.
Hierarchies of solvable models

The solvability of the mSHE comes from the fact that the
n
point average

\mathbb{E}[\mathscr{Z}(x_{1},t)\cdots\mathscr{Z}(x_{n},t)]

solves the imaginary time delta Bose gas with
n
particles. This idea, first formulated in
[
Kar87
]
, was used directly by
[
Dot10
,
CLDR10
]
, to implement the celebrated “replica method” and to derive an explicit formula for the moment generating function of
\mathscr{Z}(x,t)
. Since moments of
\mathscr{Z}(x,t)
are highly diverging (see
[
BCS14
, Section A.4]
), treating directly the mSHE through replica method yields expressions that cannot be justified mathematically and therefore other routes to produce explicit formulas are needed. The standard procedure to circumvent this problem is that of considering, in place of
\mathscr{Z}
or
\mathcal{H}
, certain more regular discretizations which preserve integrability
[
BCS14
]
. Such regularized models usually possess a ‘‘temperature” parameter
1
1

1

Here the use of the word “temperature” is motivated by the appearance of the Fermi-Dirac distribution. See
section

4.2
The same Fermi-Dirac distribution is present already in the kernel (
1.8
).

q
and a height function
h^{(q)}
, which under a proper high temperature limit converges in distribution to the solution of the KPZ equation
h^{(q)}\to\mathcal{H}
.

The most well studied of these reguralizations of the KPZ equation is arguably the Asymmetric Simple Exclusion Process (ASEP), in which case the parameter
q
modulates the asymmetry in the jump rate of particles; see
section

3.3
for more details. In this case the height function
h^{(q)}
is given by the integrated current through a location and as
q\to 1
convergence to the Cole-Hopf solution of the KPZ equation was proven in
[
BG97
]
. Considering such weak noise scaling was the approach adopted in
[
ACQ11
,
SS10
]
, where authors were able to leverage Fredholm determinant formulas found earlier for the ASEP by Tracy and Widom
[
TW08b
,
TW09a
,
TW09b
]
.

In the last decade, other discrete solvable models have been introduced in literature. The ones possessing the richest structure admit a formulation in terms of stochastic vertex models such as Corwin and Petrov’s Higher Spin Vertex Model
[
CP16
]
.
For all these models we can apply regularizations of the replica method, which consist in a combination of stochastic duality
[
Sch97
,
IS11
,
BCS14
,
CP16
]
and Bethe Ansatz
[
BCG16
,
BCPS15
,
BP18
]
.
A more indirect approach is that of using Macdonald processes
[
BC14
]
, where KPZ solvable models arise as certain marginals of a more general 2+1 growth process
[
BC14
,
OP13
,
BP16
,
MP17
,
BP19
,
BMP21
,
MP22
,
BBW18
,
BBC20
,
BBCW18
,
CD21
]
. In this last case the common approach is that of leveraging combinatorial properties of Macdonald functions to derive informations about the model.

In this paper we will mainly consider a particular regularization of the KPZ equation, that is the
q
-PushTASEP
[
BP16
,
MP17
]
. See Figure
2
below for an illustration of the model in continuous time. In its most general formulation this is, admittedly, a rather artificial model, whose definition is postponed to
section

3.1
, which nevertheless turns out to be the right one to focus on for three key reasons enumerated below.

Multivariate model.
The
q
-PushTASEP depends on a number of parameters which in the text we denote by
a_{1},a_{2},\dots,b_{1},b_{2},\dots
. Considering proper scalings of
q,a_{i},b_{j}
, we can transform the
q
-PushTASEP into several interesting other models, as schematically described in
fig.

1
. For instance taking a certain
q\to 1
limit one obtains the Log Gamma polymer model introduced by Seppalainen
[
Sep12
]
. This model is of interest on its own and in the last decade has received considerable attention; see
[
Zyg18
]
and references therein.

Half space variant.
The
q
-PushTASEP possesses a solvable variant that regularizes the mSHE in half space, called
q
-PushTASEP with particle creation
, introduced in
[
BBC20
]
. This is also a multivariate model depending on parameters denoted by
\gamma,a_{1},a_{2},\dots
, where
\gamma
governs the creation of new particles and in the KPZ picture plays the role of boundary parameter
\omega
. As we will show in the text we will be able to solve, i.e. write down exact formulas, for this half space model, for general choices of these parameters. This is remarkable since exact formulas for the ASEP in half space, that also approximates the mSHE (
1.10
), are only known for the particular choice
\omega=-\frac{1}{2}
and were found in
[
BBCW18
]
.

Relation with
q
-Whittaker measures.
Both versions of the
q
-PushTASEP hinted above have joint law described respectively by the
q
-Whittaker measure
[
BC14
]
and by the half space
q
-Whittaker measure
[
BBC20
]
, which are probability measures over partitions
\mu
of the form respectively

(1.23)

\mathbb{P}(\mu)\propto\mathscr{P}_{\mu}(a;q)\mathscr{Q}_{\mu}(b;q),\qquad\mathbb{P}(\mu)\propto\mathbf{1}_{\mu^{\prime}\,\text{even}}\mathscr{Q}_{\mu}(\gamma,a;q).

Here
\mathscr{P}_{\mu},\mathscr{Q}_{\mu}
are the
q
-Whittaker polynomials;
see
section

2
for precise definitions. This is important since the
q
-Whittaker polynomials have a nice combinatorial structure. In particular adopting a bijective approach, we were recently able to discover and prove certain new summation identities
[
IMS21b
]
relating the
q
-Whittaker measures with the periodic Schur measure
[
Bor07
]
and the Free boundary Schur measure
[
BBNV18
]
; see (
2.26
), (
2.27
) in the text. These are other measures over partitions
\lambda
having respectively the form

(1.24)

\mathbb{P}(\lambda)\propto\sum_{\rho}q^{|\rho|}s_{\lambda/\rho}(a)s_{\lambda/\rho}(b),\qquad\mathbb{P}(\lambda)\propto\mathbf{1}_{\lambda^{\prime}\,\text{even}}\sum_{\rho^{\prime}\,\text{even}}q^{|\rho|/2}s_{\lambda/\rho}(a),

where
s_{\lambda/\rho}
are the skew Schur polynomials, defined below in
section

2
. They have notable determinantal and pfaffian structures which we will recall in
section

4
.

Figure 1.
Schematic representation of hierarchy of models related to the
q
-PushTASEP.

1.5.
Methods

The location of a tagged particle
\mathsf{x}_{N}(T)
in the
q
-PushTASEP is equivalent in distribution to the length of the first row of a random partition
\mu
sampled according to the
q
-Whittaker measure
[
MP17
]

(1.25)

\mathsf{x}_{N}(T)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathcal{D}}}{{=}}\mu_{1}+N.

A summation identity discovered in
[
IMS21a
,
IMS21b
]
relates the first row in the
q
-Whittaker measure with the first row
\lambda_{1}
in the periodic Schur measure, up to a shift

(1.26)

\mu_{1}+\chi\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathcal{D}}}{{=}}\lambda_{1}.

Here
\chi
is an independent random variable with explicit distribution and this result is proven below in
theorem

4.10
. The combination of the previous two relations immediately grants Fredholm determinant formulas describing the distribution of the tagged particle
\mathsf{x}_{N}(T)
, borrowing known results on the periodic Schur measure
[
Bor07
,
BB19
]
.

Once formulas are obtained for the most general model, i.e. the
q
-PushTASEP, we will consider various scaling limits. A change of specializations of symmetric functions in the
q
-Whittaker measure will give us Fredholm determinant formulas for the ASEP, thanks to a notable relation between the Hall-Littlewood measure (seen as a particular case of the
q
-Whittaker measure) and the ASEP (seen as a particular specialization of the Stochastic Six Vertex Model) discovered in
[
BBW18
]
. A Fredholm determinant representation for the probability distribution of the free energy of the log Gamma polymer model will be derived through a
q\to 1
limit of formulas for the
q
-PushTASEP. In doing so a number of delicate estimates of decay for (ratio of)
q
-Pochhammer symbols will be required and we will present them in
appendix

B
. In literature, bounds for
q
-Pochhammer symbols are usually derived using certain complicated integral representations involving other special functions; see, for instance, the recent work
[
CK21
]
and references therein. In our case, all new results given in
appendix

B
will have completely elementary and self contained proofs, which is also a novel, although somewhat technical, aspect of our work. Considering a scaling limit, called
intermediate disorder regime

[
AKQ14
]
, of formula for the Log Gamma polymer model we will prove
theorem

1.1
.

As anticipated, we will also derive exact formulas for KPZ models in half space. Conceptually, the approach we follow mirrors that used to derive formulas in full space setting. Our starting point is the identity in distribution
[
BBC20
]

(1.27)

\mathsf{x}^{\mathrm{hs}}_{N}(T)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathcal{D}}}{{=}}\mu_{1}^{\mathrm{hs}}+N,

between the
q
-PushTASEP with particle creation on the left hand side and the half space
q
-Whittaker measure on the right hand side. We then relate, through a summation identity discovered in
[
IMS21b
]
, a particular case of the half space
q
-Whittaker measure with the free boundary Schur measure
[
BBNV18
]
. In formulas our relation reads

(1.28)

\mu_{1}^{\mathrm{hs}}+\chi\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathcal{D}}}{{=}}\lambda_{1}^{\mathrm{hs}}.

Combining (
1.27
) and (
1.28
) we are able to relate the rightmost particle
\mathsf{x}^{\mathrm{hs}}_{T}(T)
of the
q
-PushTASEP with particle creation with an explicit pfaffian point process. Strictly speaking such correspondence only holds when the parameter governing the creation of new particles, which plays the role of boundary parameter in the KPZ picture, is set to
\gamma=0
. To extend the correspondence to all non-negative values of
\gamma
we will use a special symmetry of the half space
q
-Whittaker measure found in
[
BBC20
]
; see
proposition

2.7
in the text. In this way we find a Fredholm pfaffian representation for the probability distribution of
\mathsf{x}_{T}^{\mathrm{hs}}(T)
valid for a general choice of parameters. We then move to consider scaling limits of our formula for the
q
-PushTASEP. Taking the
q\to 1
limit already considered in full space setting we derive a Fredholm pfaffian representation for the probability distribution of the point-to-point partition function of the Log Gamma polymer in half space. The asymptotic analysis in this case builds up on the study made for the full space model, although it ends up being slightly more involved; also in this case technical estimates are needed and they are reported in
appendix

B
. Formulas we get for the Log Gamma polymer model are amenable to a subsequent scaling limit, considered in
[
Wu20
]
, under which the polymer partition function reduces to the solution
\mathscr{Z}^{\mathrm{hs}}
of the half space mSHE (
1.10
) and in this way we prove
theorem

1.3
.

In
section

6
and
section

7
we will perform the large time-space asymptotic limits of our Fredholm determinant and pfaffian formulas. In this way we recover GUE limiting fluctuations results for the Log Gamma polymer model in full space. The emphasis in the proof we provide should be on the simplicity of the asymptotic analysis, given by the “free fermionic” nature of our Fredholm operator. This should be compared with other existing techniques (see e.g.
[
BCD21
]
), where a rigorous application of the saddle point method requires involved manipulations of integral contours of the Fredholm kernel.

The asymptotic analysis of our Fredholm pfaffian formulas will be performed employing a standard saddle point analysis. We will focus on three special cases. In
section

6.3
we will consider the asymptotic limit of Fredholm pfaffian formulas for the half space
q
-Whittaker measure for
q=0
. Doing so we will lay out the generic structure of the asymptotic analysis of our pfaffian formulas and we will additionally prove a new representation of the Baik-Rains crossover distribution originally defined in
[
BR01c
]
. Subsequently we will compute the thermodynamic limit of the polymer partition function for the half space Log Gamma polymer model, proving Baik-Rains phase transition. Analogous computations will be reported in
section

7.4
, where we report the proof of
theorem

1.7
.

1.6.
Novelty of our approach.

Lastly, let us emphasize how our techniques differ from those available in the pre-existing literature and what are the advantages of our approach. In simple terms we have implemented a bijective approach to the study of the
q
-Whittaker measure, and considering symmetries of our construction we have established correspondences between solvable KPZ models in full and half space with explicit determinantal and pfaffian point processes of free fermionic origin.
At the level of formulas, correspondences between KPZ models and free fermions at positive temperature had been known for a long time, since the works of
[
ACQ11
,
Dot10
,
CLDR10
,
SS10
]
in 2010, because the kernel (
1.8
) for the solution of the KPZ equation (
1.4
) can be interpreted as the correlation kernel for a positive temperature free fermion (see for instance
[
DLDMS15
]
for an early application).
The correspondence is formulated in a slightly different manner by Borodin and Gorin
[
BG16
]
, who understood the Fredholm determinant solution of the KPZ equation as a
multiplicative nonlocal statistics
of the Airy point process.
The latter holds more in general for solvable regularizations of the KPZ equation, as found by Borodin in
[
Bor18
]
and was extended in half space setting, for the particular case of ASEP, by Barraquand, Borodin, Corwin, Wheeler
[
BBCW18
]
to study the KPZ equation on the half line with critical boundary parameter
\omega=-\frac{1}{2}
.

The correspondence we establish and use in this paper are conceptually different. The bijection connecting
q
-Whittaker measure and periodic Schur measure, which we found in
[
IMS21b
]
, translates to a connection between KPZ models and free fermion at positive tempereture, and gives precise relations between
local observables
in both models. Once the connection is made, one immediately concludes the existence of Fredholm determinant and pfaffian formulas, without doing any further calculation.

Our new approach proposed in this paper to study KPZ models can be considered as “positive temperature” version of the works of Johansson
[
Joh00
]
and of Baik and Rains
[
BR01a
,
BR01b
,
BR01c
]
around 2000. Johansson applied the Robinson-Schensted-Knuth (RSK) correspondence to map the problem of directed polymer at zero temperature, or the totally asymmetric simple exclusion process (TASEP), in full space to a free fermion at zero temperature. Baik and Rains used the symmetries of RSK correspondence to study the above models in half space. The ideas and methods in these works are quite powerful and have been applied to many models in various settings but are not suited for studying positive temperature models, for which different methodologies using Bethe ansatz or Macdonald operators have been developed
[
BC14
,
BCS14
]
. In
[
IMS21b
]
we succeeded in constructing a generalization of the RSK algorithm,
which allows us to
study positive temperature KPZ models by connecting them to free fermions at finite temperature, both in full and half space. Currently, our methods only work for the study of “single point” functions, as in the works of
[
Joh00
,
BR01a
,
BR01c
,
BR01b
]
. It would be of great interest to understand if these can be extended to extract information for “multi point” observables. For this task a more careful analysis of our bijective construction seems to be required.

1.7.
Outline

In
section

2
we recall basic notions in combinatorics and symmetric functions. There, we also define the
q
-Whittaker measure and the half space
q
-Whittaker measure. In
section

3
we introduce the solvable models in the KPZ class we study in the rest of the paper, i.e. the
q
-PushTASEP, the ASEP and the Log Gamma polymer. In
section

4
we define the periodic Schur measure and the free boundary Schur measure giving also Fredholm determinant and pfaffian formulas for them. In
section

4.5
we state the fundamental identities relating
q
-Whittaker measures with periodic and free boundary Schur measure. In
section

5
we state Fredholm determinant and pfaffian formulas for all the models introduced in
section

3
. Their asymptotic limit is computed in
section

6
where we prove convergence to the GUE Tracy-Widom distribution and to the Baik-Rains crossover. In
section

7
we will state and prove formulas for the solution of the KPZ/mSHE equation in full and half space. In
section

7.4
we will compute the large time fluctuations of the solution
\mathscr{Z}^{\mathrm{hs}}(0,t)
of the half space mSHE (
1.10
) at the origin establishing Baik-Rains phase transition. In
appendix

A
we recall basic definitions and properties of Fredholm determinants and pfaffians. Finally, in
appendix

B
we will prove useful bounds needed to establish formulas for Log Gamma polymer model in full and half space.

2.
q
-Whittaker Measure and its half space variant

Here we define the
q
-Whittaker symmetric functions and recall some of their properties and notable summation identities. In
sections

2.4
and
2.5
we define the
q
-Whittaker measures, which are important in the study of solvable models in the KPZ class.

2.1.
q
-Whittaker and Schur polynomials

Symmetric functions we consider in this paper are labeled by partitions, i.e. weakly decreasing lists of nonnegative integers
\lambda=(\lambda_{1}\geq\lambda_{2}\geq\cdots\geq 0)
such that
\lambda_{n}=\lambda_{n+1}=\cdots=0
, for
n
large enough. For a given partition
\lambda
we define its transpose
\lambda^{\prime}=(\lambda_{1}^{\prime}\geq\lambda_{2}^{\prime}\geq\cdots)
setting
\lambda_{i}^{\prime}=\#\{j:\lambda_{j}\geq i\}
. Skew partitions, denoted as
\lambda/\rho
, are also used and these are pairs
\lambda,\rho
, satisfying
\lambda_{i}\geq\rho_{i}
for all
i
. Two partitions
\lambda
and
\rho
are said to interlace if
\lambda_{1}\geq\rho_{1}\geq\lambda_{2}\geq\rho_{2}\geq\cdots
and this condition is denoted by the notation
\rho\prec\lambda
.

For the next definition we recall the notion of complete homogeneous symmetric polynomials in
n
variables
x_{1},\dots,x_{n}
,

(2.1)

h_{\ell}(x)=\sum_{1\leq i_{1}\leq\cdots\leq i_{\ell}\leq n}x_{i_{1}}\cdots x_{i_{\ell}},

where
\ell\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}
. By convention we set
h_{0}(x)=1
and
h_{\ell}(x)=0
for
\ell<0
.

Definition 2.1

(Schur polynomials)
.

For any skew partition
\lambda/\rho
and a set of
n
variables
x=(x_{1},\dots,x_{n})
, we define the
skew Schur polynomial

(2.2)

s_{\lambda/\rho}(x)=\det\left[h_{\lambda_{i}-\rho_{j}-i+j}(x)\right]_{i,j=1}^{N},

where the number
N
is large enough so that
\lambda_{N}=\rho_{N}=0
. Setting
\rho=(0,0,\dots)
we obtain the Schur polynomial
s_{\lambda}(x)
.

Next we present a notable
q
-deformation of the Schur polynomials. For a fixed parameter
q\in(0,1)
, we recall the notion of
q
-Pochhammer symbols

(2.3)

(z;q)_{n}=\prod_{i=0}^{n-1}(1-zq^{i})\qquad\text{for }n=0,1,\dots,\qquad(z;q)_{\infty}=\prod_{i=0}^{\infty}(1-zq^{i})

and of
q
-binomials

(2.4)

\binom{n}{j}_{\hskip-2.0ptq}=\frac{(q;q)_{n}}{(q;q)_{j}(q;q)_{n-j}}.

Definition 2.2

(
q
-Whittaker polynomials)
.

Given a skew partition
\mu/\varkappa
, the
skew
q
-Whittaker polynomial
in
n
variables
\mathscr{P}_{\mu/\varkappa}(x_{1},\dots,x_{n};q)
is defined by the recursive relation

(2.5)

\mathscr{P}_{\mu/\varkappa}(x_{1},\dots,x_{n};q)=\sum_{\eta}\mathscr{P}_{\eta/\varkappa}(x_{1},\dots,x_{n-1};q)\mathscr{P}_{\mu/\eta}(x_{n};q),

where

(2.6)

\mathscr{P}_{\mu/\eta}(z;q)=\mathbf{1}_{\eta\prec\mu}\prod_{i\geq 1}z^{\mu_{i}-\eta_{i}}\binom{\mu_{i}-\mu_{i+1}}{\mu_{i}-\eta_{i}}_{\hskip-2.0ptq}.

We also define the
dual
q
-Whittaker polynomials

(2.7)

\mathscr{Q}_{\mu}(x;q)=\mathdutchcal{b}_{\mu}(q)\mathscr{P}_{\mu}(x;q)\qquad\text{and}\qquad\mathscr{Q}_{\mu/\eta}(x;q)=\frac{\mathdutchcal{b}_{\mu}(q)}{\mathdutchcal{b}_{\eta}(q)}\mathscr{P}_{\mu/\eta}(x;q),

where

(2.8)

\mathdutchcal{b}_{\mu}(q)=\prod_{i\geq 1}\frac{1}{(q;q)_{\mu_{i}-\mu_{i+1}}}.

Setting
q=0
both polynomials
\mathscr{P}_{\mu/\varkappa}
and
\mathscr{Q}_{\mu/\varkappa}
become the skew Schur polynomials
s_{\mu/\varkappa}
and this gives a well known alternative definitions of
s_{\mu/\varkappa}
.

The
q
-Whittaker polynomials can be seen as a special case of the more general Macdonald polynomials
[
Mac95
, Section VI]
. These are special symmetric polynomials depending on two parameters
q,t
and denoted commonly as
P_{\mu}(x;q,t)
. Setting
t=0
we recover the
q
-Whittaker polynomial
\mathscr{P}_{\mu}(x;q)=P_{\mu}(x;q,t=0)
. Another interesting particular case of the Macdonald polynomials is given setting
q=0
and keeping
t
positive. These are the Hall-Littlewood polynomials, which are also of interest in the context of integrable probability and we will introduce them in the next subsection, albeit through a different route.

2.2.
Specializations of symmetric functions

The
q
-Whittaker polynomials enjoy the stability property

(2.9)

\mathscr{P}_{\mu/\eta}(x_{1},\dots,x_{n-1},x_{n}=0;q)=\mathscr{P}_{\mu/\eta}(x_{1},\dots,x_{n-1};q)

and this allows, through an inverse limit procedure, to define them in the algebra of symmetric functions
\mathbf{\Lambda}_{\mathbb{C}}
. In
[
Mac95
, Section VI.4]
, this is explained in the more general case of Macdonald functions. Implications of this observation include the extension of the notion of “variables” of a symmetric function, which then become evaluation homomorphisms of the algebra
\mathbf{\Lambda}_{\mathbb{C}}
.

Definition 2.3
.

A
specialization

\varrho
of the algebra of symmetric functions is an algebra homomorphism
\varrho:\mathbf{\Lambda}_{\mathbb{C}}\mapsto\mathbb{C}
. If
f\in\mathbf{\Lambda}_{\mathbb{C}}
we will use the notation
f(\varrho)=\varrho(f)
.

When dealing with explicit specializations it is convenient to define their action on the algebraic basis of
\mathbf{\Lambda}_{\mathbb{C}}
of power sum symmetric functions
\{p_{n}:n\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}\}
, setting the values
\varrho(p_{n})
. Rules for addition and scalar multiplication of specializations are straightforward from the definition and we have

(2.10)

(\varrho_{1}+\varrho_{2})(p_{n})=\varrho_{1}(p_{n})+\varrho_{2}(p_{n}),\qquad(c\varrho)(p_{n})=c^{n}\varrho(p_{n}),

for all specializations
\varrho,\varrho_{1},\varrho_{2}
and
c\in\mathbb{C}
. Three types of specializations will be considered mainly in this paper. These are:

(1)

alpha specializations
of complex parameters
x=(x_{1},x_{2},\dots)
, where

(2.11)

\varrho:p_{n}\mapsto p_{n}(x)=x_{1}^{n}+x_{2}^{n}+\cdots,

evaluates the power sum symmetric functions numerically at
x_{1},x_{2},\dots
and we always assume that
\sum_{i\geq 1}|x_{i}|<\infty
;

(2)

q
-beta specializations
of complex parameters
x=(x_{1},x_{2},\dots)
, where one sets

(2.12)

\varrho(p_{n})=(-1)^{n-1}(1-q^{n})p_{n}(x),

assuming again
\sum_{i\geq 1}|x_{i}|<\infty
. For brevity we will denote the
q
-beta specialization of a symmetric function
f
by
f(\widehat{x})
;

(3)

Plancherel specializations
of parameter
\tau\in\mathbb{C}
defined by
\varrho(p_{n})=\delta_{n,1}\tau
.

Evaluating
q
-beta specializations of
q
-Whittaker functions yields another remarkable family of symmetric polynomials (or functions)

(2.13)

\mathscr{H}_{\mu^{\prime}/\eta^{\prime}}(x;q)=\mathscr{Q}_{\mu/\eta}(\widehat{x};q),

called
Hall-Littlewood polynomials
; see
[
Mac95
, Section VI eq. (5.1)]
. In literature the Hall-Littlewood polynomials usually depend on a parameter
t
, which is
q
in the notation (
2.13
). Because of such simple relation between
\mathscr{P}
and
\mathscr{H}
functions, in this paper we will not emphasize their difference.

Remark 2.4

(Positive specializations)
.

For probabilistic applications we will be interested in
q
-Whittaker-
positive specializations

\varrho
, which are defined by the property that
\varrho(\mathscr{P}_{\mu})\geq 0
for all partitions
\mu
. These were characterized by Matveev in
[
Mat19
]
for the more general case of Macdonald functions. Adapting
[
Mat19
, Theorem 1.4]
to our case, we see that a specialization
\varrho
is
q
-Whittaker-positive if and only if it can be expressed as

(2.14)

\varrho=\varrho_{x}+\varrho_{\widehat{y}}+\varrho^{\mathrm{pl}}_{\tau}

where
\varrho_{x},\varrho_{\widehat{y}},\varrho^{\mathrm{pl}}_{\tau}
are respectively an alpha,
q
-beta and Plancherel specializations of parameters
x=(x_{1},x_{2},\dots)
,
y=(y_{1},y_{2},\dots)
and
\tau
such that
x_{i},y_{i},\tau\geq 0
for all
i
and

(2.15)

\sum_{i=1}^{\infty}(x_{i}+y_{i})<\infty.

Analogously
Schur-positive
specializations
\varrho
are defined imposing
\varrho(s_{\mu})\geq 0
for all
\mu
. In this case the Edrei-Thoma’s theorem
[
ASW52
,
Edr53
,
Whi52
]
, says that a specialization is Schur positive if and only if it is of the form (
2.14
), setting
q=0
in
\varrho_{\widehat{y}}
.

2.3.
Summation identities

The
q
-Whittaker functions possess a number of interesting properties and satisfy notable summation identities. Summing over products of two
q
-Whittaker functions one obtains the
Cauchy identity
that reads

(2.16)

\sum_{\mu}\mathscr{P}_{\mu/\nu}(a;q)\mathscr{Q}_{\mu/\varkappa}(b;q)=\Pi(a,b)\sum_{\eta}\mathscr{P}_{\varkappa/\eta}(a;q)\mathscr{Q}_{\nu/\eta}(b;q),

where

(2.17)

\Pi(a,b)=\prod_{k\geq 0}H(q^{k}a,b)\qquad\text{and}\qquad H(a,b)=\exp\left\{\sum_{n\geq 1}\frac{p_{n}(a)p_{n}(b)}{n}\right\}.

Setting
\nu=\varkappa=(0,0,\dots)
, identity (
2.16
) simplifies as

(2.18)

\sum_{\mu}\mathscr{P}_{\mu}(a;q)\mathscr{Q}_{\mu}(b;q)=\Pi(a;b).

Here
a
and
b
are generic specializations for which the function
H(a,b)
is numerically convergent. In the simplest case, when
a
and
b
are alpha specializations of finitely many parameters
(a_{1},\dots,a_{n})
,
(b_{1},\dots,b_{t})
, such that
|a_{i}b_{j}|<1
for all
i,j
, we have

(2.19)

\Pi(a,b)=\prod_{i=1}^{n}\prod_{j=1}^{t}\frac{1}{(a_{i}b_{j};q)_{\infty}}.

Summations over single
q
-Whittaker functions yield the
Littlewood identity

(2.20)

\sum_{\mu}\mathdutchcal{b}^{\mathrm{el}}_{\mu}(q)\mathscr{P}_{\mu}(a;q)=\widetilde{\Pi}(a),

where
\mathdutchcal{b}^{\mathrm{el}}_{\mu}
is defined as

(2.21)

\mathdutchcal{b}_{\mu}^{\mathrm{el}}(q)=\prod_{i=2,4,6,\dots}\frac{\mathbf{1}_{\mu_{i-1}=\mu_{i}}}{(q;q)_{\mu_{i}-\mu_{i+1}}},

and

(2.22)

\widetilde{\Pi}(a)=\prod_{k\geq 0}\widetilde{H}(q^{k}a),\qquad\qquad\widetilde{H}(a)=\exp\left\{\sum_{n\geq 1}\frac{p_{2n-1}(a)}{2n-1}+\frac{p_{n}(a)^{2}}{2n}\right\}.

In (
2.21
) the superscript
el
means “even length” as
\mathdutchcal{b}^{\mathrm{el}}_{\mu}
vanishes unless
\mu^{\prime}
is even, i.e.
\mu^{\prime}_{i}
is even for all
i
.
When
a
is an alpha specialization of finitely many parameters
(a_{1},\dots,a_{n})
we have

(2.23)

\widetilde{\Pi}(a)=\prod_{1\leq i<j\leq n}\frac{1}{(a_{i}a_{j};q)_{\infty}},

as it can be verified by evaluating the summations in the expression of
\widetilde{H}(a)
.

Interestingly, using homogeneity of Schur functions and their skew Cauchy identities it is possible to reproduce
\Pi(a,b),\widetilde{\Pi}(a)
appearing on the right hand side of (
2.18
), (
2.20
). We have
[
SS90
, Corollary 6.7]

(2.24)

\sum_{\lambda,\rho}q^{|\rho|}s_{\lambda/\rho}(a)s_{\lambda/\rho}(b)=\frac{1}{(q;q)_{\infty}}\Pi(a;b)

and
[
SS90
, Corollary 6.10]

(2.25)

\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\lambda,\rho:\\
\lambda^{\prime},\rho^{\prime}\text{ even}\end{subarray}}q^{|\rho|/2}s_{\lambda/\rho}(a)=\frac{1}{(q;q)_{\infty}}\widetilde{\Pi}(a).

Indeed, similarities between identities (
2.24
), (
2.25
) and (
2.18
), (
2.20
) are not merely a coincidence and their mutual relations were understood in
[
IMS21a
,
IMS21b
]
. We recall these results in the following theorems.

Theorem 2.5
.

Fix
|q|<1
and specializations of symmetric functions
a
and
b
. Then, for all
k=0,1,2,\dots
, we have

(2.26)

\sum_{\ell=0}^{k}\frac{q^{\ell}}{(q;q)_{\ell}}\sum_{\mu:\mu_{1}=k-\ell}\mathscr{P}_{\mu}(a;q)\mathscr{Q}_{\mu}(b;q)=\sum_{\lambda,\rho:\lambda_{1}=k}q^{|\rho|}s_{\lambda/\rho}(a)s_{\lambda/\rho}(b).

Notice that equality (
2.26
) represents a mutual refinement of (
2.18
) and (
2.24
). A similar mutual refinement between (
2.20
) and (
2.25
) is given below.

Theorem 2.6
.

Fix
|q|<1
and a specialization of symmetric functions
a
. Then, for all
k=0,1,2,\dots
, we have

(2.27)

\sum_{\ell=0}^{k}\frac{q^{\ell}}{(q;q)_{\ell}}\sum_{\mu:\mu_{1}=k-\ell}\mathdutchcal{b}_{\mu}^{\mathrm{el}}(q)\mathscr{P}_{\mu}(a;q)=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\lambda,\rho:\lambda^{\prime},\rho^{\prime}\,\mathrm{even}\\
\lambda_{1}=k\end{subarray}}q^{|\rho|/2}s_{\lambda/\rho}(a).

In
[
IMS21b
]
,
theorems

2.5
and
2.6
were proven bijectively only in the case where
a
and
b
are alpha specializations. Nevertheless, as pointed out in
[
IMS21b
, Remark 10.8 and Remark 10.14]
the same identities hold for general specializations following simple stability considerations such as (
2.9
).

2.4.
q
-Whittaker measure

The
q
-Whittaker measure

[
BC14
]
is the probability measure on the set of partitions given by

(2.28)

\mathbb{W}^{(q)}_{a;b}(\mu)=\frac{1}{\Pi(a;b)}\mathdutchcal{b}_{\mu}(q)\mathscr{P}_{\mu}(a;q)\mathscr{P}_{\mu}(b;q),

where
a,b
are (
q
-Whittaker) positive specializations such that the function
H(a,b)
is numerically convergent. In case
a
and
b
are
q
-beta specializations
\widehat{a},\widehat{b}
of parameters
(a_{1},\dots,a_{n})
,
(b_{1},\dots,b_{t})
, the
q
-Whittaker polynomials
\mathscr{P}_{\mu}(\widehat{a};q),\mathscr{P}_{\mu}(\widehat{b};q)
turn into the
dual
Hall-Littlewood polynomials labeled by the transposed partitions
\mu^{\prime}
and the measure
\mathbb{W}^{(q)}_{\widehat{a};\widehat{b}}
turns into the Hall-Littlewood measure
[
Mac95
, Chapter VI eq. (5.1)]
. This is another important measure in integrable probability and it is related to KPZ models as we will discuss below; for more on the subject see
[
CSV11
,
BBW18
,
BM18
,
BBCW18
,
BP19
]
. In this paper we will not introduce a different notation for the Hall-Littlewood measure and we will consider it as a particular specialization of the
q
-Whittaker measure.

2.5.
Half space
q
-Whittaker measure

Introducing the function

(2.29)

\mathscr{E}_{\mu}(\gamma;q)=\sum_{\eta}\mathdutchcal{b}_{\eta}^{\mathrm{el}}(q)\mathscr{Q}_{\mu/\eta}(\gamma;q),

we define, as in
[
BBC20
]
, the
half space
q
-Whittaker measure

(2.30)

\mathbb{HW}^{(q)}_{a;\gamma}(\mu)=\frac{1}{\widetilde{\Pi}(a)\Pi(a;\gamma)}\mathscr{E}_{\mu}(\gamma,q)\mathscr{P}_{\mu}(a,q).

Here
a
and
\gamma
are
q
-Whittaker positive specializations such that functions
\widetilde{H}(a)
and
H(a,\gamma)
are numerically convergent. Combining Cauchy identities (
2.16
) and (
2.20
) one can verify that the normalization constant in the right hand side of (
2.30
) is correct.

We will mostly be interested in the case when
\gamma
is a single variable alpha specialization. In such situation, the half space
q
-Whittaker measure possesses a symmetry that allows to distribute the parameter
\gamma
of the function
\mathscr{E}
on the specialization of the
\mathscr{P}
function.

Proposition 2.7

(
[
BBC20
]
Proposition 2.6)
.

Let
\bm{a}
be a
q
-Whittaker positive specialization and
\gamma
be a single variable alpha specialization. Consider a random partition
\mu
distributed according to
\mathbb{HW}^{(q)}_{\bm{a};\gamma}
and another random partition
\lambda
distributed according to
\mathbb{HW}^{(q)}_{\bm{a}^{\prime};0}
, where
\bm{a}^{\prime}=(a,\gamma)
. Then
\{\mu_{1},\mu_{3},\dots\}
and
\{\lambda_{1},\lambda_{3},\dots\}
have the same distribution.

We will be interested in the marginal
\mu_{1}
of the half space
q
-Whittaker measure as it relates to solvable KPZ models; see
section

3
. For this purpose the symmetry reported in
proposition

2.7
is very convenient as it reduces the study of the measure with specializations
\bm{a}
and
\gamma
to the simpler case when
\gamma=0
.

3.
Models in the KPZ class and
q
-Whittaker measures

In this section, we explain the relation between KPZ models and
q
-Whittaker measure, both in the whole space case and in the half space.

3.1.
q
-PushTASEP

The
geometric
q
-PushTASEP
is a stochastic interacting particle system introduced in
[
MP17
]
. In order to describe the random jumps of particles, we recall the
q
-deformed beta binomial distribution

(3.1)

\bm{\varphi}_{q,\xi,\eta}(s|m)=\xi^{s}\frac{(\eta/\xi;q)_{s}(\xi;q)_{m-s}}{(\eta;q)_{m}}\frac{(q;q)_{m}}{(q;q)_{s}(q;q)_{m-s}},

which is a probability distribution on
s\in\{0,\dots,m\}
, for any
m\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}
, whenever parameters
q,\xi,\eta
make the above expression nonnegative. Two such choices are given by
q,\xi\in[0,1),\eta\in[0,\xi]
and
q>1,\xi\in q^{\mathbb{Z}_{\leq 0}},\eta=0
. Setting
\eta=0,m=\infty
and taking
q,\xi\in[0,1)
, we obtain the
q
-Geometric
distribution of parameter
\xi
,

(3.2)

\bm{\varphi}_{q,\xi,0}(s|\infty)=\xi^{s}\frac{(\xi;q)_{\infty}}{(q;q)_{s}},\qquad s\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0},

which we denote by
q
-Geo
(\xi)
.

In the
q
-PushTASEP, a finite number
N
of particles occupy, following the exclusion rule, sites of the lattice
\mathbb{Z}
and their position is recorded in an array
\mathsf{X}(T)=(\mathsf{x}_{1}(T)<\cdots<\mathsf{x}_{N}(T))
, where
T\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}
denotes the time. During the time step
T\to T+1
, the array
\mathsf{X}(T)
is updated sequentially from left to right and

(3.3)

\mathsf{x}_{k}(T+1)=\mathsf{x}_{k}(T)+V_{k,T}+W_{k,T},\qquad\text{for }k=1,\dots,N,

where
V_{k,T}\sim q
-
\mathrm{Geo}(a_{k}b_{T+1})
and

(3.4)

W_{k,T}\sim\bm{\varphi}_{q^{-1},q^{\mathrm{gap}_{k}(T)},0}(\bullet\,|\,\mathsf{x}_{k-1}(T+1)-\mathsf{x}_{k-1}(T)).

In the previous expression we assume
\mathrm{gap}_{j}(T)=\mathsf{x}_{j}(T)-\mathsf{x}_{j-1}(T)-1
and we set, by convention
\mathsf{x}_{0}(T)=-\infty
. In (
3.3
) we see that the random movement of a particle splits into two independent contributions:
W_{k,T}
consists in a random pushing that the particle
\mathsf{x}_{k-1}
impresses on
\mathsf{x}_{k}
, while
V_{k,T}
is an independent jump of
\mathsf{x}_{k}
.

Markov rules (
3.3
), although explicit are far from intuitive or natural. An interesting simplification can be obtained considering a
continuous time
limit, setting
b_{i}=\varepsilon
,
T=\tau/\varepsilon
and
\varepsilon\to 0
. This procedure yields the continuous time
q
-PushTASEP first defined in
[
BP16
]
; for a visualization see
fig.

2
.

The
q
-PushTASEP is deeply connected to marginals of the
q
-Whittaker measure, as recalled in the next proposition.

Proposition 3.1

(
[
MP17
]
, Section 6.3)
.

Consider
\bm{a},\bm{b}
alpha specializations of parameters respectively
(a_{1},\dots,a_{N})\in(0,1)^{N}
and
(b_{1},\dots,b_{T})\in(0,1)^{T}
. Let
\mu\sim\mathbb{W}_{\bm{a};\bm{b}}^{(q)}
and let
\mathsf{X}(T)
be a
q
-PushTASEP under initial conditions
\mathsf{x}_{k}(0)=k
for
k=1,\dots,N
. Then, we have the following equality in distribution

(3.5)

\mathsf{x}_{N}(T)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathcal{D}}}{{=}}\mu_{1}+N.

Figure 2.
Visualization of the dynamics in the continuous time
q
-PushTASEP. Each particle
\mathsf{x}_{k}
possesses an exponential clock with parameter
a_{k}
and once the clock rings at time
\tau
it performs a jump to its right
\mathsf{x}_{k}(\tau_{+})=\mathsf{x}_{k}(\tau)+1
. The movement of the
k
-th particle triggers an instantaneous pushing mechanism and
\mathsf{x}_{j}(\tau_{+})=\mathsf{x}_{j}(\tau)+1
with probability

q^{\mathrm{gap}_{j}(\tau)}\mathbf{1}_{\mathsf{x}_{j-1}(\tau_{+})=\mathsf{x}_{j-1}(\tau)+1}
, for all
j>k
.

3.2.
q
-PushTASEP with particle creation

The
q
-PushTASEP admits a solvable variant where the stochastic dynamics
do not preserve the number of particles, which are added to the system at each time step. This model was introduced in
[
BBC20
]
and we will refer to it as
q
-PushTASEP with particle creation
. At any time
T\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}
, the system consists of exactly
T+1
particles located in the lattice
\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}
and whose positions are

(3.6)

0=\mathsf{x}_{0}^{\mathrm{hs}}(T)<\mathsf{x}_{1}^{\mathrm{hs}}(T)<\cdots<\mathsf{x}_{T}^{\mathrm{hs}}(T)<\infty,

recorded in the array
\mathsf{X}^{\mathrm{hs}}(T)
. During the time step from
T
to
T+1
, the configuration gets updated sequentially from the particle with label 1 to the right following the
q
-PushTASEP rules described by (
3.3
), where we assume
V_{k,T}\sim q\text{-}\mathrm{Geo}(a_{k}a_{T+1})
. Once the position of the rightmost particle
\mathsf{x}_{T}^{\mathrm{hs}}(T+1)
has been determined a new particle is added to its right and
\mathsf{x}_{T+1}^{\mathrm{hs}}(T+1)=\mathsf{x}_{T}^{\mathrm{hs}}(T+1)+1+\widetilde{V}_{T+1}
, where
\widetilde{V}_{T+1}\sim q
-
\mathrm{Geo}(\gamma a_{T+1})
is an independent increment. Notice that the
0
-th particle never moves. Here we have assumed that
a_{i},\gamma
are positive numbers such that
a_{i}a_{j},\gamma a_{i}<1
for all
1\leq i<j
. By construction, we will only consider
empty initial conditions
, that is
\mathsf{X}^{\mathrm{hs}}(0)=\{\mathsf{x}_{0}(0)=0\}
.

The salient feature of this model is the fact that during the evolution, new particles are added at the right of the system with spacing modulated by the parameter
\gamma
. Such property, makes the
q
-pushTASEP with particle creation a regularization of the KPZ equation in half space, although to see this several non trivial scalings are necessary. As for the “full space” version of the
q
-PushTASEP, this is, admittedly not necessarily the most intuitive model to consider. More canonical models of growth with boundary effects have been considered in the past. These include the Asymmetric Simple Exclusion Process
[
BBCW18
,
TW13a
,
TW13b
]
or other discrete polymer models
[
OSZ14
]
, but unfortunately their solvability, for generic values of boundary strength, does not lead directly to manageable or rigorous exact formulas.

The next proposition provides the relation between the
q
-PushTASEP with particle creation and the half space
q
-Whittaker measure.

Proposition 3.2

(
[
BBC20
]
Proposition 4.24)
.

Consider parameters
a_{1},\dots,a_{N},\gamma\in(0,1)
and let
\bm{a}
be the alpha specialization with parameters
(a_{1},\dots,a_{N})
. Let
\mu\sim\mathbb{HW}^{(q)}_{\bm{a};\gamma}
and let
\mathsf{X}^{\mathrm{hs}}(T)
be the
q
-PushTASEP with particle creation and empty initial conditions with parameters
\bm{a},\gamma
. Then, we have the following equality in distribution

(3.7)

\mathsf{x}^{\mathrm{hs}}_{N}(N)=\mu_{1}+N.

3.3.
ASEP

The asymmetric simple exclusion process is a canonical model of transport of particles in one dimension. It was introduced in
[
Spi70
]
and its stochastic integrable stucture has been investigated intensively in the last two decades
[
Sch97
,
TW08a
,
TW08b
,
TW09a
,
IS11
,
BCS14
,
BO17
]
. On the lattice
\mathbb{Z}
at time
\tau\geq 0
we consider a set of particles, with locations
2
2

2

Notice that in this case the particle ordering is
opposite
to the one adopted for the
q
-PushTASEP in
sections

3.1
and
3.2
.

\mathsf{x}_{1}(\tau)>\mathsf{x}_{2}(\tau)>\cdots
. Each particle possesses two exponential clocks of rate respectively
q\in[0,1)
and
1
. When the first clock rings the particle attempts a jump to its left neighboring site and when the second one rings it attempts a jump to its right. Jumps are successful provided the target location is empty; see
fig.

3
. Particle configurations we consider always possess a rightmost particle, whose location is
\mathsf{x}_{1}
. For this we can define the integrated current through a location
x
as

(3.8)

\mathsf{J}(x,\tau)=\sum_{k\geq 1}\mathbf{1}_{\mathsf{x}_{k}(\tau)>x}-\sum_{k\geq 1}\mathbf{1}_{\mathsf{x}_{k}(0)>x}.

The ASEP is deeply related to the Hall-Littlewood measure. This was essentially discovered in
[
BBW18
]
, where authors established a more general correspondence between a marginal of the Hall-Littlewood measure and the stochastic six vertex model. The convergence of the stochastic six vertex model to the ASEP was proven in
[
Agg16
]
.

Proposition 3.3
.

Consider the
q
-Whittaker measure
\mathbb{W}_{\widehat{a};\widehat{b}}^{(q)}
where
\widehat{a},\widehat{b}
are
q
-beta specializations of parameters
(a_{1},\dots,a_{N})
and
(b_{1},\dots,b_{T})
. Let
\mu\sim\mathbb{W}_{\widehat{a};\widehat{b}}^{(q)}
and consider the scaling

(3.9)

T=\frac{\tau}{\varepsilon},\qquad N=\frac{\tau}{\varepsilon}+x,\qquad a_{i}=b_{j}=1-\frac{(1-q)\varepsilon}{2},\qquad\mu_{1}=N-\mathsf{J}^{(\varepsilon)}(x,\tau).

Then, as
\varepsilon\to 0
, the random variable
\mathsf{J}^{(\varepsilon)}(x,\tau)
converges in law to the integrated current
\mathsf{J}(x,\tau)
of the ASEP with initial conditions
\mathsf{x}_{k}(0)=-k
for
k=1,2\dots
.

Figure 3.
Visualization of the dynamics in the ASEP.

As hinted earlier, the ASEP can be seen as a particular case of a more general stochastic solvable model, i.e. the stochastic six vertex model
[
GS92
,
BCG16
]
. In fact, combinatorial techniques allow to transform the stochastic six vertex model into many more solvable systems, including the
q
-PushTASEP; see
[
BP18
,
CP16
,
BMP21
]
. Since the stochastic six vertex model is related to the Hall-Littlewood measure
[
BBW18
]
, correspondences as those of
proposition

3.1
or
proposition

3.3
, leading to results we will elaborate in the following sections, could be given for a full hierarchy of solvable models. To keep our argument concise we will not report such results here.

3.4.
Log gamma polymer

The
Log Gamma polymer
model is a discrete solvable random polymer model introduced by Seppaläinen in
[
Sep12
]
.
We recall that an
inverse gamma random variable of parameter
\alpha
, which we will denote as
\mathrm{Gamma}^{-1}(\alpha)
, has probability density function

(3.10)

p_{\mathrm{Gamma}^{-1}(\alpha)}(x)=\frac{x^{-\alpha-1}e^{-1/x}}{\Gamma(\alpha)}.

On each site
(i,j)
of the planar lattice
\Lambda=\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}\times\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}
we place a random variable
w_{i,j}\sim\mathrm{Gamma}^{-1}(A_{i}+B_{j})
, where
A_{i},B_{j}
are positive real numbers.
For a fixed point
(T,N)
, we consider the set
\Pi_{(1,1)\to(T,N)}
of up-right paths
\varpi=(\varpi_{1},\dots,\varpi_{N+T-1})
on
\Lambda
with endpoints
\varpi_{1}=(1,1)
and
\varpi_{N+T-1}=(T,N)
. Here by up-right path we mean that the increments
\varpi_{i+1}-\varpi_{i}
are either
(1,0)
or
(0,1)
for any
i
; see
fig.

4
, left panel. To any path
\varpi
we associate the random weight

(3.11)

w(\varpi)=\prod_{\ell=1}^{N+T-1}w_{\varpi_{\ell}}

and we define the partition function

(3.12)

Z(N,T)=\sum_{\varpi\in\Pi_{(1,1)\to(T,N)}}w(\varpi).

Remark 3.4
.

Let us explain the origin of the name “Log Gamma” polymer.
Canonically the partition function of a directed polymer with fixed endpoints at
(1,1)
and
(T,N)
, has the form

(3.13)

Z(N,T)=\sum_{\varpi\in\Pi_{(1,1)\to(T,N)}}e^{\beta E(\varpi)},

where
E
is the energy of the polymer
\varpi
and
\beta
is the inverse temperature of the system. In case the energy is taken as a sum of independent terms
E(\varpi)=\sum_{\ell}E_{\varpi_{\ell}}
and
E_{(i,j)}\sim-\log\mathrm{Gamma}(A_{i}+B_{j})
and the parameter
\beta=1
we recover the partition function (
3.12
).

(a)

(b)

Figure 4.
Visualizations of the Log Gamma polymer model in full space (a) and in half space (b).

The Log Gamma polymer model arises as a scaling limit of the
q
-PushTASEP, as it was proven by Matveev and Petrov in
[
MP17
, Theorem 8.7]
. A particular case of such result is stated in the next proposition.

Proposition 3.5
.

Let
\mathsf{X}(T)
be a
q
-PushTASEP with initial conditions
\mathsf{x}_{k}(0)=k
for
k=1,\dots,N
. Consider the scaling
q=e^{-\varepsilon}
,
a_{i}=e^{-\varepsilon A_{i}}
,
b_{j}=e^{-\varepsilon B_{j}}
and

(3.14)

\mathsf{x}_{N}(T)=(N+T-1)\varepsilon^{-1}\log\varepsilon^{-1}+\varepsilon^{-1}\log Z^{(\varepsilon)}(T,N).

Then, as
\varepsilon\to 0
, the random variable
Z^{(\varepsilon)}(T,N)
converges in distribution to
Z(T,N)
.

3.5.
Half space Log Gamma Polymer

Here we consider a variant of the Log Gamma polymer model in the restricted lattice
\Lambda^{\mathrm{hs}}=\{(i,j)\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 1}\times\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 1}:i\geq j\}
. In this case to each site
(i,j)
of
\Lambda^{\mathrm{hs}}
we associate a random variable

(3.15)

w_{i,j}\sim\begin{cases}\mathrm{Gamma}^{-1}(A_{i}+\varUpsilon)\qquad&\text{if }i=j,\\
\mathrm{Gamma}^{-1}(A_{i}+A_{j})\qquad&\text{if }i>j,\end{cases}

where
\varUpsilon>-\min\{A_{i}:i=1,2,\dots\}
is the
boundary strength
. For any
N\leq T
, define, as in the previous subsection, the set
\Pi^{\mathrm{hs}}_{(1,1)\to(T,N)}
of up-right paths
\varpi=(\varpi_{1},\dots,\varpi_{T+N-1})
on
\Lambda^{\mathrm{hs}}
with endpoints
\varpi_{1}=(1,1)
and
\varpi_{T+N-1}=(T,N)
. The weight of a path
\varpi
is defined as in (
3.11
) and the partition function of the half space Log Gamma polymer is the random function

(3.16)

Z^{\mathrm{hs}}(N,T)=\sum_{\varpi\in\Pi^{\mathrm{hs}}_{(1,1)\to(T,N)}}w(\varpi).

As in the whole space case, the random function
Z^{\mathrm{hs}}
can be recovered as a scaling limit of the
q
-PushTASEP with particle creations, as recalled next.

Proposition 3.6
.

Let
\mathsf{X}^{\mathrm{hs}}(T)
be a
q
-PushTASEP with particle creation and empty initial conditions. Consider the scaling
q=e^{-\varepsilon}
,
a_{i}=e^{-\varepsilon A_{i}},\gamma=e^{-\varepsilon\varUpsilon}
and

(3.17)

\mathsf{x}^{\mathrm{hs}}_{N}(T)=(N+T-1)\varepsilon^{-1}\log\varepsilon^{-1}+\varepsilon^{-1}\log Z^{\mathrm{hs}}(T,N;\varepsilon),

with
T\geq N
. Then, as
\varepsilon\to 0
, the random variable
Z^{\mathrm{hs}}(T,N;\varepsilon)
converges in distribution to
Z^{\mathrm{hs}}(T,N)
.

Proof.

This is proven in
[
BBC20
]
, combining Proposition 6.27 and Proposition 4.24 of the same paper.
∎

4.
Periodic and free boundary Schur measures

In this section we define two important objects: the periodic and the free boundary Schur measures. These are discrete models of free fermions at positive temperature and as such their law is described in terms of determinantal and pfaffian point processes. The fundamental identities (
2.26
) and (
2.27
) relate them precisely to the KPZ models discussed in the previous section.

4.1.
Periodic Schur measure

The
periodic Schur measure
was first introduced by Borodin in
[
Bor07
]
as a generalization of Okounkov’s Schur measure
[
Oko01
]
. It is a measure on the set of partitions given by

(4.1)

\mathbb{PS}_{a,b}^{(q)}(\lambda)=\frac{1}{(q;q)_{\infty}^{-1}\Pi(a;b)}\sum_{\rho}q^{|\rho|}s_{\lambda/\rho}(a)s_{\lambda/\rho}(b),

where
s_{\lambda/\rho}(x)
is the skew Schur function (
2.2
). Here
a,b
are Schur-positive specializations and the normalization constant is a consequence of the Cauchy identity (
2.24
). While the Schur measure is a model of free fermions at
zero temperature
, the periodic Schur measure, after a simple trasformation describes the grand canonical ensemble of free fermions at
positive temperature
, modulated by the parameter
q
; see
[
BB19
]
.
As found in
[
Bor07
]
a certain independent shift of coordinates of
\lambda
transforms the periodic Schur measure into a determinantal point process. We say that an integer valued random variable
S
has
theta
distribution of parameters
q\in(0,1),\zeta>0
, in short
S\sim\mathrm{Theta}(\zeta;q)
, if

(4.2)

\mathbb{P}(S=k)=\frac{1}{\vartheta_{3}(\zeta;q)}q^{k^{2}/2}\zeta^{k},\qquad k\in\mathbb{Z},

where

(4.3)

\vartheta_{3}(\zeta;q)=(q,-\sqrt{q}\zeta,-\sqrt{q}/\zeta;q)_{\infty}

is the Jacobi theta function. The factorized form of the normalizaztion constant is the celebrated Jacobi triple product identity
[
Ism05
, Theorem 12.3.2]
. We can now define the
shift-mixed periodic Schur measure
as the point process

(4.4)

\mathfrak{S}(\lambda,S)=\left(\lambda_{i}-i+\frac{1}{2}+S\right)_{i=1,2,\dots}\subset\mathbb{Z}^{\prime},

where we used the notation
\mathbb{Z}^{\prime}=\mathbb{Z}+\frac{1}{2}
, and
\lambda\sim\mathbb{PS}_{a,b}^{(q)}
,
S\sim\mathrm{Theta}(\zeta;q)
are independent.

Theorem 4.1

(
[
Bor07
]
)
.

The shift-mixed periodic Schur measure is a determinantal point process with correlation kernel

(4.5)

K(x,y)=\frac{1}{(2\pi\mathrm{i})^{2}}\oint_{|z|=r}\frac{\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}z}{z^{x+1}}\oint_{|w|=r^{\prime}}\frac{\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}w}{w^{-y+1}}\frac{F(z)}{F(w)}\kappa(z,w),

where
x,y\in\mathbb{Z}^{\prime}
and

(4.6)

F(z)=\prod_{\ell\geq 0}\frac{H(q^{\ell}a;z)}{H(q^{\ell}b;z^{-1})},

(4.7)

\kappa(z,w)=\sqrt{\frac{w}{z}}\frac{(q;q)_{\infty}^{2}}{(w/z,qz/w;q)_{\infty}}\frac{\vartheta_{3}(\zeta z/w;q)}{\vartheta_{3}(\zeta;q)}.

The function
H
was defined in (
2.17
) and the integration contour’s radii satisfy
1<r/r^{\prime}<q^{-1}
and
R^{-1}\leq r,r^{\prime}\leq R
, where
R
is the minimum of convergence radii of
H(a;z)
and
H(b;z)
.

We will be interested in the rightmost coordinate of the shift-mixed periodic Schur measure
\lambda_{1}+S
. Using basic notions of determinantal point processes its probability distribution can be written in terms of the Fredholm determinant of the kernel
K
of (
4.5
). We have, for any
s\in\mathbb{R}
,

(4.8)

\mathbb{P}[\lambda_{1}+S\leq s]=\det(1-K)_{\ell^{2}(\mathbb{Z}_{>s}^{\prime})},

where
\mathbb{Z}_{>s}^{\prime}
denotes the set of half integers larger than to
s
.

4.2.
Alternative representation of the Fredholm determinant via Ramanujan’s bilateral sum.

To state the main result of the subsection we recall the identity

(4.9)

\sum_{m\in\mathbb{Z}^{\prime}}\frac{z^{m}}{1+\zeta^{-1}q^{-m}}=z^{-1/2}\frac{(q;q)_{\infty}^{2}}{(1/z,qz;q)_{\infty}}\frac{\vartheta_{3}(\zeta z;q)}{\vartheta_{3}(\zeta;q)},

which holds for
1<|z|<q^{-1}
and it is a particular case of Ramanujan’s
{}_{1}\psi_{1}
sum
[
Ism05
, Theorem 12.3.1]
.

Proposition 4.2
.

[
IMS21a
, Proposition 2.3]

Let
\lambda
be distributed according to the periodic Schur measure (
4.1
) and let
S\sim\mathrm{Theta}(\zeta;q)
. Then, for any
s\in\mathbb{Z}
, we have

(4.10)

\mathbb{P}\left[\lambda_{1}+S\leq s\right]=\det\left[1-f_{\zeta,s}\cdot M\right]_{\ell^{2}(\mathbb{Z}^{\prime})},

where
M
is given by

(4.11)

M(m,n)=\oint_{|z|=r}\frac{\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}z}{2\pi\mathrm{i}}\oint_{|w|=r^{\prime}}\frac{\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}w}{2\pi\mathrm{i}}\frac{F(z)}{F(w)}\frac{z^{n-1/2}}{w^{m+1/2}}\frac{1}{z-w},

and
f_{\zeta,s}
is the Fermi-Dirac distribution
3
3

3

Setting
q=e^{-\varepsilon/k_{B}T}
and
\zeta=e^{\mu/k_{B}T}
, where
T
is the temperature,
\varepsilon
is the quantized energy,
k_{B}
is Boltzmann constant and
\mu
is the chemical potential, then
f_{\zeta,s}(m)
assumes the canonical form of the Fermi-Dirac distribution.

(4.12)

f_{\zeta,s}(m)=\frac{1}{1+\zeta^{-1}q^{-s-m}}.

Proof.

Using (
4.9
), the kernel
K
can be rewritten as

(4.13)

K(x,y)=\sum_{m\in\mathbb{Z}^{\prime}}\frac{1}{1+\zeta^{-1}q^{-m}}\oint_{|z|=r}\frac{\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}z}{2\pi\mathrm{i}}\oint_{|w|=r^{\prime}}\frac{\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}w}{2\pi\mathrm{i}}\frac{w^{y-1-m}}{z^{x+1-m}}\frac{F(z)}{F(w)}.

Defining

(4.14)

A(x,m)=\oint_{|z|=r}\frac{\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}z}{2\pi\mathrm{i}}\frac{F(z)}{z^{x+1-m}}\qquad\text{and}\qquad B(n,y)=\oint_{|w|=r^{\prime}}\frac{\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}w}{2\pi\mathrm{i}}\frac{w^{y-1-m}}{F(w)},

we observe that

(4.15)

\begin{split}K(x+s,y+s)&=\sum_{m\in\mathbb{Z}^{\prime}}\frac{1}{1+\zeta^{-1}q^{-m}}A(x+s,m)B(m,y+s)\\
&=\sum_{m\in\mathbb{Z}^{\prime}}\frac{1}{1+\zeta^{-1}q^{-m}}A(x,m-s)B(m-s,y)\\
&=\sum_{m^{\prime}\in\mathbb{Z}^{\prime}}f_{\zeta,s}(m^{\prime})A(x,m^{\prime})B(m^{\prime},y).\end{split}

In this way, from the notable symmetry of the Fredholm determinant
\det[1-AfB]=\det[1-fBA]
, we find that

(4.16)

\det[1-K]_{\ell^{2}(\mathbb{Z}^{\prime}_{>s})}=\det\left[1-f_{\zeta,s}\cdot M\right]_{\ell^{2}(\mathbb{Z}^{\prime})},

where we noticed that

(4.17)

M(m,n)=\sum_{x=0}^{\infty}B(m,x)A(x,n).

Relation (
4.8
) now concludes the proof.
∎

The determinantal expression stated in
proposition

4.2
relates the probability distribution of the rightmost particle in the shift mixed periodic Schur measure to an average of a multiplicative observable of a determinantal point process with correlation kernel
M
.

Corollary 4.3
.

Under the hypothesis of
proposition

4.2
, we have

(4.18)

\mathbb{P}\left[\lambda_{1}+S\leq s\right]=\mathbb{E}_{M}\left[\prod_{i=1}^{\infty}\frac{1}{1+\zeta q^{s+\mathfrak{a}_{i}}}\right],

where
\{\mathfrak{a}_{i}:i\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 1}\}
is a determinantal point process with correlation kernel
M
.

Proof.

Notable properties of determinantal point process imply that, for any function
g
decaying “sufficiently well”, we have

(4.19)

\mathbb{E}_{M}\left[\prod_{i=1}^{\infty}(1+g(\mathfrak{a}_{i}))\right]=\det[1+g\cdot M]_{\ell^{2}(\mathbb{Z}^{\prime})},

so that setting
g=-f_{\zeta,s}
and using (
4.10
) we prove relation (
4.18
).
∎

Remark 4.4
.

Comparing kernels
M
with
K
, evaluated at
q=0
, we see that
M
is the correlation kernel of “holes” in the Schur measure
\mathbb{PS}^{(q=0)}_{a,b}
(see
[
BO17
]
), with specializations
a,b
consisting of geometric progressions of single parameter alpha specializations of ratio
q
, i.e.
a=(a_{1},qa_{1},q^{2}a_{1},\dots,a_{2},qa_{2},q^{2}a_{2},\dots)
,
b=(b_{1},qb_{1},q^{2}b_{1},\dots,b_{2},qb_{2},q^{2}b_{2},\dots)
.

4.3.
Free boundary Schur measure

The
free boundary Schur measure
was introduced by Betea, Bouttier, Nejjar and Vuletic in
[
BBNV18
]
. Rather than the general process considered in the original paper
[
BBNV18
]
, we will only focus on a particular case that turns out to be very important in connection to KPZ models in half space. We define the probability measure on the set of partitions with
even column
length

(4.20)

\mathbb{FBS}_{a}^{(q)}(\lambda)=\frac{\mathbf{1}_{\lambda^{\prime}\text{ is even}}}{(q;q)_{\infty}^{-1}\widetilde{\Pi}(a)}\sum_{\rho:\rho^{\prime}\text{ is even}}q^{|\rho|/2}s_{\lambda/\rho}(a).

Here
a
is a Schur positive specialization and the normalization term
\widetilde{\Pi}(a)
was introduced in (
2.22
). As in the case of the periodic Schur measure, the free boundary Schur measure (
4.20
) itself does not possess a nice determinantal nor pfaffian structure, but its shift-mixed version is shown to be a pfaffian point process. Let
S\sim\mathrm{Theta}(\zeta^{2};q^{2})
be independent of
\lambda
. Then, the
shift-mixed free boundary Schur measure
is the point process

(4.21)

\mathfrak{S}(\lambda,2S)=\left(\lambda_{i}-i+\frac{1}{2}+2S\right)_{i=1,2,\dots}\subset\mathbb{Z}^{\prime}.

In the next theorem we give a characterization of
\mathfrak{S}(\lambda,2S)
. For this we need to define the function
\Delta^{\prime}
as

(4.22)

\Delta^{\prime}(x,y)=\mathbf{1}_{y=x+1}-\mathbf{1}_{y=x-1}

and the difference operator
\nabla_{x}
as

(4.23)

\nabla_{x}f(x)=\frac{1}{2}[f(x+1)-f(x-1)].

Theorem 4.5

(
[
BBNV18
,
BBNV19
]
)
.

The shift-mixed free boundary Schur measure is a pfaffian point process with
2\times 2
matrix correlation kernel

(4.24)

K^{\mathrm{hs}}(x,y)=\left(\begin{matrix}k(x,y)&-2\nabla_{y}k(x,y)\\
-2\nabla_{x}k(y,x)&4\nabla_{x}\nabla_{y}k(x,y)+\Delta^{\prime}(x,y)\end{matrix}\right),

where

(4.25)

k(x,y)=\frac{1}{(2\pi\mathrm{i})^{2}}\oint_{|z|=r}\frac{\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}z}{z^{x+3/2}}\oint_{|w|=r}\frac{\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}w}{w^{y+5/2}}F(z)F(w)\kappa^{\mathrm{hs}}(z,w),

(4.26)

F(z)=\prod_{\ell\geq 0}\frac{H(q^{\ell}a;z)}{H(q^{\ell}a;z^{-1})}

and

(4.27)

\kappa^{\mathrm{hs}}(z,w)=\frac{(q,q,w/z,qz/w;q)_{\infty}}{(1/z^{2},1/w^{2},1/zw,qwz;q)_{\infty}}\frac{\vartheta_{3}(\zeta^{2}z^{2}w^{2};q^{2})}{\vartheta_{3}(\zeta^{2};q^{2})}.

The function
H(a,z)
was defined in (
2.17
), while the integration contours are positively oriented and their radius satisfies
1<r<\min(q^{-1/2},R)
, where
R
is the convergence radius of
H(a;z)
.

Proof.

Result of
theorem

4.5
, although implicitly derived in
[
BBNV18
]
, is more explicitly stated in
[
BBNV19
]
and the expression of the
2\times 2
matrix correlation kernel
K^{\mathrm{hs}}
is a reformulation of a particular case of equations (4.2)-(4.4) of
[
BBNV19
]
, where in their notation we set
u=\sqrt{q},v=1,a_{1}=a_{2}=0
. Let us show how it is derived. From
[
BBNV19
]
eq. (4.2)-(4.4) one sees that the shift mixed free boundary Schur measure is a pfaffian point process with
2\times 2
matrix correlation kernel

(4.28)

\widetilde{K}(x,y)=\left(\begin{matrix}\widetilde{K}_{1,1}(x,y)&\widetilde{K}_{1,2}(x,y)\\
-\widetilde{K}_{1,2}(y,x)&\widetilde{K}_{2,2}(x,y)+\Delta^{\prime}(x,y)\end{matrix}\right),

defined as

(4.29)

\displaystyle\widetilde{K}_{1,1}(x,y)

\displaystyle=\oint_{|z|=r}\frac{\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}z}{2\pi\mathrm{i}}\oint_{|w|=r^{\prime}}\frac{\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}w}{2\pi\mathrm{i}}\frac{F(z)F(w)}{z^{x+3/2}w^{y+5/2}}\kappa^{\mathrm{hs}}(z,w),

(4.30)

\displaystyle\widetilde{K}_{1,2}(x,y)

\displaystyle=\oint_{|z|=r}\frac{\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}z}{2\pi\mathrm{i}}\oint_{|w|=1/r^{\prime}}\frac{\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}w}{2\pi\mathrm{i}}\frac{F(z)F(1/w)}{z^{x+3/2}w^{-y+1/2}}\kappa^{\mathrm{hs}}(z,1/w)(1-w^{2}),

(4.31)

\displaystyle\widetilde{K}_{2,2}(x,y)

\displaystyle=\oint_{|z|=1/r}\frac{\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}z}{2\pi\mathrm{i}}\oint_{|w|=1/r^{\prime}}\frac{\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}w}{2\pi\mathrm{i}}\frac{F(1/z)F(1/w)}{{z^{-x+3/2}w^{-y+1/2}}}\kappa^{\mathrm{hs}}(1/z,1/w)(1-z^{2})(1-w^{2}),

with
1<r,r^{\prime}<\min(q^{-1},R)
.
Notice that in
[
BBNV19
]
the element
\widetilde{K}_{2,2}
has integration contours
|z|=r
and
|w|=r^{\prime}
and the function
\Delta^{\prime}
is absent. To reach our expression we shift both contours and doing so the only pole one encounters is at
z=1/w
, whose contribution is

(4.32)

\begin{split}\oint_{|w|=1/r^{\prime}}\frac{\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}w}{2\pi\mathrm{i}}w^{y-x}\left(\frac{1}{w^{2}}-1\right)&=\mathbf{1}_{y=x+1}-\mathbf{1}_{y=x-1}=\Delta^{\prime}(x,y).\end{split}

With a change of variable
w\to 1/w
in (
4.30
) and
z\to 1/z,w\to 1/w
in (
4.31
) we can now make integration contours to be the same for each component
\widetilde{K}_{i,j}
. Finally we notice that, after such change of variables, the
\widetilde{K}_{i,j}
’s differ from each other by the sole presence of factors
(w-1/w)
and
(z-1/z)
in the integrand, so that each term can be easily matched with discrete derivatives
\nabla_{y},\nabla_{x}
of the function
k(x,y)=\widetilde{K}_{1,1}(x,y)
as in (
4.24
), using the simple relation

(4.33)

\nabla_{x}\,\frac{1}{z^{x}}=-\frac{1}{2}\left(z-\frac{1}{z}\right)\frac{1}{z^{x}}.

This concludes the proof.
∎

Remark 4.6
.

Setting
q=0
, the free boundary Schur measure becomes the
Pfaffian Schur measure
defined in
[
BR05
]
(see also an earlier work
[
SI04
]
) and studied in
[
Gho19
,
BBCS18
,
BBNV18
]
. Below in
section

6.3
we will denote this measure by
\mathbb{FBS}^{(q=0)}
and we will present an asymptotic analysis for the first row
\lambda_{1}
of a random partition
\lambda
.

As in case of the periodic Schur measure, we will be interested in the rightmost coordinate of the shift-mixed free boundary Schur measure. Its probability distribution function is explicitly written as the Fredholm pfaffian

(4.34)

\mathbb{P}[\lambda_{1}+2S\leq s]=\mathrm{Pf}(J-K^{\mathrm{hs}})_{\ell^{2}(\mathbb{Z}_{>s}^{\prime})}.

Recall that
J
is the matrix (
1.13
). In the following proposition we provide an alternative expression for the probability law of
\lambda_{1}
, which is slightly more convenient to consider degenerations and asymptotic limits.

Proposition 4.7
.

Let
\lambda
be a random partition distributed according to the free boundary Schur measure and
S\sim\mathrm{Theta}(\zeta^{2};q^{2})
. Then the probablity distribution of
\lambda_{1}+2S
admits the Fredholm pfaffian representation

(4.35)

\mathbb{P}[\lambda_{1}+2S\leq s]=\mathrm{Pf}(J-L)_{\ell^{2}(\mathbb{Z}^{\prime}_{>s})},

where
L
is the
2\times 2
matrix kernel

(4.36)

L(x,y)=\left(\begin{matrix}k(x,y)&-\nabla_{y}k(x,y)\\
-\nabla_{x}k(x,y)&\nabla_{x}\nabla_{y}k(x,y)\end{matrix}\right),

and the function
k
is given by (
4.25
), while
\nabla_{x},\nabla_{y}
were defined in (
4.23
).

Proof.

This is a slight generalization of
[
BBCS18
, Proposition 5.2]
, where partial derivatives
\partial_{x},\partial_{y}
are replaced by discrete difference operators
\nabla_{x},\nabla_{y}
. We find that the proof reported in
[
BBCS18
]
adapts well to our situation and so here we are only going to sketch the argument. Notation we introduce below will be close to that of
[
BBCS18
]
. Consider the expansion of Fredholm pfaffians of kernels
L
and
K^{\mathrm{hs}}
as

(4.37)

\mathrm{Pf}[J-L]_{\ell^{2}(\mathbb{Z}^{\prime}_{>s})}=\sum_{\ell\geq 0}\frac{(-1)^{\ell}}{\ell!}a_{\ell},\qquad\mathrm{Pf}\left[J-K^{\mathrm{hs}}\right]_{\ell^{2}(\mathbb{Z}^{\prime}_{>s})}=\sum_{\ell\geq 0}\frac{(-1)^{\ell}}{\ell!}b_{\ell},

where

(4.38)

a_{\ell}=\sum_{x_{1},\dots,x_{\ell}>s}\mathrm{Pf}[L(x_{i},x_{j})]_{i,j=1}^{\ell},\qquad b_{\ell}=\sum_{x_{1},\dots,x_{\ell}>s}\mathrm{Pf}\left[K^{\mathrm{hs}}(x_{i},x_{j})\right]_{i,j=1}^{\ell}.

The
2\times 2
matrix kernel
K^{\mathrm{hs}}
can be written as a sum

(4.39)

K^{\mathrm{hs}}(x,y)=A(x,y)+\left(\begin{matrix}0&0\\
0&\Delta^{\prime}(x,y)\end{matrix}\right),

with
A_{1,1}(x,y)=k(x,y)
,
A_{1,2}(x,y)=-2\nabla_{y}k(x,y)
,
A_{1,2}(x,y)=-2\nabla_{x}k(x,y)
,
A_{2,2}(x,y)=4\nabla_{x}\nabla_{y}k(x,y)
. Using a notable minor expansion for pfaffians of sums of
n\times n
matrices
N,M
(see
[
Ste90
, Lemma 4.2]
)

(4.40)

\mathrm{Pf}[M+N]=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}I\subset\{1,\dots n\}\\
\#I\text{ even}\end{subarray}}(-1)^{|I|-\#I/2}\mathrm{Pf}[M_{I}]\mathrm{Pf}[N_{I^{c}}]

where
\#I
is the cardinality of
I
, while
|I|
is the sum of its elements, we can express
b_{\ell}
as

(4.41)

\frac{(-1)^{\ell}}{\ell!}b_{\ell}=\sum_{m=\lceil\ell/2\rceil}^{\ell}b_{\ell}^{(m)}.

In the above expression we have condensated in the term
b_{\ell}^{(m)}
all summations involving exactly a product of degree
\ell-m
of
\Delta^{\prime}
functions originating from the evaluation of minors of the matrix
\left(\begin{smallmatrix}0&0\\
0&\Delta^{\prime}(x_{i},x_{j})\end{smallmatrix}\right)_{i,j=1}^{m}
. A computation shows that terms
b_{\ell}^{(m)}
have the explicit expression

(4.42)
