\begin{split}b_{\ell+i}^{(\ell)}&=\frac{(-1)^{\ell+i}}{(\ell+i)!}\sum_{x_{1},\dots,x_{\ell+i}>s}\left(\sum_{1\leq j_{1}<\cdots<j_{2i}\leq\ell+i}\mathrm{Pf}[\Delta^{\prime}(x_{j_{r}},x_{j_{s}})]_{r,s=1}^{i}(-1)^{i}\mathrm{Pf}\left[A_{\ell+i}^{\widehat{2j_{1}},\dots,\widehat{2j_{2i}}}\right]\right)\\
&=\frac{(-1)^{\ell}}{(\ell+i)!}\binom{\ell+i}{2i}\sum_{x_{1},\dots,x_{\ell+i}>s}\mathrm{Pf}[\Delta^{\prime}(x_{r},x_{s})]_{r,s=\ell-i+1}^{\ell+i}\mathrm{Pf}\left[A_{\ell+i}^{\widehat{2(\ell-i+1)},\dots,\widehat{2(\ell+i)}}\right],\end{split}

where we have denoted by
A_{n}^{\widehat{2j_{1}},\dots,\widehat{2j_{r}}}
is the
2(n-r)\times 2(n-r)
matrix resulting from removing rows and columns
2j_{1},\dots,2j_{r}
from the matrix
(A(x_{\alpha},x_{\beta}))_{\alpha,\beta=1}^{n}
. Notice that the second equality in (
4.42
) follows from the fact that, through a change of
x
-variables all terms in the sum inside the parentheses become equal, since the
x_{i}
are all summed over the same domain. We now evaluate the sum in the right hand side of (
4.42
), expanding one of the Pfaffians as in (
A.7
). We find

(4.43)

\begin{split}&\sum_{x_{1},\dots,x_{\ell+i}>s}\mathrm{Pf}[\Delta^{\prime}(x_{r},x_{s})]_{r,s=\ell-i+1}^{\ell+i}\mathrm{Pf}\left[A_{\ell+i}^{\widehat{2(\ell-i+1)},\dots,\widehat{2(\ell+i)}}\right]\\
&=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\alpha=\{(r_{1},s_{1}),\dots,(r_{i},s_{i})\}\\
r_{j},s_{j}\in\{\ell-i+1,\dots,\ell+i\}\\
r_{1}<\cdots<r_{i},\,\,r_{j}<s_{j}\end{subarray}}\epsilon(\alpha)\sum_{x_{1},\dots,x_{\ell+i}>s}\prod_{j=1}^{i}\Delta^{\prime}(x_{r_{j}},x_{s_{j}})\mathrm{Pf}\left[A_{\ell+i}^{\widehat{2(\ell-i+1)},\dots,\widehat{2(\ell+i)}}\right]\\
&=\frac{(2i)!}{2^{i}i!}\sum_{x_{1},\dots,x_{\ell+i}>s}\prod_{j=1}^{i}\Delta^{\prime}(x_{\ell-i+2j-1},x_{\ell-i+2j})\mathrm{Pf}\left[A_{\ell+i}^{\widehat{2(\ell-i+1)},\dots,\widehat{2(\ell+i)}}\right]\end{split}

where in the third line we recognized that, changing
x
-variables and absorbing the sign
\varepsilon(\alpha)
into the pfaffian all terms of the summation over permutations
\alpha
in the second line are equal. We can now evaluate the action of operators
\Delta^{\prime}
on the pfaffian and, after taking summations over variables
x_{\ell+1},\dots x_{\ell+i}
, we find

(4.44)

\begin{split}&2^{i}\,\nabla_{x_{\ell}}\cdots\nabla_{x_{\ell+i}}\mathrm{Pf}\left[A_{\ell+i}^{\widehat{2(\ell-i+1)},\dots,\widehat{2(\ell+i)}}\right]\bigg|_{x_{\ell}=\cdots=x_{\ell+i}}\\
&=2^{i}\,\mathrm{Pf}\left[\nabla_{x_{\ell}}\cdots\nabla_{x_{\ell+i}}A_{\ell+i}^{\widehat{2(\ell-i+1)},\dots,\widehat{2(\ell+i)}}\bigg|_{x_{\ell}=\cdots=x_{\ell+i}}\right]\\
&=(-1)^{i}2^{\ell}\mathrm{Pf}[L(x_{i},x_{j})]_{i,j=1}^{\ell}.\end{split}

Substituting the last relation and (
4.43
) into (
4.42
) we obtain

(4.45)

\begin{split}b_{\ell+i}^{(\ell)}=\frac{(-1)^{\ell+i}}{(\ell+i)!}\binom{\ell+i}{2i}\frac{(2i)!}{2^{i}i!}2^{\ell}a_{\ell}=\frac{(-1)^{\ell}a_{\ell}}{\ell!}\binom{\ell}{i}2^{k-i}(-1)^{i},\end{split}

so that

(4.46)

\sum_{i=0}^{\ell}b_{\ell+i}^{(\ell)}=\frac{(-1)^{\ell}}{\ell!}a_{\ell}\sum_{i=0}^{\ell}\binom{\ell}{i}2^{k-i}(-1)^{i}=\frac{(-1)^{\ell}}{\ell!}a_{\ell}.

We can now turn the Fredholm pfaffian expansion of
J-K^{\mathrm{hs}}
into that of
J-L
as

(4.47)

\begin{split}\mathrm{Pf}[J-K^{\mathrm{hs}}]&=\sum_{\ell\geq 0}\frac{(-1)^{\ell}}{\ell!}b_{\ell}=\sum_{m\geq 0}\sum_{i=0}^{m}b_{m+i}^{(m)}=\sum_{\ell\geq 0}\frac{(-1)^{\ell}}{\ell!}a_{\ell}=\mathrm{Pf}[J-L],\end{split}

where in the second equality we used (
4.41
) and operated an exchange of summation indices and in the third equality we renamed
m
back to
\ell
. This manipulations are justified by absolute convergence of all terms involved, which is consequence of exponential decay of coefficients of matrix kernels
L(x,y)
and
K^{\mathrm{hs}}(x,y)
as
x,y\to\infty
. This
completes the proof.
∎

4.4.
Alternatve representation of Fredholm pfaffian via a
q
-trigonometric identity by Gosper

Following Gosper
[
Gos01
]
, we define the
q
-analog of the sine and cosine functions as

(4.48)

\sin_{q}(\pi z)=q^{\left(z-\frac{1}{2}\right)^{2}}\frac{(q^{2z},q^{2-2z};q^{2})_{\infty}}{(q,q;q^{2})_{\infty}}\qquad\text{and}\qquad\cos_{q}(z)=\sin_{q}(\frac{\pi}{2}-z).

In the same paper, Gosper conjectured, via numerical experiments a number of properties of his
q
-trigonometric functions. Among these we have the following
q
-analog of the addition formula for angles, proven more recently in
[
EB18
]
,

(4.49)

\sin_{q}(x+y)=\frac{\sin_{q}(x-y)}{\sin_{q^{2}}(x-y)}\left[\sin_{q^{2}}(x)\cos_{q^{2}}(y)+\sin_{q^{2}}(y)\cos_{q^{2}}(x)\right].

Using this remarkable relation and Ramanujan’s bilateral sum (
4.9
) we obtain an alternative representation of the Fredholm pfaffian in the right hand side of (
4.35
).

Proposition 4.8
.

Let
\lambda
be distributed according to the free boundary Schur measure (
4.20
) and
S\sim\mathrm{Theta}(\zeta^{2};q^{2})
. Then we have

(4.50)

\mathbb{P}\left[\lambda_{1}+2S\leq s\right]=\mathrm{Pf}\left[J-f_{\zeta^{2},s}\cdot M^{\mathrm{hs}}\right]_{\ell^{2}(\mathbb{Z}^{\prime})},

where
M^{\mathrm{hs}}
is the
2\times 2
matrix kernel

(4.51)

M^{\mathrm{hs}}(m,n)=\left(\begin{matrix}M^{\mathrm{hs}}_{1,1}(m,n)&M^{\mathrm{hs}}_{1,2}(m,n)\\
-M^{\mathrm{hs}}_{1,2}(n,m)&M^{\mathrm{hs}}_{2,2}(m,n)\end{matrix}\right),

with coefficients

(4.52)

\begin{split}M^{\mathrm{hs}}_{1,1}(m,n)=\oint_{|z|=r}\frac{\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}z}{2\pi\mathrm{i}}\oint_{|z|=r}\frac{\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}w}{2\pi\mathrm{i}}&\frac{F(z)}{z^{m+5/2}}\frac{F(w)}{w^{n+5/2}}\frac{z-w}{1-zw}\frac{1+zw}{2}\\
&\times\frac{z(1/z^{2},q^{2}z^{2};q^{2})_{\infty}}{(1/z^{2};q)_{\infty}}\frac{w(1/w^{2},q^{2}w^{2};q^{2})_{\infty}}{(1/w^{2};q)_{\infty}},\end{split}

(4.53)

\begin{split}M^{\mathrm{hs}}_{1,2}(m,n)=\oint_{|z|=r}\frac{\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}z}{2\pi\mathrm{i}}\oint_{|z|=r}\frac{\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}w}{2\pi\mathrm{i}}&\frac{F(z)}{z^{m+5/2}}\frac{F(w)}{w^{n+5/2}}\frac{z-w}{1-zw}\frac{1+zw}{2}\\
&\times\frac{z(1/z^{2},q^{2}z^{2};q^{2})_{\infty}}{(1/z^{2};q)_{\infty}}\frac{(qw^{2},q/w^{2};q^{2})_{\infty}}{(q;q^{2})_{\infty}^{2}(1/w^{2};q)_{\infty}},\end{split}

(4.54)

\begin{split}M^{\mathrm{hs}}_{2,2}(m,n)=\oint_{|z|=r}\frac{\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}z}{2\pi\mathrm{i}}\oint_{|z|=r}\frac{\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}w}{2\pi\mathrm{i}}&\frac{F(z)}{z^{m+5/2}}\frac{F(w)}{w^{n+5/2}}\frac{z-w}{1-zw}\frac{1+zw}{2}\\
&\times\frac{(qz^{2},q/z^{2};q^{2})_{\infty}}{(q;q^{2})_{\infty}^{2}(1/z^{2};q)_{\infty}}\frac{(qw^{2},q/w^{2};q^{2})_{\infty}}{(q;q^{2})_{\infty}^{2}(1/w^{2};q)_{\infty}}\end{split}

and
f_{\zeta^{2},s}
is given in (
4.12
).
The notation adopted above is the same as in
theorem

4.5
.

Proof.

The addition formula (
4.49
) implies, setting
z=q^{-2x},w=q^{2y}
and rescaling
q\to q^{1/2}
, the identity

(4.55)

(w/z,qz/w;q)_{\infty}=\frac{1}{z}\frac{(1/zw,qzw;q)_{\infty}}{(1/z^{2}w^{2},q^{2}z^{2}w^{2};q^{2})_{\infty}}\left[g_{1}(z)g_{2}(w)-g_{1}(w)g_{2}(z)\right],

where we defined

(4.56)

g_{1}(z)=z(1/z^{2},q^{2}z^{2};q^{2})_{\infty},\qquad g_{2}(w)=\frac{(qw^{2},q/w^{2};q^{2})_{\infty}}{(q;q^{2})^{2}_{\infty}}.

Using (
4.55
) and (
4.9
) we can rewrite the function
\kappa^{\mathrm{hs}}
of (
4.27
) as

(4.57)

\kappa^{\mathrm{hs}}(z,w)=w\frac{g_{1}(z)g_{2}(w)-g_{1}(w)g_{2}(z)}{(1/z^{2},1/w^{2};q)_{\infty}}\sum_{m\in\mathbb{Z}^{\prime}}\frac{(zw)^{2m}}{1+\zeta^{-2}q^{-2m}}.

Defining

(4.58)

a(x)=\oint_{|z|=r}\frac{\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}z}{2\pi\mathrm{i}}\frac{F(z)}{z^{x+3/2}}\frac{g_{1}(z)}{(1/z^{2};q)_{\infty}},\qquad b(y)=\oint_{|w|=r}\frac{\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}w}{2\pi\mathrm{i}}\frac{F(w)}{w^{y+3/2}}\frac{g_{2}(w)}{(1/w^{2};q)_{\infty}},

we observe that

(4.59)

k(x,y)=\sum_{m\in\mathbb{Z}^{\prime}}\frac{1}{1+\zeta^{-2}q^{-2m}}\left[a(x-2m)b(y-2m)-a(y-2m)b(x-2m)\right],

where
k(x,y)
was given in (
4.25
). Now, defining

(4.60)

A(x,m)=\left(\begin{matrix}-\nabla_{x}a(x+2m)&\nabla_{x}b(x+2m)\\
-a(x+2m)&b(x+2m)\end{matrix}\right),\qquad B(m,y)=\left(\begin{matrix}b(y+2m)&-\nabla_{y}b(y+2m)\\
a(y+2m)&-\nabla_{y}a(y+2m)\end{matrix}\right),

we can express the matrix kernel
L
as

(4.61)

\begin{split}JL(x+s,y+s)&=\sum_{m\in\mathbb{Z}^{\prime}}\frac{1}{1+\zeta^{-2}q^{-2m}}A(x+s,m)B(m,y+s)\\
&=\sum_{m\in\mathbb{Z}^{\prime}}\frac{1}{1+\zeta^{-2}q^{-2m}}A(x,m-\frac{s}{2})B(m-\frac{s}{2},y)\\
&=\sum_{m^{\prime}\in\mathbb{Z}^{\prime}}f_{\zeta^{2},s}(m^{\prime})A(x,\frac{m^{\prime}}{2})B(\frac{m^{\prime}}{2},y).\end{split}

Using the notable relation
\mathrm{Pf}[J-J^{T}AfB]=\mathrm{Pf}[J-fBAJ^{T}]
, we find that

(4.62)

\mathrm{Pf}[J-L]_{\ell^{2}(\mathbb{Z}_{>s}^{\prime})}=\mathrm{Pf}\left[J-f_{\zeta^{2},s}\cdot M^{\mathrm{hs}}\right]_{\ell^{2}(\mathbb{Z}^{\prime})},

since

(4.63)

M^{\mathrm{hs}}(m,n)=\sum_{x=1}^{\infty}B(\frac{m}{2},x)A(x,\frac{n}{2})J^{T}.

This completes the proof.
∎

The identity (
4.50
) implies that the probability distribution of the right edge particle of the shift mixed free boundary Schur measure is equivalent to the expectation of the multiplicative Fermi-Dirac factor over a pfaffian point process with correlation kernel
M^{\mathrm{hs}}
.

Corollary 4.9
.

Under the hypothesis of
proposition

4.8
, we have

(4.64)

\mathbb{P}\left[\lambda_{1}+2S\leq s\right]=\mathbb{E}_{M^{\mathrm{hs}}}\left[\prod_{i=1}^{\infty}\frac{1}{1+\zeta^{2}q^{s+\mathfrak{a}_{i}}}\right],

where
\{\mathfrak{a}_{i}:i\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 1}\}
is a pfaffian point process with correlation kernel
M^{\mathrm{hs}}
.

Proof.

Notable properties of pfaffian point process imply that, for any function
g
with “nice” decay properties, we have

(4.65)

\mathbb{E}_{M^{\mathrm{hs}}}\left[\prod_{i=1}^{\infty}(1+g(\mathfrak{a}_{i}))\right]=\mathrm{Pf}\left[J+g\cdot M^{\mathrm{hs}}\right]_{\ell^{2}(\mathbb{Z}^{\prime})},

so that setting
g=-f_{\zeta^{2},s}
and using (
4.50
) we prove relation (
4.64
).
∎

We think of (
4.64
) as a pfaffian variant of (
4.18
). In
remark

4.4
we pointed out the relation between the correlation kernel
M
and the Schur measure and one could naively expect a similar relation between the correlation kernel
M^{\mathrm{hs}}
and the Pfaffian Schur measure. However, from a simple inspection of formulas given in
proposition

4.8
, this does not appear to be true. It is in fact an interesting problem to give a physical interpretation of the pfaffian point process associated to
M^{\mathrm{hs}}
.

4.5.
Equivalence with marginals of
q
-Whittaker measures

Here we state the fundamental connections between models of determinantal and pfaffian measures presented above in this section and discrete KPZ solvable models discussed in
section

3
.

Theorem 4.10

(
[
IMS21a
]
)
.

Let
\mu\sim\mathbb{W}^{(q)}_{a;b}
and
\chi\sim q\text{-}\mathrm{Geo}(q)
be indipendent random variables. Consider also
\lambda\sim\mathbb{PS}^{(q)}_{a,b}
. Then the following equivalence in distribution holds

(4.66)

\mu_{1}+\chi\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathcal{D}}}{{=}}\lambda_{1}.

Theorem

4.10
is an immediate consequence of the summation identity (
2.26
). In
[
IMS21a
]
we proved the same theorem, along with identity (
2.26
), by matching Fredholm determinant expressions for shift-mixed periodic Schur measure with those of
q
-Whittaker measure found in
[
IS19
]
.

The following theorem is new and it is a half space extension of
theorem

4.10
.

Theorem 4.11
.

Let
\mu\sim\mathbb{HW}^{(q)}_{a;0}
and
\chi\sim q\text{-}\mathrm{Geo}(q)
be indipendent random variables. Consider also
\lambda\sim\mathbb{FBS}^{(q)}_{a}
. Then the following equivalence in distribution holds

(4.67)

\mu_{1}+\chi\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathcal{D}}}{{=}}\lambda_{1}.

Proof.

This is straightforward consequence of the refined Littlewood identity (
2.27
).
∎

Unlike for
theorem

4.10
, a proof of equality (
4.67
) based on integrable probabilistic techniques is at the moment not available. This is because rigorous methods using Bethe ansatz have proven to be difficult to implement for the study of half space
q
-Whittaker measure and through such techniques it remains a challenge to obtain explicit and manageable pfaffian formulas for interesting physical observables; see
[
BBC20
]
.

5.
Fredholm determinant and pfaffian formulas for KPZ models

Leveraging equalities in distribution established in
theorem

4.10
and
theorem

4.11
along with the simple structure of the periodic and free boundary Schur measures we are able to produce determinantal and pfaffian formulas for observables of KPZ solvable models. Determinantal formulas reported below, although new, add to a number of analogous results discovered in the last 15 years
[
TW08a
,
TW09a
,
BC14
,
BCS14
,
BCR13
,
MP17
]
and in many cases appear to be simpler. Pfaffian formulas, on the other hand, are completely original and have no analog in the existing mathematical literature.

5.1.
Fredholm determinant formulas for
q
-PushTASEP and ASEP in full space

Let us start presenting a representation for the probability distribution of a tagged particle in
q
-PushTASEP in terms of a Fredholm determinant of a free-fermionic type kernel.

Corollary 5.1
.

Let
\mathsf{X}(T)
be the
q
-PushTASEP with initial conditions
\mathsf{x}_{k}(0)=k
, for
k=1,\dots,N
and
\chi\sim q
-
\mathrm{Geo}(q)
,
S\sim\mathrm{Theta}(\zeta,q)
independent random variables. Then we have

(5.1)

\mathbb{P}\left(\mathsf{x}_{N}(T)+\chi+S\leq N+s\right)=\det\left(1-K_{\mathrm{push}}\right)_{\ell^{2}(\mathbb{Z}_{>s}^{\prime})},

where the kernel
K_{\mathrm{push}}
is obtained from (
4.5
) setting

(5.2)

F(z)=\frac{\prod_{i=1}^{T}(b_{i}/z;q)_{\infty}}{\prod_{i=1}^{N}(a_{i}z;q)_{\infty}}.

Proof.

This is obtained combining
theorem

4.10
,
proposition

3.1
and
theorem

4.1
.
∎

Remark 5.2
.

The left hand side of (
5.1
) admits an alternative expression as
q
-Laplace transform

(5.3)

\mathbb{E}\left(\frac{1}{(-\zeta q^{\frac{1}{2}+s+N-\mathsf{x}_{N}(T)};q)_{\infty}}\right)

of the probability density function of
\mathsf{x}_{N}(T)
. This is a consequence of the identity

(5.4)

\mathbb{P}(\chi+S\leq n)=\frac{1}{(-\zeta q^{\frac{1}{2}+n};q)_{\infty}},

where
\chi
and
S
are as in
corollary

5.1
. For a direct proof of (
5.4
) see
[
IMS21a
, Lemma 2.4]
.

The first instance of a determinantal formula for the
q
-PushTASEP was given in
[
BCFV15
, Theorem 3.3]
and it was based on clever manipulations of integral formulas for
q
-moments; see
[
BC14
]
. The kernel presented in
[
BCFV15
]
, as others obtained starting from
q
-moments formulas, does not appear to be related with determinantal point processes or free fermions. As such, asymptotic analysis is rather involved, although doable, as one has to deal with a complicated pole structure of the kernel. It would be very useful to obtain our expression (
5.1
) by a proper summation of
q
-moments.

Next we present a Fredholm determinant formula of free fermionic type for the ASEP.

Corollary 5.3
.

Let
\mathsf{J}(x,\tau)
be the integrated current of the ASEP with initial conditions
\mathsf{x}_{k}(0)=-k
, for
k=1,2,\dots
and let
\chi\sim q
-
\mathrm{Geo}(q)
and
S\sim\mathrm{Theta}(\zeta,q)
be independent random variables. Then, we have

(5.5)

\mathbb{P}\left(-\mathsf{J}(x,\tau)+\chi+S\leq s\right)=\det(1-K_{\mathrm{ASEP}})_{\ell^{2}(\mathbb{Z}^{\prime}_{>s})},

where the kernel
K_{\mathrm{ASEP}}
is obtained from (
4.5
) setting

(5.6)

F(z)=e^{-(1-q)\frac{z\tau}{1+z}}(1+1/z)^{x}.

Proof.

Consider the
q
-Whittaker measure
\mathbb{W}^{(q)}_{a^{\prime},b^{\prime}}
, where
a^{\prime},b^{\prime}
are
q
-beta specializations with parameters
(a_{1},\dots,a_{N})
,
(b_{1},\dots,b_{T})
. Then, combining
theorem

4.10
and
theorem

4.1
we have

(5.7)

\mathbb{P}(\mu_{1}+\chi+S\leq r)=\det(1-\tilde{K})_{\ell^{2}(\mathbb{Z}^{\prime}_{>r})},

where
\tilde{K}
is obtained from (
4.5
) setting

(5.8)

F(z)=\frac{\prod_{i=1}^{N}(1+za_{i})}{\prod_{i=1}^{T}(1+b_{i}/z)}.

This is simply a result of the definition of
q
-beta specialization (
2.12
). Considering the scaling (
3.9
), under which
\mu_{1}-N
approaches in law the current of the ASEP with step initial data, the left hand side of (
5.7
) becomes

(5.9)

\mathbb{P}(-\mathsf{J}^{(\varepsilon)}(x,\tau)+\chi+S\leq r-N),

while the function
F(z)
becomes

(5.10)

F(z)=z^{N}(1+1/z)^{x}e^{\tau\frac{(1-q)}{2}\left(1-\frac{2z}{1+z}\right)+O(\varepsilon)}.

From the explicit expression (
4.5
) we see that rescaling
\tilde{K}(x+N,y+N)
and
r=s+N
one can prove the convergence

(5.11)

\det(1-\tilde{K})_{\ell^{2}(\mathbb{Z}^{\prime}_{>r})}\xrightarrow[\varepsilon\to 0]{}\det(1-K_{\mathrm{ASEP}})_{\ell^{2}(\mathbb{Z}^{\prime}_{>s})},

which combined with
proposition

3.3
, proves (
5.5
).
∎

Using
corollary

5.3
, along with
corollary

4.3
one can recover results found by Borodin and Olshanski in
[
BO17
, Theorem 10.2]
relating the integrated current of the ASEP with the discrete Laguerre ensemble. Similarly to the observation made at the end of
section

3.3
, one could use the correspondence between the
q
-Whittaker measure and the periodic Schur measure to deduce determinantal formula for the full hierarchy of solvable models descending from the stochastic six vertex model. Nevertheless we will not take this route in this paper having decided to treat only the most representative cases.

5.2.
Fredholm determinant representation for Log Gamma polymer in full space

We report a Fredholm determinant formula for the Laplace transform of the probability density function of the Log Gamma polymer partition function
Z(N,N)
. In literature a resembling expression was derived originally in
[
BCR13
]
, where the integral kernel possessed a single contour integral representation; see also
[
BCD21
]
. Compared to such knows formulas, the one we present below is noticeably simpler and the kernel possesses a double contour integral representation. This feature is a result of the direct relation between Log Gamma polymers, seen as a scaling limit of
q
-PushTASEP as in
proposition

3.5
, with free fermions at positive temperature.

Theorem 5.4
.

Let
Z(N,N)
be the partition function of the Log Gamma polymer model on the quadrant. Then we have

(5.12)

\mathbb{E}\left[e^{-e^{-\varsigma+\log Z(N,N)}}\right]=\det(1-\mathbf{K})_{\mathbb{L}^{2}(\varsigma,+\infty)},

where the kernel is given by

(5.13)

\mathbf{K}(X,Y)=\int_{\mathrm{i}\mathbb{R}-d}\frac{\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}Z}{2\pi\mathrm{i}}\int_{\mathrm{i}\mathbb{R}+d^{\prime}}\frac{\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}W}{2\pi\mathrm{i}}\frac{\pi}{\sin(\pi(W-Z))}\frac{g_{A,B}(Z)}{g_{A,B}(W)}e^{ZX-WY},

where
d,d^{\prime}>0
, such that
\frac{1}{2N}<d^{\prime}+d<1
and

(5.14)

g_{A,B}(Z)=\prod_{i=1}^{N}\frac{\Gamma(A_{i}+Z)}{\Gamma(B_{i}-Z)}.

The proof of
theorem

5.4
is based on a scaling limit of the Fredholm determinant formula (
5.1
). We will present it below in the subsection.

Remark 5.5
.

A Fredholm determinant representation analogous to (
5.12
) should be also available for the partition function
Z(T,N)
for generic
T,N
. In this case the kernel
\mathbf{K}
gets modified by changing the factor
g_{A,B}(Z)
into
\prod_{i=1}^{N}\Gamma(A_{i}+Z)\prod_{i=1}^{T}\Gamma(B_{i}-Z)^{-1}
. This claim should follow from an adaptation of arguments presented in the proof of
theorem

5.4
and technical results developed in
appendix

B
. We will not discuss any further such generalizations in this paper.

Remark 5.6
.

The Laplace transform in the left hand side of (
5.12
) admits an alternative expression using the identity

(5.15)

\mathbb{E}\left[e^{-e^{-\varsigma+U}}\right]=\mathbb{P}\left[U+\mathcal{G}\leq\varsigma\right],

which holds for any random variable
U
and
\mathcal{G}
is an independent standard Gumbel random variable. This can be seen as a continuous variant of the identity shown in
remark

5.2
.

Lemma 5.7
.

The Fredholm determinant of the kernel
\mathbf{K}
is absolutely convergent.

Proof.

Using the parameterization of integration variables
Z=-d+\mathrm{i}u
,
W=d+\mathrm{i}v
, with
d\in(\frac{1}{4},\frac{1}{2})
, we easily find the estimate

(5.16)

\begin{split}\left|\mathbf{K}(X,Y)\right|\leq e^{-d(X+Y)}\int_{\mathbb{R}}\frac{\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}u}{2\pi}\int_{\mathbb{R}}\frac{\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}v}{2\pi}&\left|\frac{\pi}{\sin\left[\pi(2d+\mathrm{i}(v-u))\right]}\right|\\
&\times\prod_{i=1}^{N}\left|\frac{\Gamma(A_{i}-d+\mathrm{i}u)}{\Gamma(B_{i}+d-\mathrm{i}u)}\frac{\Gamma(B_{i}-d-\mathrm{i}v)}{\Gamma(A_{i}+d+\mathrm{i}v)}\right|.\end{split}

The integral in the previous expression is bounded by a constant. To show this we use the estimates

(5.17)

\left|\frac{1}{\sin[\pi(\alpha+\mathrm{i}u)]}\right|\leq C_{\alpha}e^{-\pi|u|},

and

(5.18)

\left|\frac{\Gamma(a+\mathrm{i}u)}{\Gamma(b-\mathrm{i}u)}\right|\leq C_{a,b}\left(1+|u|\right)^{a-b}.

In the first inequelity
C_{\alpha}
is a constant depending on
\alpha\notin\mathbb{Z}
, while the second one holds for
a\notin\mathbb{Z}_{\leq 0}
and is a consequence of the following adaptation of the Stirling’s approximation

(5.19)

|\Gamma(a+\mathrm{i}u)|=\sqrt{2\pi}\,|u|^{a-\frac{1}{2}}e^{-a-\frac{|u|\pi}{2}}\left(1+O(|u|^{-1})\right).

Then, the integral in the right hand side of (
5.16
) is bounded by

(5.20)

C\int_{\mathbb{R}}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}u\int_{\mathbb{R}}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}ve^{-\pi|u-v|}\left(\frac{1+|u|}{1+|v|}\right)^{\sum_{i=1}^{N}A_{i}-B_{i}}(1+|u|)^{-2Nd}(1+|v|)^{-2Nd},

which is again bounded by a constant since
4Nd>1
, as it can be clearly seen after a change of variables
u\mapsto\frac{\varphi+\theta}{2},v\mapsto\frac{\varphi-\theta}{2}
. We have then shown that

(5.21)

\left|\mathbf{K}(X,Y)\right|\leq Ce^{-d(X+Y)},

where
C
is a constant independent of
X,Y
. We can now estimate the series expansion of the Fredholm determinant of
\mathbf{K}
as

(5.22)

\begin{split}&\sum_{\ell\geq 0}\frac{1}{\ell!}\int_{[\varsigma,+\infty)^{\ell}}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}X_{1}\cdots\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}X_{\ell}\left|\det[\mathbf{K}(X_{i},X_{j})]_{i,j=1}^{\ell}\right|\\
&\leq\sum_{\ell\geq 0}\frac{1}{\ell!}\int_{[\varsigma,+\infty)^{\ell}}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}X_{1}\cdots\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}X_{\ell}\,\ell^{\ell/2}C^{\ell}e^{-d(X_{1}+\cdots+X_{\ell})}\\
&=\sum_{\ell\geq 0}\frac{1}{\ell!}\ell^{\ell/2}\left(\frac{Ce^{-\varsigma d}}{d}\right)^{\ell}<\infty,\end{split}

where in the first inequality we used the Hadamard’s bound (
A.8
). This completes the proof.
∎

We now present the proof of
theorem

5.4
. For this we introduce the
q
-Gamma function
of a complex variable
X\in\mathbb{C}\setminus\mathbb{Z}_{\leq 0}

(5.23)

\Gamma_{q}(X)=(1-q)^{1-X}\frac{(q;q)_{\infty}}{(q^{X};q)_{\infty}}.

Arguments used to prove our formula (
5.12
) use a number of technical bounds on ratio of
q
-Pochhammer symbols and
q
-Gamma functions, which for the sake of readability are reported in
appendix

B
.

Proof of
theorem

5.4
.

Our starting point is the Fredholm determinant representation for the probability distribution of a tagged particle
\mathsf{x}_{N}(T)
in the
q
-PushTASEP (
5.1
).
Adopting the scaling

(5.24)

q=e^{-\varepsilon},\qquad a_{i}=e^{-\varepsilon A_{i}},\qquad b_{i}=e^{-\varepsilon B_{i}},\qquad\zeta=(1-q)^{2N}e^{-\varsigma},

we find that, assuming
\chi\sim q
-
\mathrm{Geo}(q)
and
S\sim\mathrm{Theta}(\zeta;q)
and using
proposition

3.5
,
lemmas

B.1
and
B.2
,

(5.25)

\mathsf{x}_{N}(N)+\chi+S=\varepsilon^{-1}\log Z(N,N)+\varepsilon^{-1}\mathcal{G}-\varepsilon^{-1}\varsigma+\varepsilon^{-1/2}\mathcal{N}(0,1)+O(1),

where
\mathcal{G}\sim\mathrm{Gumbel}
and
\mathcal{N}(0,1)
is a standard normal random variable.
In this way, the left hand side of (
5.1
) becomes

(5.26)

\begin{split}\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0}\mathbb{P}(\mathsf{x}_{N}(N)+\chi+S<N+s)&=\mathbb{P}(\log Z(N,N)+\mathcal{G}<\varsigma)\\
&=\mathbb{E}\left(e^{-e^{-\varsigma+\log Z(N,N)}}\right),\end{split}

where the second equality was discussed in
remark

5.6
and comes from a direct evaluation of the probability in the middle term using the definition of the Gumbel distribution.

We now move our attention to the right hand side of (
5.1
). Under the scaling (
5.24
) the integral kernel
K_{\mathrm{push}}
, using the notion of
q
-Gamma function (
5.23
), can be written as

(5.27)

\begin{split}K_{\mathrm{push}}(\varepsilon^{-1}X,\varepsilon^{-1}Y)=\frac{\varepsilon}{(2\pi\mathrm{i})^{2}}&\int_{\begin{subarray}{c}Z\in\mathcal{D}(-d,\varepsilon)\\
W\in\mathcal{D}(d,\varepsilon)\end{subarray}}\frac{\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}Z\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}W}{q^{(Z-W)/2}}e^{XZ-YW}\\
&\times\prod_{i=1}^{N}\frac{\Gamma_{q}(A_{i}+Z)\Gamma_{q}(B_{i}-W)}{\Gamma_{q}(B_{i}-Z)\Gamma_{q}(A_{i}+W)}\Gamma_{q}(Z-W)\Gamma_{q}(1+W-Z)\\
&\times(1-q)^{2N(Z-W)}\frac{\vartheta_{3}(q^{Z-W}e^{\varsigma}(1-q)^{2N};q)}{\vartheta_{3}(e^{\varsigma}(1-q)^{2N};q)},\end{split}

where, compared to (
4.5
), we operated a change of variables
z=q^{Z},w=q^{W}
, taking as radii of the integration contours
r=q^{d},r^{\prime}=q^{-d}
. For the integration contours we have used the notation

(5.28)

\mathcal{D}(d,\varepsilon)=d+\mathrm{i}[-\frac{\pi}{\varepsilon},\frac{\pi}{\varepsilon}].

By virtue of
lemmas

B.3
and
B.4
, the integrand in (
5.27
) converges pointwise to the integrand of (
5.13
). To prove the pointwise convergence
\varepsilon^{-1}K_{\mathrm{push}}(\varepsilon^{-1}X,\varepsilon^{-1}Y)\xrightarrow{\varepsilon\to 0}\mathbf{K}(X+\varsigma,Y+\varsigma)
we notice that the integrand in (
5.27
) is bounded in absolute value by

(5.29)

C_{1}e^{-d(X+Y)}\prod_{i=1}^{N}(1+|u|)^{-(B_{i}-A_{i})-2d}(1+|v|)^{-(A_{i}-B_{i})-2d}e^{-C_{2}|u-v|},

where
u=\Im\{Z\},v=\Im\{W\}
and
C_{1},C_{2}
are constants independent of
q,Z,W,X,Y
; this can be shown combining
lemmas

B.3
,
B.6
and
B.7
. The function in (
5.29
) is summable, as can be proven following the same ideas used in
lemma

5.7
.
Again bounding the integrand of (
5.27
) with (
5.29
) we obtain the bound

(5.30)

\left|\varepsilon^{-1}K_{\mathrm{push}}(\varepsilon^{-1}X,\varepsilon^{-1}Y)\right|\leq Ce^{-d(X+Y)},

which hold for a constant
C
independent of
\varepsilon,X,Y
. This implies that

(5.31)

\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0}\det(1-K_{\mathrm{push}})_{\ell^{2}(\mathbb{Z}_{>s}^{\prime})}=\det(1-\mathbf{K})_{\mathbb{L}^{2}(\varsigma,+\infty)},

by dominated convergence, concluding the proof.
∎

5.3.
Fredholm pfaffian representation for
q
-PushTASEP in half space

Here we present a Fredholm pfaffian formula for the distribution of the rightmost particle in the
q
-PushTASEP with particle creation. With the exception of
[
BBCW18
]
, where a Pfaffian representation was found for the current distribution of the ASEP with an open boundary kept at critical density,
corollary

5.8
below represents the first result of this kind for discrete solvable models and it holds for any admissible choice of parameters
\gamma,a_{1},a_{2},\dots
.

Corollary 5.8
.

Let
\mathsf{X}^{\mathrm{hs}}(T)
be the geometric
q
-PushTASEP with particle creation with parameters
\gamma,a_{1},a_{2},\dots\in(0,1)
and empty initial conditions. Consider also
\chi\sim q
-
\mathrm{Geo}(q)
and
S\sim\mathrm{Theta}(\zeta^{2},q^{2})
to be independent random variables. Then we have

(5.32)

\mathbb{P}\left(\mathsf{x}^{\mathrm{hs}}_{N}(N)-N+\chi+2S\leq s\right)=\mathrm{Pf}\left(J-L_{\mathrm{push}}\right)_{\ell^{2}(\mathbb{Z}^{\prime}_{>s})},

where
L_{\mathrm{push}}
is the
2\times 2
matrix kernel obtained from (
4.36
), setting

(5.33)

F(z)=\frac{(\gamma/z;q)_{\infty}}{(\gamma z;q)_{\infty}}\prod_{i=1}^{N}\frac{(a_{i}/z;q)_{\infty}}{(a_{i}z;q)_{\infty}}.

Proof.

As in
section

3.2
, we call
\gamma,a_{1},a_{2},\dots
the parameters describing the jump process in the
q
-PushTASEP with particle creation. Defining
a^{\prime}=(a_{1},\dots,a_{N},\gamma)
and taking
\tilde{\mu}\sim\mathbb{HW}^{(q)}_{a^{\prime};0}
we have the equality in distribution

(5.34)

\mathsf{x}^{\mathrm{hs}}_{N}(N)=\tilde{\mu}_{1}+N,

which follows combining
proposition

3.2
with the symmetry of the half space
q
-Whittaker measure of
proposition

2.7
. We can now use
theorem

4.11
to express the law of
\tilde{\mu}_{1}+\chi+2S
in terms of the rightmost particle in the Free boundary Schur measure whose Fredholm pfaffian representation is reported in
proposition

4.7
, concluding the proof.
∎

Remark 5.9
.

Notice that, unlike in the case of
q
-PushTASEP in full space, the left hand side of (
5.32
) is not the
q
-Laplace transform of the probability distribution of
\mathsf{x}^{\mathrm{hs}}_{N}(N)
.

The
q
-PushTASEP with particle creation was introduced and studied in
[
BBC20
]
. In the same paper authors, using Macdonald difference operators, recovered integral representations for joint
q
-moments of the model and a representation of the
q
-Laplace transform of the distribution of a tagged particle as a series of nested integrals; see
[
BBC20
, Corollary 4.7]
. Due to the presence of certain cross terms it was not possible for the authors to rearrange their expression as the expansion of a Fredholm pfaffian or determinant. Our result (
5.32
) indicates that an observable different than the
q
-Laplace transform of
\mathsf{x}^{\mathrm{hs}}_{N}(N)
has the potential to be studied through
q
-moment computations.

5.4.
Fredholm pfaffian representation for Log Gamma polymer in half space

Here we consider the point-to-point partition function
Z^{\mathrm{hs}}(N,N)
in the Log Gamma polymer in half space and we present Fredholm pfaffian representations for the Laplace transform of its probability distribution. First, in
theorem

5.10
, we will consider the case when the boundary parameter
\varUpsilon
is positive. To extend such solution to negative values of
\varUpsilon
requires an analytic continuation, which will lead to the result of
theorem

5.12
, presented in the next subsection. Formulas reported below are ameanable of asymptotic analysis, which will be performed in
section

6
.

Theorem 5.10
.

Let
Z^{\mathrm{hs}}(N,N)
be the partition function of the Log Gamma polymer model in half space with parameters
A_{1},\dots,A_{N}>0
and boundary strength
\varUpsilon>0
. Take a number
d

such that

(5.35)

0<d<\min\left\{\frac{1}{2},\varUpsilon,A_{1},\dots,A_{N}\right\}\qquad\text{and}\qquad d>\frac{1}{2N}.

Then, we have

(5.36)

\mathbb{E}\left[e^{-e^{-\varsigma+\log Z^{\mathrm{hs}}(N,N)}}\right]=\mathrm{Pf}(J-\mathbf{L})_{\mathbb{L}^{2}(\varsigma,+\infty)},

where
\mathbf{L}
is the
2\times 2
matrix kernel

(5.37)

\mathbf{L}(X,Y)=\left(\begin{matrix}\mathbf{k}(X,Y)&-\partial_{Y}\mathbf{k}(X,Y)\\
-\partial_{X}\mathbf{k}(X,Y)&\partial_{X}\partial_{Y}\mathbf{k}(X,Y)\end{matrix}\right)

with

(5.38)

\mathbf{k}(X,Y)=\int_{\mathrm{i}\mathbb{R}-d}\frac{\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}Z}{2\pi\mathrm{i}}\int_{\mathrm{i}\mathbb{R}-d}\frac{\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}W}{2\pi\mathrm{i}}e^{XZ+YW}g_{\varUpsilon;A}(Z)g_{\varUpsilon;A}(W)\mathsf{k}^{\mathrm{hs}}(Z,W),

(5.39)

g_{\varUpsilon;A}(Z)=\frac{\Gamma(\varUpsilon+Z)}{\Gamma(\varUpsilon-Z)}\prod_{i=1}^{N}\frac{\Gamma(A_{i}+Z)}{\Gamma(A_{i}-Z)}

and

(5.40)

\mathsf{k}^{\mathrm{hs}}(Z,W)=\Gamma(-2Z)\Gamma(-2W)\frac{\sin\left[\pi(Z-W)\right]}{\sin\left[\pi(Z+W)\right]}.

In literature explicit formulas for the expectation
\mathbb{E}\left[e^{-e^{-\varsigma}Z^{\mathrm{hs}}(N,N)}\right]
were derived in
[
OSZ14
, Corollary 5.6]
and
[
BBC20
, Corollary 6.40]
, although in none of these cases they were expressed as Fredholm pfaffians. It would be interesting to understand if it is possible to prove our formula (
5.58
) starting from these other explicit results. Moreover, recently in
[
BR22
]
authors were able to prove, using Bethe Ansatz, pfaffian formulas for a polymer model in half space with certain Beta weights. It would be interesting to understand if our techniques can be extended to prove identities for the Beta polymer model in half space.

The proof of
theorem

5.10
will be presented at the end of this subseciton. It is based on a scaling limit of the Fredholm pfaffian formula of
corollary

5.8
to establish which technical results reported in
appendix

B
will be used. In the next proposition, we confirm that the right hand side of (
5.36
) defines a numerically convergent series and hence the relation is well posed.

Proposition 5.11
.

The Fredholm pfaffian of the
2\times 2
matrix kernel
\mathbf{L}
is absolutely convergent.

Proof.

We make use of the asymptotic expansion (
5.19
) to estimate the integrand in (
5.38
). Following ideas already elaborated in the proof of
lemma

5.7
we obtain the bound

(5.41)

\begin{split}\left|\mathbf{k}(X,Y)\right|&\leq Ce^{-d(X+Y)}\int_{\mathbb{R}}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}u\int_{\mathbb{R}}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}v[(1+|u|)(1+|v|)]^{-2Nd-1/2}e^{-\pi|u+v|}\\
&\leq\frac{C}{2Nd}e^{-d(X+Y)},\end{split}

where
C
is a constant independent of
X,Y
.
Conditions (
5.35
) on parameter
d
imply that the double integral in the right hand side of the inequality above is convergent and bounded by a constant independent of
X,Y
. Through similar argument we easily get

(5.42)

|\mathbf{k}(X,Y)|,|\partial_{X}\mathbf{k}(X,Y)|,|\partial_{Y}\mathbf{k}(X,Y)|,|\partial_{X}\partial_{Y}\mathbf{k}(X,Y)|\leq De^{-d(X+Y)},

where
D
is another constant. We can now estimate each term of the expansion of the Fredholm pfaffian of
\mathbf{L}
and, using the Hadamard’s bound (
A.8
), we have

(5.43)

\begin{split}&\sum_{\ell\geq 0}\frac{1}{\ell!}\int_{[\varsigma,+\infty)^{\ell}}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}X_{1}\cdots\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}X_{\ell}\left|\mathrm{Pf}[\mathbf{L}(X_{i},X_{j})]_{i,j=1}^{\ell}\right|\\
&\leq\sum_{\ell\geq 0}\frac{1}{\ell!}\int_{[\varsigma,+\infty)^{\ell}}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}X_{1}\cdots\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}X_{\ell}(2\ell)^{\ell/2}D^{\ell}e^{-d(X_{1}+\cdots+X_{\ell})}\\
&=\sum_{\ell\geq 0}\frac{1}{\ell!}\ell^{\ell/2}\left(\frac{\sqrt{2}De^{-\varsigma d}}{d}\right)^{\ell}<\infty,\end{split}

which proves the result.
∎

Proof of
theorem

5.10
.

We proceed as in
theorem

5.4
. Our starting point in this case is the Fredholm pfaffian representation (
5.32
) of the probability distribution for the particle
\mathsf{x}^{\mathrm{hs}}_{N}(N)
of a
q
-PushTASEP with particle creation. Using the convergence result of
proposition

3.6
, along with
lemmas

B.1
and
B.2
, imposing the scaling,

(5.44)

q=e^{-\varepsilon},\qquad a_{i}=e^{-\varepsilon A_{i}},\qquad\gamma=e^{-\varepsilon\varUpsilon},\qquad\zeta=(1-q^{2})^{N}e^{-\varsigma/2},

we have

(5.45)

\mathsf{x}_{N}^{\mathrm{hs}}(N)-N+\chi+2S=\varepsilon^{-1}\left(\log Z^{\mathrm{hs}}(N,N)+\mathcal{G}-\varsigma+\sqrt{2\varepsilon}\mathcal{N}(0,1)\right)+O(1).

This shows that

(5.46)

\begin{split}\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0}\mathbb{P}(\mathsf{x}_{N}^{\mathrm{hs}}(N)-N+\chi+2S<s)&=\mathbb{P}(\log Z^{\mathrm{hs}}(N,N)+\mathcal{G}<\varsigma)\\
&=\mathbb{E}\left(e^{-e^{-\varsigma+\log Z^{\mathrm{hs}}(N,N)}}\right),\end{split}

and it takes care of the left hand side of (
5.32
). Let us now take the same limit on the right hand side. We can rewrite the
2\times 2
matrix kernel prescribed by
corollary

5.8
as

(5.47)

L_{\mathrm{push}}(\varepsilon^{-1}X,\varepsilon^{-1}Y)=\left(\begin{matrix}\varepsilon&-\nabla_{y}\\
-\nabla_{x}&\varepsilon^{-1}\nabla_{x}\nabla_{y}\end{matrix}\right)k(\varepsilon^{-1}X,\varepsilon^{-1}Y),

with

(5.48)

k(\varepsilon^{-1}X,\varepsilon^{-1}Y)=\frac{1}{(2\pi\mathrm{i})^{2}}\frac{\varepsilon^{2}}{(1-q)^{2}}\int_{\begin{subarray}{c}Z\in\mathcal{D}(-d,\varepsilon)\\
W\in\mathcal{D}(-d,\varepsilon)\end{subarray}}\frac{\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}Z\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}W}{q^{(Z+3W)/2}}e^{XZ+YW}f(Z)f(W)\tilde{\kappa}^{\mathrm{hs}}(Z,W).

Here, with respect to (
4.25
) we operated the change of variables
z=q^{Z},w=q^{W}
and we made use of auxiliary functions

\displaystyle\tilde{\kappa}^{\mathrm{hs}}(Z,W)=\frac{\Gamma_{q}(-2Z)\Gamma_{q}(-2W)\Gamma_{q}(1+Z+W)\Gamma_{q}(-Z-W)}{\Gamma_{q}(W-Z)\Gamma_{q}(1+Z+W)}E_{q}(\varsigma,Z+W),

\displaystyle f(Z)=\frac{\Gamma_{q}(\varUpsilon+Z)}{\Gamma_{q}(\varUpsilon-Z)}\prod_{i=1}^{N}\frac{\Gamma_{q}(A_{i}+Z)}{\Gamma_{q}(A_{i}-Z)},

\displaystyle E_{q}(\varsigma,U)=(1-q)^{2NU}\frac{\vartheta_{3}((1-q)^{2N}e^{-\varsigma}q^{2U};q^{2})}{\vartheta_{3}((1-q)^{2N}e^{-\varsigma};q^{2})}.

For the integration contours we used again the notation (
5.28
). Notice that the discrete derivatives
\nabla_{x}
and
\nabla_{y}
modify the function
k
by inserting respectively factors
\frac{1}{2}(q^{-Z}-q^{Z})\approx\varepsilon Z
and
\frac{1}{2}(q^{-W}-q^{W})\approx\varepsilon W
in the integrand. By means of estimates reported in
lemmas

B.3
and
B.7
and
proposition

B.8
we can bound the absolute value of the integrand in the expression of
k
with summable functions proving that

(5.49)

\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0}k(\varepsilon^{-1}X,\varepsilon^{-1}Y)=\mathbf{k}(X+\varsigma,Y+\varsigma),

by the bounded convergence theorem and moreover that

(5.50)

|k(\varepsilon^{-1}X,\varepsilon^{-1}Y)|\leq Ce^{-d(X+Y)},

for some constant
C
independent of
\varepsilon,X,Y
. Analogous arguments are applicable to other elements of the matrix kernel
L_{\mathrm{push}}
and we find

\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0}\left(\varepsilon^{-1}\nabla_{y}k(\varepsilon^{-1}X,\varepsilon^{-1}Y),\varepsilon^{-1}\nabla_{x}k(\varepsilon^{-1}X,\varepsilon^{-1}Y),\varepsilon^{-2}\nabla_{x}\nabla_{y}k(\varepsilon^{-1}X,\varepsilon^{-1}Y)\right),\\
=\left(\partial_{Y}\mathbf{k}(X+\varsigma,Y+\varsigma),\partial_{X}\mathbf{k}(X+\varsigma,Y+\varsigma),\partial_{X}\partial_{Y}\mathbf{k}(X+\varsigma,Y+\varsigma)\right)

and moreover

|\varepsilon^{-1}\nabla_{y}k(\varepsilon^{-1}X,\varepsilon^{-1}Y)|,|\varepsilon^{-1}\nabla_{x}k(\varepsilon^{-1}X,\varepsilon^{-1}Y)|,|\varepsilon^{-2}\nabla_{x}\nabla_{y}k(\varepsilon^{-1}X,\varepsilon^{-1}Y)|\leq Ce^{-d(X+Y)},

where the constant
C
can be chosen to be the same for all terms. These results imply, by bounded convergence, that

(5.51)

\lim_{\varepsilon\downarrow 0}\mathrm{Pf}(J-L_{\mathrm{push}})_{\ell^{2}(\mathbb{Z}^{\prime}_{>s})}=\mathrm{Pf}(J-\mathbf{L})_{\mathbb{L}^{2}(\varsigma,+\infty)},

proving the desired identity (
5.36
).
∎

5.5.
Analytic extension of Fredholm pfaffian

We now want to characterize the distribution of the half space polymer partition function
Z^{\mathrm{hs}}(N,N)
for choices of boundary parameter
\varUpsilon\leq 0
. This will require an analytic continuation of the relation (
5.36
). From the explicit representation of the function
\mathbf{k}
, we see that moving the parameter
\varUpsilon
from the positive to the negative real semi-axis several singularities for the
Z
and
W
variables cross the integration contours corresponding to values
-\varUpsilon,-\varUpsilon-1,\dots,-\varUpsilon-m+1
, if we assume that
\varUpsilon
ends up in the interval
(-m,-m+1]
. Defining functions

(5.52)

\mathbf{A}_{j}(X)=\frac{(-1)^{j}}{j!}e^{-(j+\varUpsilon)X}\frac{\Gamma(2\varUpsilon+2j)}{\Gamma(2\varUpsilon+j)}\prod_{i=1}^{N}\frac{\Gamma(A_{i}-\varUpsilon-j)}{\Gamma(A_{i}+\varUpsilon+j)},

(5.53)

\mathbf{B}(Y)=\int_{\mathrm{i}\mathbb{R}-d}\frac{\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}W}{2\pi\mathrm{i}}e^{YW}\Gamma(-2W)\frac{\Gamma(1-\varUpsilon+W)}{\Gamma(1-\varUpsilon-W)}\prod_{i=1}^{N}\frac{\Gamma(A_{i}+W)}{\Gamma(A_{i}-W)},

we evaluate the
Z
-residue at
-\varUpsilon-j
of the integrand of
\mathbf{k}
and from (
5.38
), we find it to be

(5.54)

\mathrm{Res}_{Z=-\varUpsilon-j}\left\{\parbox{62.59596pt}{\centering$Z$-integrand in \eqref{eq:k_hs_log_gamma}\@add@centering}\right\}=\mathbf{A}_{j}(X)\mathbf{B}(Y).

Analogously the
W
-residue at
-\varUpsilon-j
of the integrand of
\mathbf{k}
is

(5.55)

\mathrm{Res}_{W=-\varUpsilon-j}\left\{\parbox{62.59596pt}{\centering$W$-integrand in \eqref{eq:k_hs_log_gamma}\@add@centering}\right\}=-\mathbf{A}_{j}(Y)\mathbf{B}(X).

These computations allow us to identify the analytic continuation of the
2\times 2
matrix kernel
\mathbf{L}
, by adding residues
\mathbf{A}_{j}(X)\mathbf{B}(Y)-\mathbf{A}_{j}(Y)\mathbf{B}(X)
to the function
\mathbf{k}
in each component of (
5.37
). However, when
\varUpsilon
is negative and
j+\varUpsilon<0
we see that functions
\mathbf{A}_{j}(X)
’s are exponentially diverging as
X
grows. This means that the analytic continuation of
\mathbf{L}
naively proposed above produces an operator which is not well defined on
\mathbb{L}^{2}(\varsigma,\infty)\times\mathbb{L}^{2}(\varsigma,\infty)
and therefore we cannot immediately make sense of its Fredholm pfaffian. In the next theorem we resolve this issue and present the mathematically correct extension of identity (
5.36
). To state our result we introduce the scalar product
\langle(\cdot,\cdot)|(\cdot,\cdot)\rangle
on the Hilbert space
\mathbb{L}^{2}(\varsigma,\infty)\times\mathbb{L}^{2}(\varsigma,\infty)
as

(5.56)

\langle(f_{1},f_{2})|(g_{1},g_{2})\rangle=\int_{\varsigma}^{+\infty}\left[f_{1}(x)g_{1}(x)+f_{2}(x)g_{2}(x)\right]\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}x.

Theorem 5.12
.

Let
Z^{\mathrm{hs}}
be the partition function of the Log Gamma polymer model in half space with parameters
A_{1},\dots,A_{N}>0
and boundary strength
\varUpsilon
such that
-\min\{A_{1},\dots,A_{N}\}<\varUpsilon\leq 0
. Let
m\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}
and
\varepsilon\in\{0,1\}
be such that
\varUpsilon\in(-m+\frac{1-\varepsilon}{2},-m+\frac{2-\varepsilon}{2}]
and take a number
d
satisfying

(5.57)

0<d<\min\left\{\frac{1}{2},\varUpsilon+m,A_{1},\dots,A_{N}\right\}\qquad\text{and}\qquad d>\frac{1}{2N}.

Then, there exists
N^{*}
such that, for all
N\geq N^{*}
we have

(5.58)

\begin{split}\mathbb{E}\left[e^{-e^{-\varsigma+\log Z^{\mathrm{hs}}(N,N)}}\right]=&\mathrm{Pf}\left[J-\widehat{\mathbf{L}}\right]_{\mathbb{L}^{2}(\varsigma,+\infty)}\\
&\qquad\times\bigg(1-\langle(\mathbf{B},-\mathbf{B}^{\prime})|\left(1-J^{T}\widehat{\mathbf{L}}\right)^{-1}|(\mathbf{A}^{\prime},\mathbf{A})\rangle\bigg),\end{split}

where

(5.59)

\widehat{\mathbf{L}}(X,Y)=\mathbf{L}(X,Y)-\left(\begin{matrix}1&-\partial_{Y}\\
-\partial_{X}&\partial_{X}\partial_{Y}\end{matrix}\right)\sum_{j=m}^{2m+\varepsilon-2}\left[\mathbf{A}_{j}(X)\mathbf{B}(Y)-\mathbf{A}_{j}(Y)\mathbf{B}(X)\right],

(5.60)

\mathbf{A}(X)=\sum_{j=0}^{2m+\varepsilon-2}\mathbf{A}_{j}(X).

Notice that when
m=0
we have
\widehat{\mathbf{L}}=\mathbf{L}
, since the summations in (
5.59
) is empty.

Lemma 5.13
.

Consider parameters
A_{1},\dots,A_{N}>0
and
\varUpsilon>-\min\{A_{1},\dots,A_{N}\}
. Then there exists
N^{*}
, depending on
\varUpsilon
such that for each
N\geq N^{*}
the operator
\mathbf{L}
has norm
\|\mathbf{L}\|<1
.

Proof.

This is a straightforward consequence of bound (
5.41
) and of analogous estimates for partial derivatives of
\mathbf{k}
, which one can easily produce also when the parameter
\varUpsilon
becomes negative.
∎

Proof of
theorem

5.12
.

As hinted above before the statement of the theorem, identity (
5.58
) will follow from (
5.36
) after an analytic continuation in the parameter
\varUpsilon
to the region
\varUpsilon\leq 0
. We first observe that the Laplace transform
\mathbb{E}\left(e^{-e^{-\varsigma}Z^{\mathrm{hs}}(N,N)}\right)
is clearly an analytic function of the variable
\varUpsilon
in the region
\varUpsilon>-\min\{A_{1},\dots,A_{N}\}
, since the
\varUpsilon
dependence is given by boundary random variables (
3.15
). To show that the same is true for the Fredholm pfaffian in the right hand side of (
5.36
), we show that the right hand side of (
5.58
) is an analytic function of the variable
\varUpsilon
that is well defined for
\varUpsilon>-m
and that it equals
\mathrm{Pf}[J-\mathbf{L}]
, of (
5.36
) when
\Re\{\varUpsilon\}\in(0,1)
.

Let us start by assuming that parameters
A_{1},\dots,A_{N}>2m
. This hypothesis can be removed at any time through analytic continuation and it only serves to ensure that through the procedure we describe below we do not have to worry about possible singularities coming from factors
\Gamma(A_{i}+Z),\Gamma(A_{i}+W)
in the function
\mathbf{k}
in (
5.38
).
As discussed above, the problem with the argument presented prior to the statement of the theorem is that perturbations of the kernel
\mathbf{L}
given by combinations of functions
\mathbf{A}_{j},\mathbf{B}
and their derivatives define an unbounded operator when
\varUpsilon<0
. Let us then assume that
\varUpsilon>0
and define the kernel
\mathbf{R}(X,Y)

(5.61)

\mathbf{R}(X,Y)=\left(\begin{matrix}\mathbf{A}(X)\mathbf{B}(Y)-\mathbf{A}(Y)\mathbf{B}(X)&-\mathbf{A}(X)\mathbf{B}^{\prime}(Y)+\mathbf{A}^{\prime}(Y)\mathbf{B}(X)\\
-\mathbf{A}^{\prime}(X)\mathbf{B}(Y)+\mathbf{A}(Y)\mathbf{B}^{\prime}(X)&\mathbf{A}^{\prime}(X)\mathbf{B}^{\prime}(Y)-\mathbf{A}^{\prime}(Y)\mathbf{B}^{\prime}(X)\end{matrix}\right).

By simple estimates

(5.62)

|\mathbf{A}(X)|,|\mathbf{A}^{\prime}(X)|\leq De^{-\varUpsilon X},\qquad|\mathbf{B}(Y)|,|\mathbf{B}^{\prime}(Y)|\leq De^{-dY},

holding for a constant
D>0
, which can be made arbitrarily small choosing
N
large, we deduce that, in case
\varUpsilon>0
,
\mathbf{R}(X,Y)
defines a rank 2 operator
\mathbf{R}:\mathbb{L}^{2}(-\varsigma,+\infty)\times\mathbb{L}^{2}(-\varsigma,+\infty)\to\mathbb{L}^{2}(-\varsigma,+\infty)\times\mathbb{L}^{2}(-\varsigma,+\infty)
. Applying
proposition

A.2
we produce the identity

(5.63)

\begin{split}\mathrm{Pf}(J-\mathbf{L})&=\mathrm{Pf}(J-(\mathbf{L}-\mathbf{R})-\mathbf{R})\\
&=\mathrm{Pf}(J-(\mathbf{L}-\mathbf{R}))\bigg(1-\langle(\mathbf{B},-\mathbf{B}^{\prime})|(1-J^{T}(\mathbf{L}-\mathbf{R}))^{-1}|(\mathbf{A}^{\prime},\mathbf{A})\rangle\bigg).\end{split}

In the right hand side the inverse operator is defined by the Neumann series
(1-J^{T}(\mathbf{L}-\mathbf{R}))^{-1}=\sum_{\ell\geq 0}\left(J^{T}(\mathbf{L}-\mathbf{R})\right)^{\ell}
and such series is absolutely convergent for
N
large enough as a result of
lemma

5.13
and (
5.62
). We now show that the right hand side of (
5.63
) remains well defined and analytic even when we drag the parameter
\varUpsilon
in the region
\Re\{\varUpsilon\}<0
. First we focus on the kernel
\mathbf{L}-\mathbf{R}
. Moving
\varUpsilon
inside the segment
(-m+\frac{1-\varepsilon}{2},-m+\frac{2-\varepsilon}{2}]
, poles at
-\varUpsilon,-\varUpsilon-1,\dots,-\varUpsilon-m+1
cross the integration contours of the function
\mathbf{k}
so that the kernel
\mathbf{L}
becomes

(5.64)

\mathbf{L}+\left(\begin{matrix}1&-\partial_{Y}\\
-\partial_{X}&\partial_{X}\partial_{Y}\end{matrix}\right)\sum_{j=0}^{m-1}\left[\mathbf{A}_{j}(X)\mathbf{B}(Y)-\mathbf{A}_{j}(Y)\mathbf{B}(X)\right]

and hence the first
m
terms in the sum over
j
of
\mathbf{A}
(
5.60
) in
\mathbf{R}
cancel and
\mathbf{L}-\mathbf{R}
gets transformed into
\widehat{\mathbf{L}}
. This proves that,
for the parameter
\varUpsilon
in this parameter region, the right hand side of (
5.63
) is represented as

(5.65)

\mathrm{Pf}(J-\widehat{\mathbf{L}})\bigg(1-\langle(\mathbf{B},-\mathbf{B}^{\prime})|(1-J^{T}\widehat{\mathbf{L}})^{-1}|(\mathbf{A}^{\prime},\mathbf{A})\rangle\bigg),

as long as we can show that both factors in the right hand side are absolutely convergent. The kernel
\widehat{\mathbf{L}}
can be assumed to have operator norm
\|\widehat{\mathbf{L}}\|<1
, by choosing
N
large. This follows from
lemma

5.13
and from estimates

(5.66)

|\mathbf{A}_{j}(X)|,|\mathbf{A}_{j}^{\prime}(X)|\leq D^{\prime}e^{-(\varUpsilon+j)X},\qquad|\mathbf{B}(Y)|,|\mathbf{B}^{\prime}(Y)|\leq D^{\prime}e^{(\varUpsilon-\delta)Y},

where
D^{\prime}>0
can be taken small if
N
is large and the bound for functions
\mathbf{B},\mathbf{B}^{\prime}
is derived from (
5.53
) choosing integration contour
\mathrm{i}\mathbb{R}+\varUpsilon-\delta
, with
\delta\in(0,1)
. Thus, the first factor
\mathrm{Pf}(J-\widehat{\mathbf{L}})
is well defined. Showing that the scalar product
\langle(\mathbf{B},-\mathbf{B}^{\prime})|(1-J^{T}\widehat{\mathbf{L}})^{-1}|(\mathbf{A}^{\prime},\mathbf{A})\rangle
is well posed for
\varUpsilon<0
is a slightly more laborious task. As noted in (
5.62
), the functions
\mathbf{A},\mathbf{A}^{\prime}
diverge exponentially for
\varUpsilon<0
and hence we have to prove that for any
\ell\geq 0
the quantity

(5.67)

\langle(\mathbf{B},-\mathbf{B}^{\prime})|(J^{T}\widehat{\mathbf{L}})^{\ell}|(\mathbf{A}^{\prime},\mathbf{A})\rangle

is finite and exponentially decaying in
\ell
. When
\ell=0
the scalar product,

\langle(\mathbf{B},-\mathbf{B}^{\prime})|(\mathbf{A}^{\prime},\mathbf{A})\rangle,

is convergent since

(5.68)

\mathbf{B}(X)\mathbf{A}^{\prime}(X),\mathbf{B}^{\prime}(X)\mathbf{A}(X)=O(e^{-\delta X}),

which is a consequence of estimates (
5.62
), (
5.66
) respectively for functions
\mathbf{A},\mathbf{A}^{\prime}
and
\mathbf{B},\mathbf{B}^{\prime}
. Let us now consider the case
\ell=1
, which is illuminating to treat the general
\ell
case. We need to evaluate the asymptotic behavior of the function

(5.69)

\left(J^{T}\widehat{\mathbf{L}}\right)\left(\begin{matrix}\mathbf{A}^{\prime}\\
\mathbf{A}\end{matrix}\right)(X)=\int_{\varsigma}^{+\infty}J^{T}\widehat{\mathbf{L}}(X,Y)\left(\begin{matrix}\mathbf{A}^{\prime}(Y)\\
\mathbf{A}(Y)\end{matrix}\right)\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}Y.

For this we will deform the integration contours of function
\mathbf{k}
and its derivatives, entering coefficients of
\widehat{\mathbf{L}}
. Let us focus on the (1,1) element,

(5.70)

\widehat{\mathbf{L}}_{1,1}(X,Y)=\mathbf{k}(X,Y)-\sum_{j=m}^{2m+\varepsilon-2}\left[\mathbf{A}_{j}(X)\mathbf{B}(Y)-\mathbf{A}_{j}(Y)\mathbf{B}(X)\right].

Moving the
Z
and
W
integration contours for the integral expression of
\mathbf{k}
in (
5.38
) from
\mathrm{i}\mathbb{R}-d
to
\mathrm{i}\mathbb{R}+\varUpsilon-\delta
we cross a number of poles for the
Z
and
W
variables. These occur at points
-\varUpsilon-m,\dots,-\varUpsilon-2m+\varepsilon-2
and at points
Z=-W-1,\dots-W-2m-\varepsilon+2
, as a result of presence of functions
\Gamma(\varUpsilon+Z),\Gamma(\varUpsilon+W)
and
\frac{1}{\sin[\pi(W+Z)]}
. The crossing of the first set of poles produces residues that cancel out with summation in the right hand side of (
5.70
) and this is the motivation for including such terms in the kernel
\widehat{\mathbf{L}}
in the first place. Residues coming from poles
Z=-W-j
for
j=1,\dots,2m+\varepsilon-2
do not cancel out and their value is

(5.71)

S_{j}(X,Y)=-\int_{\mathrm{i}\mathbb{R}+R}\frac{\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}W}{2\pi\mathrm{i}}e^{YW-X(W+j)}g_{\varUpsilon;A}(W)g_{\varUpsilon;A}(-W-j)\frac{\Gamma(2W+2j)}{\Gamma(2W+1)},

where in the integration contour
R
can be taken in the interval
(\varUpsilon-1,-\varUpsilon-j+1).

We obtain the equality

(5.72)

\widehat{\mathbf{L}}_{1,1}(X,Y)=\mathbf{k}_{\varUpsilon-\delta}(X,Y)+\sum_{j=1}^{2m+\varepsilon-2}S_{j}(X,Y),

where
\mathbf{k}_{\varUpsilon-\delta}
denotes the function
\mathbf{k}
of (
5.38
) where contours are taken to be
\mathrm{i}\mathbb{R}+\varUpsilon-\delta
. Straightforward arguments lead to the following bounds for each term appearing in the right hand side of (
5.72
)

(5.73)

|\mathbf{k}_{\varUpsilon-\delta}(X,Y)|\leq Ce^{(\varUpsilon-\delta)(X+Y)},\qquad|S_{j}(X,Y)|\leq C\,e^{(-R-j)X+RY},

which we can combine to obtain

(5.74)

|\widehat{\mathbf{L}}_{1,1}(X,Y)|\leq C^{\prime}e^{(-R-1)X+RY}.

Similar arguments apply to all other components of the kernel
\widehat{\mathbf{L}}
, so that we get the estimate

(5.75)

\widehat{\mathbf{L}}(X,Y)=O\left(e^{(-R-1)X+RY}\right),

which holds for
\varUpsilon-\delta\leq R\leq-\varUpsilon
.
This allows us to evaluate expression (
5.69
) and choosing
R=\varUpsilon-\delta
we find

(5.76)

\left(J^{T}\widehat{\mathbf{L}}\right)\left(\begin{matrix}\mathbf{A}^{\prime}\\
\mathbf{A}\end{matrix}\right)(X)=O\left(e^{(-\varUpsilon+\delta-1)X}\right).

The argument produced above shows that the application of the operator
J^{T}\widehat{\mathbf{L}}
to the pair of functions
\left(\begin{matrix}\mathbf{A}^{\prime}\\
\mathbf{A}\end{matrix}\right)
lowers their exponential growth by a factor
e^{-(1-\delta)X}
. Iteratively, by properly choosing the value of
R
from time to time, we can then show that

(5.77)

\left(J^{T}\widehat{\mathbf{L}}\right)^{r}\left(\begin{matrix}\mathbf{A}^{\prime}\\
\mathbf{A}\end{matrix}\right)(X)=O\left(e^{-\varUpsilon X-r(1-\delta)X}\right)

for
r=1,2,\dots,M
, where
M
is the smallest integer such that
-\varUpsilon-M(1-\delta)<0
. Thanks to estimates (
5.77
) we can now prove that the scalar product (
5.67
) consists of absolutely convergent integrals for any
\ell\geq 0
and that

(5.78)

|\langle(\mathbf{B},-\mathbf{B}^{\prime})|(J^{T}\widehat{\mathbf{L}})^{\ell}|(\mathbf{A}^{\prime},\mathbf{A})\rangle|\leq\mathrm{const.}\|\widehat{\mathbf{L}}\|^{\ell-M}.

Since
\|\widehat{\mathbf{L}}\|<1
for
N
chosen properly large we conclude that the scalar product in the right hand side of (
5.65
) is well defined, and analytic in the parameter
\varUpsilon
whenever
\Re\{\varUpsilon\}>-m+\frac{1-\varepsilon}{2}
.
To conclude the proof we now like to remove the assumption
A_{1},\dots,A_{N}>2m
. This again can be done by analytic continuation of (
5.58
), noticing that, as long as
A_{i}+\varUpsilon>0
the expression in the right hand side does not possess any singularity. Taking parameters
A_{i}
uniformly larger than
-\varUpsilon
, say
A_{i}+\varUpsilon>\tilde{\delta}
for all
i\geq 1
and
\tilde{\delta}
fixed, we can choose
N
large enough so that the norm of
\widehat{\mathbf{L}}
stays smaller than one, condition necessary to make sense of terms in the right hand side of (
5.58
). This concludes the proof.
∎

6.
Asymptotic results

In this section we establish asymptotic limits of (some of) the solvable models in the KPZ class discussed in
section

3
. Most of our attention will be on models in half space as such results are new.

6.1.
Tracy-Widom distributions

Here we give the definition of Tracy-Widom distributions and of the so called Baik-Rains crossover distribution which will appear in subsequent subsections in the characterization of limiting fluctuations of KPZ models. Their expression is given in terms of Fredholm determinants or Fredholm pfaffians.

Definition 6.1
.

For any
p\in\mathbb{R}
and
\theta\in[0,2\pi]
, we define the curve
C_{p}^{\theta}\subset\mathbb{C}
as

(6.1)

C_{p}^{\theta}=\{p+|r|e^{\mathrm{sign}(r)\mathrm{i}\theta}:r\in\mathbb{R}\}.

We will use the notation
C_{p}^{\theta}
to denote integration contours and in these situations we will always assume the contour is positively oriented.

We recall the definition of the Airy function

(6.2)

\mathrm{Ai}(u)=\int_{C_{1}^{\pi/3}}e^{\frac{\alpha^{3}}{3}-u\alpha}\frac{\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\alpha}{2\pi\mathrm{i}},

of which we make extensive use below.

Definition 6.2
.

The GUE Tracy-Widom distribution is given by

(6.3)

F_{\mathrm{GUE}}(s)=\det\left(1-\mathcal{K}_{\mathrm{Airy}}\right)_{\mathbb{L}^{2}(s,+\infty)},

where
s\in\mathbb{R}
and
\mathcal{K}_{\mathrm{Airy}}
is the Airy kernel

(6.4)

\mathcal{K}_{\mathrm{Airy}}(u,v)=\int_{C^{2\pi/3}_{-1}}\frac{\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\alpha}{2\pi i}\int_{C^{\pi/3}_{1}}\frac{\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\beta}{2\pi i}\frac{e^{-\frac{\alpha^{3}}{3}+\alpha u+\frac{\beta^{3}}{3}-\beta v}}{\beta-\alpha}.

Notice that the integration contours do not intersect.

Frequently in literature the Airy kernel is defined as
\mathcal{K}_{\mathrm{Airy}}(u,v)=\int_{0}^{\infty}\mathrm{Ai}(u+\lambda)\mathrm{Ai}(v+\lambda)d\lambda
, but it is possible to see such representation is equivalent to (
6.4
).

We now define a family of probability distributions parameterized by a parameter
\xi\in[0,+\infty]
. This is the crossover distribution
F_{\mathrm{cross}}(s;\xi)
introduced for the first time in
[
BR01c
, Definition 4]
.

Definition 6.3
.

The Baik-Rains crossover distribution is the family of probability distributions on
s\in\mathbb{R}
given by

(6.5)

F_{\mathrm{cross}}(s;\xi)=\mathrm{Pf}\left(J-\mathcal{K}_{\mathrm{cross}}^{(\xi)}\right)_{\mathbb{L}^{2}(s,+\infty)}.

Here we take
\xi>0
and
\mathcal{K}_{\mathrm{cross}}^{(\xi)}
is the
2\times 2
matrix kernel

(6.6)

\mathcal{K}_{\mathrm{cross}}^{(\xi)}(u,v)=\left(\begin{matrix}\mathpzc{k}^{(\xi)}(u,v)&-\partial_{v}\mathpzc{k}^{(\xi)}(u,v)\\
-\partial_{u}\mathpzc{k}^{(\xi)}(u,v)&\partial_{u}\partial_{v}\mathpzc{k}^{(\xi)}(u,v)\end{matrix}\right),

where

(6.7)

\mathpzc{k}^{(\xi)}(u,v)=\frac{1}{4}\int_{C_{\delta}^{\pi/3}}\frac{\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\alpha}{2\pi\mathrm{i}}\int_{C_{\delta}^{\pi/3}}\frac{\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\beta}{2\pi\mathrm{i}}\frac{(\xi+\alpha)(\xi+\beta)}{(\xi-\alpha)(\xi-\beta)}\frac{\alpha-\beta}{\alpha\beta(\alpha+\beta)}e^{\frac{\alpha^{3}}{3}-\alpha u+\frac{\beta^{3}}{3}-\beta v}

and in the integration contour we chose
0<\delta<\xi
.

In literature, several equivalent representations for
F_{\mathrm{cross}}(s;\xi)
have appeared
[
BR01b
,
BBCS18
]
, although compared to these existing expressions (
6.5
) is new. The matrix kernel
\mathcal{K}_{\mathrm{cross}}^{(\xi)}
was first written in
[
KLD20
, eq. (71)]
, where authors conjectured the Fredholm pfaffian in the right hand side of (
6.5
) to be equal to the Baik-Rains crossover. Below, in
corollary

6.8
, we will prove such conjecture, hence justifying the definition of
F_{\mathrm{cross}}(s;\xi)
as Baik-Rains crossover.

The distribution
F_{\mathrm{cross}}(s;\xi)
is well defined also for
\xi=+\infty
or
\xi=0
, where it becomes respectively
F_{\mathrm{GSE}}(s)
and
F_{\mathrm{GOE}}(s)
, i.e. the Tracy-Widom distributions corresponding to the symplectic and orthogonal gaussian ensembles
[
TW96
]
. The limit
\xi\to+\infty
can be taken straightforwardly from (
6.5
) since the limiting form of
\mathcal{K}_{\mathrm{cross}}^{(\xi)}
is readily computed setting

(6.8)

\mathpzc{k}^{(\infty)}(u,v)=\lim_{\xi\to\infty}\mathpzc{k}^{(\xi)}(u,v)=\frac{1}{4}\int_{C_{1}^{\pi/3}}\frac{\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\alpha}{2\pi\mathrm{i}}\int_{C_{1}^{\pi/3}}\frac{\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\beta}{2\pi\mathrm{i}}\frac{\alpha-\beta}{\alpha\beta(\alpha+\beta)}e^{\frac{\alpha^{3}}{3}-\alpha u+\frac{\beta^{3}}{3}-\beta v}.

Definition 6.4
.

The GSE Tracy-Widom distribution has the expression

(6.9)

F_{\mathrm{GSE}}(s)=\mathrm{Pf}\left(J-\mathcal{K}_{\mathrm{GSE}}\right)_{\mathbb{L}^{2}(s,+\infty)},

where
\mathcal{K}_{\mathrm{GSE}}(u,v)=\lim_{\xi\to\infty}\mathcal{K}_{\mathrm{cross}}^{(\xi)}(u,v)
.

In order to specialize the Fredholm pfaffian expression of
F_{\mathrm{cross}}
to
\xi=0
, it requires to perform an analytic continuation of the kernel
\mathcal{K}_{\mathrm{cross}}^{(\xi)}
. When
\xi\to 0
, from the integral formula of function
\mathpzc{k}^{(\xi)}
we see that
\alpha
and
\beta
poles at
\xi
cross the integration contour and we have

(6.10)

\mathpzc{k}^{(0)}(u,v)=\lim_{\xi\to 0}\mathpzc{k}^{(\xi)}(u,v)=\mathpzc{k}^{(\infty)}(u,v)+\mathpzc{B}(u)-\mathpzc{B}(v),

where

(6.11)

\mathpzc{B}(u)=\int_{C_{1}^{\pi/3}}\frac{\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\alpha}{2\pi\mathrm{i}}\frac{e^{\frac{\alpha}{3}-\alpha u}}{2\alpha}=\frac{1}{2}\int_{0}^{+\infty}\mathrm{Ai}(\lambda+u)\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\lambda.

Definition 6.5
.

The GOE Tracy Widom distributions is given by

(6.12)

F_{\mathrm{GOE}}(s)=\mathrm{Pf}(J-\mathcal{K}_{\mathrm{GOE}})_{\mathbb{L}^{2}(s,+\infty)},

where
\mathcal{K}_{\mathrm{GOE}}(u,v)=\lim_{\xi\to 0}\mathcal{K}_{\mathrm{cross}}^{(0)}(u,v)
.

The expression given in
definition

6.4
for
F_{\mathrm{GSE}}
had appeared in
[
BBCS18
]
, whereas the expression of
definition

6.5
for
F_{\mathrm{GOE}}
is new. As for the case of
F_{\mathrm{cross}}^{(\xi)}
, in
[
KLD20
]
authors found the distribution in the right hand side of (
6.12
), conjecturing it to be the GOE Tracy-Widom distribution and we confirm this in
corollary

6.8
below.

6.2.
GUE Tracy-Widom asymptotics for models in full space

We recall the digamma function and the polygamma functions

(6.13)

\psi(z)=\frac{1}{\Gamma(z)}\frac{\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}}{\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}z}\Gamma(z),\qquad\text{and}\qquad\psi^{(n)}(z)=\frac{\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}^{n}}{\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}z^{n}}\psi(z).

Theorem 6.6
.

Let
Z(N,T)
be the partition function of the homogeneous Log Gamma polymer model in full space with parameters
A_{1}=A_{2}=\cdots=B_{1}=B_{2}=\cdots=A\in(0,\infty)
. Then, we have

(6.14)

\lim_{N\to\infty}\mathbb{P}\left[\frac{\log Z(N,N)+fN}{\sigma N^{1/3}}\leq s\right]=F_{\mathrm{GUE}}(s),

where
f=2\psi(A)
,
\sigma=-\left[\psi^{(2)}(A)\right]^{1/3}
.

The asymptotic behavior of the free energy of the point-to-point Log Gamma polymer partition function was derived already in
[
BCR13
,
KQ18
,
BCD21
]
, although in these works a different representation of the Fredholm determinant was used. Notably, the integral kernel utilized in the cited works, which dates back to
[
BC14
]
, has a complicated pole structure which makes the saddle point analysis rather involved. In fact in
[
BCR13
]
the limit (
6.14
) was established with a certain technical assumption on the parameter
A
, which was then removed in
[
KQ18
]
through a perturbation argument. In
[
BCD21
]
authors could prove, without restrictions on
A
the asymptotic limit directly from the explicit Fredholm determinant formula, though again through rather complicated contour deformations. We will see below that such issues do not arise using the formula derived in
theorem

5.4
making the evaluation of the asymptotic limit rather straightforward. Notice moreover that in
[
BCD21
]
authors treat a more general asymptotic limit than (
6.14
), as they consider the partition function
Z(T,N)
with
N\neq T
potentially. As discussed in
remark

5.5
a Fredholm determinant representation for the Laplace transform of the pdf of
Z(T,N)
with integral kernel of “free fermionic” type should be available and using that one should be able to simplify the asymptotic analysis of
[
BCD21
]
. We will not discuss such more general setting in this paper.

Proof.

We will compute the asymptotic limit of the exact formula given by
theorem

5.4
. Considering the scaling

(6.15)

\varsigma=-fN+\sigma N^{1/3}s,

we can compute the limit of the Laplace transform in the left hand side of (
5.12
), using equality (
5.15
), as

(6.16)

\lim_{N\to\infty}\mathbb{E}\left[e^{-e^{-\varsigma+\log Z(N,N)}}\right]=\lim_{N\to\infty}\mathbb{P}\left[\frac{\log Z(N,N)+fN}{\sigma N^{1/3}}\leq s\right].

We will now consider the asymptotic limit of the Fredholm determinant in the right hand side of (
5.12
). After a change of variables
X=\varsigma+u\sigma N^{1/3},Y=\varsigma+v\sigma N^{1/3}
we can rewrite the integral kernel
\mathbf{K}
as

(6.17)

\int_{\mathrm{i}\mathbb{R}-d}\frac{\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}Z}{2\pi\mathrm{i}}\int_{\mathrm{i}\mathbb{R}+d}\frac{\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}W}{2\pi\mathrm{i}}\frac{\pi}{\sin(\pi(W-Z))}\frac{e^{Nh(Z)+Z\sigma N^{1/3}(u+s)}}{e^{Nh(W)+W\sigma N^{1/3}(v+s)}},

where

(6.18)

h(Z)=\log\Gamma(A+Z)-\log\Gamma(A-Z)-fZ.

Under our assignment of parameters
f,\sigma
we see that
h(Z)
has a doubly critical point at
Z=0
and we have

(6.19)

h(0)=h^{\prime}(0)=h^{\prime\prime}(0)=0,\qquad h^{\prime\prime\prime}(0)=2\sigma^{3}>0.

Moreover, for any
d>0
, the function
\Re\{h(Z)\}
attains a maximum at
Z=-d
along the vertical line
\mathrm{i}\mathbb{R}-d
. This is a result of the evaluation of the derivative

(6.20)

\begin{split}\frac{\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}}{\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}y}\Re\left\{h(-d+\mathrm{i}y)\right\}&=\frac{\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}}{\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}y}\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\left(-\log\left|n+A-d+\mathrm{i}y\right|+\log\left|n+A+d-\mathrm{i}y\right|\right)\\
&=\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\frac{-4d(n+A)y}{[(n+A-d)^{2}+y^{2}][(n+A+d)^{2}+y^{2}]},\end{split}

which is clearly negative for
y>0
. Notice that in the first equality we made use of the product representation of the Gamma function
\Gamma(z)=z^{-1}\prod_{n\geq 1}(1+z/n)^{-1}(1+1/n)^{z}
. From these observations we easily see that, for
d
sufficiently close to
0
, the integration contour

(6.21)

\mathsf{C}^{2\pi/3}\left(-\frac{1}{\sigma N^{1/3}};d\right)\cup\mathsf{D}(-d,\sqrt{3}(d-\frac{1}{\sigma N^{1/3}}))

where

(6.22)

\mathsf{C}^{\varphi}(a;d)=C_{a}^{\varphi}\cap\{z:|\Re{z}|\leq d\}\qquad\text{and}\qquad\mathsf{D}(a,b)=\{a+\mathrm{i}y:|y|>b\}

is steep descent for the function
h(Z)
. For a representation of (
6.21
) see
fig.

5
. This allows us to evaluate the
Z
integral in the expression (
6.17
) via saddle point method. Clearly the same argument works for the evaluation of the
W
integral, in which case the integration contour is taken to be

(6.23)

\mathsf{C}^{\pi/3}\left(\frac{1}{\sigma N^{1/3}};d\right)\cup\mathsf{D}(d,\sqrt{3}(d-\frac{1}{\sigma N^{1/3}})),

as shown in
fig.

5
. The saddle point method shows that
Z
and
W
integrals in (
6.17
) are dominated by the contribution along the
\mathsf{C}^{2\pi/3}\left(-\frac{1}{\sigma N^{1/3}};d\right)
and
\mathsf{C}^{\pi/3}\left(\frac{1}{\sigma N^{1/3}};d\right)
sections of the contours. We can therefore rescale integration variables as

(6.24)

Z=-\frac{\alpha}{\sigma N^{1/3}},\qquad W=\frac{\beta}{\sigma N^{1/3}},

to compute the limiting form of the kernel
\mathbf{K}
as

(6.25)

\mathbf{K}(\varsigma+u\sigma N^{1/3},\varsigma+v\sigma N^{1/3})=\frac{1}{\sigma N^{1/3}}\mathcal{K}_{\mathrm{Airy}}(u+s,v+s)(1+O(N^{-1/3})).

The steep descent analysis allows also to recover an exponential bound

(6.26)

\left|\sigma N^{1/3}\mathbf{K}(\varsigma+u\sigma N^{1/3},\varsigma+v\sigma N^{1/3})\right|\leq Ce^{-c(u+v)},

where
C,c
are positive constants independent of
N,u,v
. The proof of the exponential inequality above is relatively straightforward and similar bounds were established, though in a slighlty different context, in
[
BFP07
]
.
We then conclude, by the bounded convergence theorem that

(6.27)

\lim_{N\to\infty}\det(1-\mathbf{K})_{\mathbb{L}^{2}(\varsigma,+\infty)}=\det(1-\mathcal{K}_{\mathrm{Airy}})_{\mathbb{L}^{2}(s,+\infty)}

and this proves (
6.14
).
∎

6.3.
Baik-Rains phase transition for half space models: zero temperature case

In this and the next subsections we undertake the evaluation of asymptotic limits of models in half space. In this subsection we focus on the simplest case of the Pfaffian Schur measure, i.e., the
q=0
case of the free boundary Schur measure, and we consider asymptotics of
\lambda_{1}
when
\lambda\sim\mathbb{FBS}^{(q=0)}_{(\bm{a},\gamma)}
. Through the Robinson-Schensted-Knuth algorithm it is known that
\lambda_{1}
describes the last passage percolation through a symmetric environment of geometric random variables; see
[
BBCS18
,
BBNV18
]
. Taking scaling limits or specializations of variables
\bm{a}
it is also known that
\lambda_{1}
describes the longest increasing subsequence of certain random involution
[
BR01c
]
or the height of a polynuclear growth model in half space at the origin
[
SI04
]
. The asymptotic limits of such models were all considered in the cited sources, although the precise form of the Fredholm pfaffian we consider below was not addressed. This represents a first motivation for us to address asymptotics of this classical model first. The new form of the matrix kernel
L
of
proposition

4.7
, combined with the use of the symmetry of the half space
q
-Whittaker measure of
proposition

2.7
leads us to discover new formulas for the limiting fluctuations of
\lambda_{1}
. Another motivation for studying asymptotics in the
q=0
case is that, since matrix kernels appearing in
corollary

5.8
,
theorem

5.10
and
theorem

5.12
all resemble each other, key ideas to compute their asymptotic limit can be given in the the simplest case.

Theorem 6.7
.

Let
\lambda
be distributed according to the free boundary Schur measure
\mathbb{FBS}^{(q=0)}_{(\bm{a},\gamma)}
with
q=0
. Here specialization
(\bm{a},\gamma)
, is
\bm{a}=(a_{1},\dots,a_{N})
with
a_{i}=a\in(0,1)
for
1\leq i\leq N
and
a\gamma\in(0,1)
. Then, the following limits hold

•

if
\gamma\in(0,1]
, rescaling
\gamma=1-\frac{\xi}{\sigma N^{1/3}}
, we have

(6.28)

\lim_{N\to\infty}\mathbb{P}\left[\frac{\lambda_{1}-NL}{\sigma N^{1/3}}\leq s\right]=F_{\mathrm{cross}}(s;\xi),

where
L=\frac{2a}{1-a}
and
\sigma=\frac{a^{1/3}(1+a)^{1/3}}{1-a}
;

•

if
\gamma\in(1,1/a)
then

(6.29)

\lim_{N\to\infty}\mathbb{P}\left[\frac{\lambda_{1}-NL_{\gamma}}{\sigma_{\gamma}N^{1/2}}\leq s\right]=\int_{-\infty}^{s}\frac{e^{-u^{2}/2}}{\sqrt{2\pi}}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}u,

where
L_{\gamma}=\frac{a(1-2a\gamma+\gamma^{2})}{(\gamma-a)(1-\gamma a)}
and
\sigma_{\gamma}=\frac{\sqrt{(1-a^{2})(\gamma^{2}-1)a\gamma^{3}}}{(\gamma-a)(1-\gamma a)}
.

Proof.

From
proposition

4.7
, for any
t\in\mathbb{R}
, we write,

(6.30)

\mathbb{P}(\lambda_{1}\leq t)=\mathrm{Pf}[J-\bar{L}]_{\ell^{2}(\mathbb{Z}^{\prime}_{>t})}.

The
2\times 2
matrix kernel is given by

(6.31)

\bar{L}(x,y)=\left(\begin{matrix}\bar{k}(x,y)&-\nabla_{y}\bar{k}(x,y)\\
-\nabla_{x}\bar{k}(y,x)&\nabla_{x}\nabla_{y}\bar{k}(x,y)\end{matrix}\right),

where

(6.32)

\begin{split}\bar{k}(x,y)=\oint_{|z|=r}\frac{\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}z}{2\pi\mathrm{i}\sqrt{z}}\oint_{|w|=r}&\frac{\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}w}{2\pi\mathrm{i}\sqrt{w}}e^{Ng(z)-x\log z}e^{Ng(w)-y\log w}\\
&\times\frac{(z-\gamma)(w-\gamma)}{(1-\gamma z)(1-\gamma w)}\frac{z-w}{(z^{2}-1)(w^{2}-1)(zw-1)},\end{split}

and

(6.33)

g(z)=\log(1-a/z)-\log(1-az).

We will prove large
N
asymptotics of the probability distribution of
\lambda_{1}
separately, depending on the value of
\gamma
.

0\leq\gamma<1
: convergence to crossover distribution.

In order to prove (
6.28
), we consider in (
6.30
) the scaling

(6.34)

t=NL+\sigma N^{1/3}s,

so that variable of kernel
\bar{L}
are rescaled as

(6.35)

x=NL+\sigma N^{1/3}u,\qquad y=NL+\sigma N^{1/3}v,

for
u,v\in(s,\infty)
.
Isolating the term of order
N
in the exponent of integral expressions for
\bar{k}
, we define the function

(6.36)

h(z)=g(z)-L\log z.

Simple computations show that
h
has a double critical point at
z=1
and we have

(6.37)

h(1)=h^{\prime}(1)=h^{\prime\prime}(1)=0,\qquad h^{\prime\prime\prime}(1)=2\sigma^{3}>0.

Moreover, for any
r>1
, the complex curve
|z|=r
is steep descent for
\Re\{h(z)\}
since

(6.38)

\frac{\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}}{\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\theta}\Re\{h(re^{\mathrm{i}\theta})\}=\frac{a(1-a^{2})\sin\theta}{|1-\frac{a}{r}e^{-\mathrm{i}\theta}|^{2}|1-are^{\mathrm{i}\theta}|^{2}}\left(r^{-1}-r\right)<0,\qquad\text{for }\theta\in(0,\pi).

Define the contour

(6.39)

C^{\pi/3}\left(1+\frac{\delta}{\sigma N^{1/3}};r\right)\cup D(\bar{\theta},r),

where

(6.40)

C^{\varphi}(a;r)=\{z=a+\rho e^{\mathrm{sign}(\rho)\mathrm{i}\varphi}:|z|<r\},\qquad D(\varphi,r)=\{re^{i\theta}:\theta\in(\varphi,2\pi-\varphi)\},

and
\bar{\theta}>0
solves the relation
1+\frac{\delta}{\sigma N^{1/3}}+\rho e^{\mathrm{i}\frac{\pi}{3}}=re^{\mathrm{i}\bar{\theta}}
for some
\rho>0
; see
fig.

5
.
Properties (
6.37
), (
6.38
) of function
h
, imply that for
r
sufficiently close to 1, but still
r-1=O(1)
, the contour (
6.39
) is steep descent for the function
h
. We now deform integration contours in (
6.32
) to be that of (
6.39
), both for the
z
and
w
variables. Here we assume that
\delta\in(0,\xi)
, so that the pole
\gamma^{-1}\approx 1+\frac{\xi}{\sigma N^{1/3}}
lies outside of the integration contour as in
fig.

5
. When
N
grows such integrals are dominated by the contribution over the curve
C^{\pi/3}\left(1+\frac{\delta}{\sigma N^{1/3}};r\right)
, in the vicinity of the critical points
z=1,w=1
. We can therefore rescale integration variables as

(6.41)

z=1+\frac{\alpha}{\sigma N^{1/3}},\qquad w=1+\frac{\beta}{\sigma N^{1/3}},

so that, thanks to (
6.37
), the exponents in integrands of
\bar{k}
become

(6.42)

Ng(z)-x\log z=\frac{\alpha^{3}}{3}-\alpha u+O(N^{-1/3}),\qquad Ng(w)-y\log w=\frac{\beta^{3}}{3}-\beta v+O(N^{-1/3}).

Expanding in the new variables
\alpha,\beta
the remaining factors in the integrands of (
6.32
) we find

(6.43)

\begin{split}&\frac{1}{\sqrt{zw}}\frac{(z-\gamma)(w-\gamma)}{(1-\gamma z)(1-\gamma w)}\frac{z-w}{(z^{2}-1)(w^{2}-1)(zw-1)}\\
&\hskip 170.71652pt=\frac{(\xi+\alpha)(\xi+\beta)}{(\xi-\alpha)(\xi-\beta)}\frac{\alpha-\beta}{4\alpha\beta(\alpha+\beta)}+O(\frac{1}{\sigma N^{1/3}}),\end{split}

proving the pointwise convergence

(6.44)

\bar{k}(x(u),y(v))\xrightarrow[N\to\infty]{}\mathpzc{k}^{(\xi)}(u,v).

Such convergence result can be strengthened and it holds uniformly for
u,v>s
, thanks to the exponential decay

(6.45)

\left|\bar{k}(x(u),y(v))\right|\leq Ce^{-\delta(u+v)},

which follows again from the steep descent property of the integration contour.

Analogous estimates, for the remaining entries of the kernel
\bar{L}
can be readily produced to show the convergence,

(6.46)

-\sigma N^{1/3}\nabla_{y}\bar{k}(x(u),y(v))\xrightarrow[N\to\infty]{}-\partial_{v}\mathpzc{k}^{(\xi)}(u,v),

(6.47)

-\sigma N^{1/3}\nabla_{x}\bar{k}(x(u),y(v))\xrightarrow[N\to\infty]{}-\partial_{u}\mathpzc{k}^{(\xi)}(u,v),

(6.48)

\sigma^{2}N^{2/3}\nabla_{x}\nabla_{y}\bar{k}(x(u),y(v))\xrightarrow[N\to\infty]{}\partial_{u}\partial_{v}\mathpzc{k}^{(\xi)}(u,v),

which again hold uniformly for
u,v>s
. Exponential decay for
u,v\to\infty
of terms in the left hand side of (
6.46
), (
6.47
), (
6.48
) is also easily derived from the steep descent of integration contour. Such convergence results for the kernel
\bar{L}
are sufficient to establish convergence of Fredholm pfaffians

(6.49)

\mathrm{Pf}\left[J-\bar{L}\right]_{\ell^{2}(\mathbb{Z}^{\prime}_{\geq t})}\xrightarrow[N\to\infty]{}\mathrm{Pf}\left[J-\mathcal{K}^{(\xi)}_{\mathrm{cross}}\right]_{\mathbb{L}^{2}{(s,+\infty)}},

proving (
6.28
) for
\gamma\in[0,1)
.

(a)

(b)

Figure 5.
In the left panel the contours described in (
6.23
) are drawn. The right panel represents the conoturs of (
6.39
).

\gamma=1
: convergence to GOE Tracy-Widom distribution.
In order to set
\gamma=1
in the Fredholm pfaffian formula (
6.30
) for the probability distribution of
\lambda_{1}
we need need to compute the analytic extension of the function
\bar{k}
in the the parameter
\gamma
, which we denote by
\bar{k}_{\mathrm{ext}}(x,y)
. From the integral expression (
6.32
) we see that
1/\gamma
is a singularity for the integrand for both variables
z
and
w
. Therefore when
1/\gamma<r
, the extended function
\bar{k}_{\mathrm{ext}}(x,y)
equals
k(x,y)
plus the residues of the integrand at poles
z=1/\gamma
and
w=1/\gamma
, i.e.

(6.50)

\bar{k}_{\mathrm{ext}}(x,y)=\bar{k}(x,y)-A(x)B(y)+A(y)B(x),

where

(6.51)

A(x)=e^{Ng(1/\gamma)-x\log 1/\gamma}\qquad\text{and}\qquad B(y)=\sqrt{\gamma}\oint_{|w|=r}\frac{\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}w}{2\pi\mathrm{i}\sqrt{w}}\frac{e^{Ng(w)-y\log w}}{w^{2}-1}.

Denoting by
\bar{L}_{\mathrm{ext}}
the analytic extension of the matrix kernel
\bar{L}
in the parameter
\gamma
we have

(6.52)

\bar{L}_{\mathrm{ext}}(x,y)=\bar{L}(x,y)+\bar{R}(x,y),

where

(6.53)

\bar{R}(x,y)=-\left(\begin{matrix}1&-\nabla_{y}\\
-\nabla_{x}&\nabla_{x}\nabla_{y}\end{matrix}\right)[A(x)B(y)-A(y)B(x)].

When
\gamma<1
, functions
A,B
are exponentially decaying and they belong to
\ell^{2}\left(\mathbb{Z}_{>t}^{\prime}\right)
. Setting
\gamma=1
, we see that the function
A
becomes constant
A\equiv 1
and hence the kernel
\bar{R}(x,y)
no longer defines an operator
\ell^{2}\left(\mathbb{Z}_{>t}^{\prime}\right)\to\ell^{2}\left(\mathbb{Z}_{>t}^{\prime}\right)
. Nevertheless we now show that the Fredholm pfaffian
\mathrm{Pf}[J-\bar{L}_{\mathrm{ext}}\big|_{\gamma=1}]
still consists in an absolutely convergent series and it is therefore well defined. First let’s compute, though saddle point method the asymptotic form of
\bar{k}
and
B
. Using again the scaling (
6.34
), (
6.35
) and following the same procedure described for the case
\gamma<1
, we find

(6.54)

\bar{k}(x(u),y(v))\xrightarrow[N\to\infty]{}\mathpzc{k}^{(\infty)}(u,v)

and

(6.55)

B(x(u))\xrightarrow[N\to\infty]{}\mathpzc{B}(u),

where the function
\mathpzc{B}
was defined around (
6.10
). Such convergence results hold uniformly for
u>s
as a result of exponential bounds

(6.56)

|\bar{k}(x(u),y(v))|\leq Ce^{-\delta(u+v)}\qquad\text{and}\qquad|B(x(u))|\leq Ce^{-\delta u},

which again follow by steep descent analysis. Analogous convergence statements hold also for discrete derivatives of
\bar{k},q
, and in general we find

(6.57)

\left(\begin{matrix}\bar{k}_{\mathrm{ext}}(x(u),y(v))&-\sigma N^{1/3}\nabla_{y}\bar{k}_{\mathrm{ext}}(x(u),y(v))\\
-\sigma N^{1/3}\nabla_{x}\bar{k}_{\mathrm{ext}}(x(u),y(v))&\sigma^{2}N^{2/3}\nabla_{x}\nabla_{y}\bar{k}_{\mathrm{ext}}(x(u),y(v))\end{matrix}\right)\xrightarrow[N\to\infty]{}\mathcal{K}_{\mathrm{GOE}}(u,v)

Let us now prove that
\mathrm{Pf}[J-\bar{L}_{\mathrm{ext}}\big|_{\gamma=1}]
is absolutely convergent for any
N
, so that the claim
\lim_{N\to\infty}\mathrm{Pf}[J-\bar{L}_{\mathrm{ext}}\big|_{\gamma=1}]=\mathrm{Pf}[J-\mathcal{K}_{\mathrm{GOE}}]
will follow from the bounded convergence theorem. Observe that, for any
\ell\geq 0
, using (
4.40
), we have

(6.58)

\mathrm{Pf}[\bar{L}_{\mathrm{ext}}(x_{i},x_{j})\big|_{\gamma=1}]_{i,j=1}^{\ell}=\mathrm{Pf}[\bar{L}(x_{i},x_{j})]_{i,j=1}^{\ell}+\sum_{1\leq a<b\leq 2\ell}(-1)^{a+b-1}\mathrm{Pf}[\bar{R}^{a,b}]\mathrm{Pf}[\bar{L}^{\widehat{a},\widehat{b}}],

where
R^{a,b}
is the
2\times 2
minor of
[\bar{R}(x_{i},x_{j})]_{i,j=1}^{\ell}
of elements at rows and columns
a,b
, while
\bar{L}^{\widehat{a},\widehat{b}}
is the minor of
[\bar{L}(x_{i},x_{j})]_{i,j=1}^{\ell}
obtained erasing rows and columns
a,b
. This follows from the fact that every minor of
[\bar{R}(x_{i},x_{j})]_{i,j=1}^{\ell}
of size larger than 2 has vanishing pfaffian, for rank reasons. Setting
a=2(i-1)+\varepsilon_{1}
and
b=2(j-1)+\varepsilon_{2}
we see that
\mathrm{Pf}[\bar{R}^{a,b}]
can only be equal to

(6.59)

-B(x_{i})+B(x_{j}),\qquad\nabla B(x_{j}),\qquad-\nabla B(x_{i}),\qquad 0

respectively for
(\varepsilon_{1},\varepsilon_{2})=(1,1),(1,2),(2,1),(2,2)
. Using bounds (
6.56
) and Hadamard’s inequality (
A.8
) we can estimate (
6.58
) as

(6.60)

\left|\mathrm{Pf}[\bar{L}_{\mathrm{ext}}(x(u_{i}),x(u_{j}))\big|_{\gamma=1}]_{i,j=1}^{\ell}\right|\leq\left[(2\ell)^{\ell/2}+\binom{2\ell}{2}2(2\ell-2)^{\frac{\ell-1}{2}}\right]C^{\ell}e^{-\delta(u_{1}+\cdots+u_{\ell})},

which proves that the series

(6.61)

\sum_{\ell\geq 0}\frac{1}{\ell!}\int_{[s,+\infty)^{\ell}}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}u_{1}\cdots\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}u_{\ell}\left|\mathrm{Pf}[\bar{L}_{\mathrm{ext}}(x(u_{i}),x(u_{j}))\big|_{\gamma=1}]_{i,j=1}^{\ell}\right|,

converges. Since
\bar{L}_{\mathrm{ext}}\big|_{\gamma=1}
converges uniformly to
\mathcal{K}_{\mathrm{GOE}}
, this proves that its Fredholm pfaffian, converges to
F_{\mathrm{GOE}}(s)
, proving (
6.28
) for
\gamma=1
.

\gamma>1
: convergence to gaussian distribution.
We have seen in (
6.52
) that, in case
\gamma\geq 1
, the analytic extension of the matrix kernel
\bar{L}
results in the addition of a rank 2 perturbation. Here we show that when
\gamma>1
such perturbation provides the dominant contribution to the Fredholm pfaffian of
\bar{L}_{\mathrm{ext}}
. Following ideas already elaborated in the proof of
theorem

5.12
, we need first to make sense of the quantity
\mathrm{Pf}[J-\bar{L}_{\mathrm{ext}}]_{\ell^{2}(\mathbb{Z}_{\geq t})}
, since when
\gamma>1
, the functions
A(x)
appearing in the rank 2 kernel
\bar{R}(x,y)
are diverging. Consider the scaling

(6.62)

t=L_{\gamma}N+\sigma_{\gamma}N^{1/2}s,\qquad x=L_{\gamma}N+\sigma_{\gamma}N^{1/2}u,\qquad y=L_{\gamma}N+\sigma_{\gamma}N^{1/2}v.

Introducing the function

(6.63)

h_{\gamma}(z)=g(z)-L_{\gamma}\log z,

we rewrite functions
\bar{k},A,B
as

(6.64)

\begin{split}\bar{k}(x(u),y(v))=\oint_{|z|=r}\frac{\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}z}{2\pi\mathrm{i}\sqrt{z}}\oint_{|w|=r}&\frac{\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}w}{2\pi\mathrm{i}\sqrt{w}}e^{N\left(h_{\gamma}(z)+h_{\gamma}(w)\right)-\sigma_{\gamma}N^{1/2}(u\log z+v\log w)}\\
&\times\frac{(z-\gamma)(w-\gamma)}{(1-\gamma z)(1-\gamma w)}\frac{z-w}{(z^{2}-1)(w^{2}-1)(zw-1)},\end{split}

(6.65)

A(x(u))=e^{Nh_{\gamma}(1/\gamma)-\sigma_{\gamma}N^{1/2}u\log 1/\gamma},\qquad B(y(v))=\sqrt{\gamma}\oint_{|w|=r}\frac{\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}w}{2\pi\mathrm{i}\sqrt{w}}\frac{e^{Nh_{\gamma}(w)-\sigma_{\gamma}N^{1/2}y\log w}}{w^{2}-1}.

It is clear that, when
\gamma<1
the kernel
\bar{L}_{\mathrm{ext}}
with such scaling, defines a bounded operator as all functions are exponentially decaying in the variables
u,v>s
. Using
proposition

A.2
, we write

(6.66)

\mathrm{Pf}[J-\bar{L}_{\mathrm{ext}}]=\mathrm{Pf}[J-\bar{L}]\left(1-\langle(B,-\nabla B)|(1-J^{T}\bar{L})^{-1}|(\nabla A,A)\rangle\right),

where all operators and the scalar product are defined on the Hilbert space of pairs of square summable functions of variable
u
such that
x>t
under scaling (
6.62
).
On the right hand side the inverse operator is defined by the Neumann series

(6.67)

(1-J^{T}\bar{L})^{-1}=\sum_{\ell\geq 0}(J^{T}\bar{L})^{\ell}.
