This series is well defined as, for
N
large enough the operator norm of
J^{T}\bar{L}
can be made arbitrarily small, as a result of presence of factors
e^{N(h_{\gamma}(z)+h_{\gamma}(w))}
for
|z|,|w|>1
in the expression of function
\bar{k}
. We can now extend expression in the right hand side of (
6.66
) allowing
\gamma>1
. In this case we can evaluate the asymptotic limit of functions
\bar{k},A,B
using steep descent analysis. Simple computations show that
h_{\gamma}
satisfies some basic properties as

(6.68)

h_{\gamma}(1/z)=-h_{\gamma}(z),\qquad h_{\gamma}(\gamma)<0,\qquad h^{\prime}_{\gamma}(\gamma)=0,\qquad h^{\prime\prime}_{\gamma}(\gamma)=\frac{\sigma_{\gamma}^{2}}{\gamma^{2}}>0.

Unlike for the case
\gamma\leq 1
, this time we choose as a integration contour the complex curve
|z|=\gamma-\frac{1}{\sigma_{\gamma}N^{1/2}}
which is steep descent for the function
\Re\{h_{\gamma}(z)\}
, following the same computation as in (
6.38
). This justifies the change of integration variables,

(6.69)

z=\gamma\left(1-\frac{\alpha}{\sigma_{\gamma}N^{1/2}}\right),\qquad w=\gamma\left(1-\frac{\beta}{\sigma_{\gamma}N^{1/2}}\right).

With such rescaling in place, we see that the exponential terms in each integral in (
6.64
), (
6.65
) become

(6.70)

Nh_{\gamma}(z)-\sigma N^{1/2}u\log z=Nh_{\gamma}(\gamma)-\sigma_{\gamma}N^{1/2}u\log\gamma+\frac{1}{2}\alpha^{2}+u\alpha

and analogously

(6.71)

Nh_{\gamma}(w)-\sigma N^{1/2}v\log w=Nh_{\gamma}(\gamma)-\sigma_{\gamma}N^{1/2}v\log\gamma+\frac{1}{2}\beta^{2}+v\beta.

When
N
is large we obtain

(6.72)

\bar{k}(x(u),y(v))=O\left(N^{-5/2}e^{2Nh_{\gamma}(\gamma)-\sigma_{\gamma}N^{1/2}(u+v)\log\gamma-c(u^{2}+v^{2})}\right),

where
c\in(0,1/2)
and

(6.73)

A(x(u))=e^{-Nh(\gamma)+\sigma N^{1/2}u\log\gamma},

(6.74)

B(y(v))=\frac{1}{\sigma_{\gamma}N^{1/2}}\frac{1}{(\gamma-1/\gamma)\sqrt{2\pi}}e^{Nh(\gamma)-\sigma N^{1/2}v\log\gamma-v^{2}/2}(1+O(N^{-1/2})).

Notice that the effect of discrete derivatives in the limit reduces to a multiplication by a factor
\frac{1}{2}(\gamma-1/\gamma)
and in particular we have

(6.75)

\nabla_{x}A(x(u))\approx\frac{1}{2}(\gamma-1/\gamma)A(x(u)),\qquad\nabla_{y}B(y(v))\approx-\frac{1}{2}(\gamma-1/\gamma)B(y(v)),

where
\approx
denotes an approximation up to errors of order
N^{-1/2}
. We can now use estimates (
6.72
), (
6.73
), (
6.74
) (
6.75
) to evaluate the right hand side of (
6.66
) obtaining

(6.76)

\lim_{N\to\infty}\mathrm{Pf}[J-\bar{L}]=1

and

(6.77)

\begin{split}\lim_{N\to\infty}\left(1-\langle(B,-\nabla B)|(1-J^{T}\bar{L})^{-1}|(\nabla A,A)\rangle\right)&=\lim_{N\to\infty}\left(1-\langle(B,-\nabla B)|(\nabla A,A)\rangle\right)\\
&=1-\int_{s}^{\infty}\frac{e^{-v^{2}/2}}{\sqrt{2\pi}}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}v.\end{split}

This concludes the proof of (
6.29
) and hence of the theorem.
∎

Corollary 6.8
.

For any
\xi\in[0,+\infty]
, the definition of Baik-Rains crossover distribution
F_{\mathrm{cross}}(\,\cdot\,;\xi)
given in
definition

6.3
is equivalent to that of
F^{\hbox to6.09pt{\vbox to6.09pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.2pt\lower-0.2pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {}{{}}{}
{}{}
{}{}
{}{}
{}{}{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 0 L 7.87 0 L 7.87 7.87 L 0 7.87 L 0 0}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
{}{{}}{}
{}{}{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 0 L 7.87 7.87}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}(\,\cdot\,;\xi)
given in
[
BR01c
, Definition 4]
.

Proof.

In
[
BR01c
]
authors proved that the crossover distribution arises as limiting distribution of the longest increasing subsequence of a random involution, there denoted by
L^{\hbox to6.09pt{\vbox to6.09pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.2pt\lower-0.2pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {}{{}}{}
{}{}
{}{}
{}{}
{}{}{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 0 L 7.87 0 L 7.87 7.87 L 0 7.87 L 0 0}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
{}{{}}{}
{}{}{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 0 L 7.87 7.87}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}
. The random variable
L^{\hbox to6.09pt{\vbox to6.09pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.2pt\lower-0.2pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {}{{}}{}
{}{}
{}{}
{}{}
{}{}{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 0 L 7.87 0 L 7.87 7.87 L 0 7.87 L 0 0}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
{}{{}}{}
{}{}{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 0 L 7.87 7.87}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}
has the same law of
\lambda_{1}
, where
\lambda
is distributed according to measure
\mathbb{HW}^{(q=0)}_{a;\gamma}
and
a
is a single Plancherel specialization; here the parameter
\gamma
counts the number of fixed points of the involution. Notice that
\mathbb{HW}^{(q=0)}_{a;\gamma}
is a Schur measure since
q=0
. From
proposition

2.7
we see that it is equivalent to take
\lambda
with law
\mathbb{HW}^{(q=0)}_{(a,\gamma);0}
, i.e. the Pfaffian Schur measure
\mathbb{FBS}^{(q=0)}_{(a,\gamma)}
. This shows that the Baik-Rains crossover arises also as limiting law of
\lambda_{1}
, when
\lambda\sim\mathbb{FBS}^{(q=0)}_{(a,\gamma)}
and
a
is a single Plancherel specialization.
In
theorem

6.7
we characterized the asymptotics of the first row in
\lambda\sim\mathbb{FBS}^{(q=0)}_{(a,\gamma)}
, when
a
is a list of single variable specializations, but it is straightforward, following the proof to show that the same limit holds also when
a
is a Plancherel specialization. This completes the proof.
∎

6.4.
Baik-Rains phase transition for half space models: positive temperature case

Guided by the analysis of Pfaffian Schur measure described in the previous subsection, we can now establish asymptotic limits for the partition function of the half space log Gamma polymer or for the rightmost particle in a
q
-PushTASEP with particle creation. Similarities between matrix kernels in the Fredholm pfaffian expansions of both models allow us to present rather concise proofs, which mirror techniques explained in detail in
theorem

6.7
.

Theorem 6.9
.

Let
Z^{\mathrm{hs}}(N,N)
be the partition function of the half space log Gamma polymer with parameters
A_{1}=A_{2}=\cdots=A\in(0,+\infty)
and boundary strength
\varUpsilon>-A
. Then, the following limits hold:

•

if
\varUpsilon\geq 0
, rescaling
\varUpsilon=\frac{\xi}{\sigma^{\mathrm{hs}}N^{1/3}}
we have

(6.78)

\lim_{N\to\infty}\mathbb{P}\left[\frac{\log Z^{\mathrm{hs}}(N,N)+f^{\mathrm{hs}}N}{\sigma^{\mathrm{hs}}N^{1/3}}\leq s\right]=F_{\mathrm{cross}}(s;\xi),

where
f^{\mathrm{hs}}=2\psi(A)
and
\sigma^{\mathrm{hs}}=\sqrt[3]{\psi^{(2)}(A)}
;

•

if
-A<\varUpsilon<0
,

(6.79)

\lim_{N\to\infty}\mathbb{P}\left[\frac{\log Z^{\mathrm{hs}}(N,N)-f^{\mathrm{hs}}_{\varUpsilon}N}{\sigma^{\mathrm{hs}}_{\varUpsilon}N^{1/2}}\leq s\right]=\int_{-\infty}^{s}\frac{e^{-u^{2}/2}}{\sqrt{2\pi}}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}u,

where
f^{\mathrm{hs}}_{\varUpsilon}=-\psi(A+\varUpsilon)-\psi(A-\varUpsilon)
and
\sigma^{\mathrm{hs}}_{\varUpsilon}=\sqrt{\psi^{(1)}(A+\varUpsilon)-\psi^{(1)}(A-\varUpsilon)}
.

Proof.

The proof is based on asymptotic analysis of Fredholm pfaffian formulas (
5.36
) and (
5.58
). As observed in (
6.16
), following a scaling of the parameter
\varsigma
, the Laplace transform
\mathbb{E}\left[e^{-e^{-\varsigma+\log Z^{\mathrm{hs}}(N,N)}}\right]
identifies the probability distribution of asymptotic fluctuations of
\log Z^{\mathrm{hs}}(N,N)
. We distinguish three cases depending on the value of
\varUpsilon
.

\varUpsilon>0
: convergence to crossover distribution.
The starting point is the exact formula (
5.36
) and we consider the scaling

(6.80)

-\varsigma=f_{\mathrm{hs}}N-\sigma_{\mathrm{hs}}N^{1/3}s,

which induces in the kernel
\mathbf{L}(x,y)
the change of variables

(6.81)

X=\varsigma+\sigma_{\mathrm{hs}}N^{1/3}u,\qquad Y=\varsigma+\sigma_{\mathrm{hs}}N^{1/3}v,

with
u,v>s
. Defining the function

(6.82)

h(Z)=\log\Gamma(A+Z)-\log\Gamma(A-Z)-f_{\mathrm{hs}}Z

we rewrite
\mathbf{k}
of (
5.38
) as

(6.83)

\begin{split}\mathbf{k}(X(u),Y(v))=\int_{\mathrm{i}\mathbb{R}-d}\frac{\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}Z}{2\pi\mathrm{i}}\int_{\mathrm{i}\mathbb{R}-d}\frac{\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}W}{2\pi\mathrm{i}}&e^{N(h(Z)+h(W))+\sigma_{\mathrm{hs}}N^{1/3}(uZ+vW)}\\
&\times\frac{\Gamma(\varUpsilon+Z)}{\Gamma(\varUpsilon-Z)}\frac{\Gamma(\varUpsilon+W)}{\Gamma(\varUpsilon-W)}\mathsf{k}^{\mathrm{hs}}(Z,W).\end{split}

As already observed in the proof of
theorem

6.6
,
h(Z)
possesses a doubly critical point at
Z=0
and for
d
sufficiently close to 0, the function
\Re\{h(Z)\}
, restricted on
Z\in\mathrm{i}\mathbb{R}-d
attains a global maximum at
Z=-d
. We deform both
Z
and
W
integration contours to the curve

(6.84)

\mathsf{C}^{2\pi/3}\left(-\frac{\delta}{\sigma N^{1/3}};d\right)\cup\mathsf{D}(-d,\sqrt{3}(d-\frac{\delta}{\sigma N^{1/3}})),

where we recall the notation of (
6.22
) and
\delta\in(0,\xi)
. We see that, by the properties of function
h
, the contour (
6.84
) is steep descent and for large
N
, the dominant part of both
Z
and
W
integrals comes from the integration over the curve
\mathsf{C}^{2\pi/3}\left(-\frac{\delta}{\sigma N^{1/3}};d\right)
. After a change of variables

(6.85)

Z=-\frac{\alpha}{\sigma_{\mathrm{hs}}N^{1/3}},\qquad W=-\frac{\beta}{\sigma_{\mathrm{hs}}N^{1/3}}

from expression (
6.83
), we see that

(6.86)

\mathbf{k}(X(u),Y(v))=\mathpzc{k}^{(\xi)}(u,v)(1+O(N^{-1/3}))

and similarly for other elements of the matrix kernel,
\mathbf{L}

\displaystyle\partial_{X}\mathbf{k}(X(u),Y(v))=\frac{1}{\sigma_{\mathrm{hs}}N^{1/3}}\partial_{u}\mathpzc{k}^{(\xi)}(u,v)(1+O(N^{-1/3})),

\displaystyle\partial_{Y}\mathbf{k}(X(u),Y(v))=\frac{1}{\sigma_{\mathrm{hs}}N^{1/3}}\partial_{v}\mathpzc{k}^{(\xi)}(u,v)(1+O(N^{-1/3})),

\displaystyle\partial_{X}\partial_{Y}\mathbf{k}(X(u),Y(v))=\frac{1}{\sigma_{\mathrm{hs}}^{2}N^{2/3}}\partial_{u}\partial_{v}\mathpzc{k}^{(\xi)}(u,v)(1+O(N^{-1/3})).

Exponential bounds such as

(6.87)

|\mathbf{k}(X(u),Y(v))|,|\sigma_{\mathrm{hs}}N^{1/3}\partial_{Y}\mathbf{k}(X(u),Y(v))|,\\
|\sigma_{\mathrm{hs}}N^{1/3}\partial_{X}\mathbf{k}(X(u),Y(v))|,|\sigma_{\mathrm{hs}}^{2}N^{2/3}\partial_{X}\partial_{Y}\mathbf{k}(X(u),Y(v))|<\mathrm{const.}e^{-\delta(u+v)}

are also straightforward from steep descent analysis and this proves, through bounded convergence that

(6.88)

\lim_{N\to\infty}\mathrm{Pf}\left[J-\mathbf{L}\right]_{\mathbb{L}^{2}(\varsigma,+\infty)}=\mathrm{Pf}\left[J-\mathcal{K}^{(\xi)}_{\mathrm{cross}}\right]_{\mathbb{L}^{2}(s,+\infty)}

and hence (
6.78
) for
\varUpsilon>0
.

\varUpsilon=0
: convergence to GOE Tracy-Widom distribution.
In
section

5.4
right before
theorem

5.12
, we have already observed that, when
\varUpsilon
approaches
0
, for the representation (
5.38
) of the function
\mathbf{k}
, it becomes no longer possible to choose integration contours
\mathrm{i}\mathbb{R}-d
such that
0<d<\varUpsilon
. Denoting by
\mathbf{k}_{\mathrm{ext}}(X,Y)
the analytic extension of
\mathbf{k}
in the parameter
\varUpsilon
we have

(6.89)

\begin{split}\mathbf{k}_{\mathrm{ext}}(X,Y)\big|_{\varUpsilon=0}&=\left(\mathbf{k}(X,Y)-\mathbf{A}_{0}(X)\mathbf{B}(Y)+\mathbf{A}_{0}(Y)\mathbf{B}(X)\right)\big|_{\varUpsilon=0}\\
&=\left(\mathbf{k}(X,Y)-\mathbf{B}(Y)+\mathbf{B}(X)\right)\big|_{\varUpsilon=0}\end{split}

where functions
\mathbf{A}_{0},\mathbf{B}
were defined in (
5.52
) (
5.53
) and we have used the fact that
\mathbf{A}_{0}(X)\big|_{\varUpsilon=0}=1
. We also define
\mathbf{L}_{\mathrm{ext}}
to be the analytic extension of
\mathbf{L}
in the parameter
\varUpsilon
and we have

(6.90)

\mathbf{L}_{\mathrm{ext}}(X,Y)\big|_{\varUpsilon=0}=\mathbf{L}(X,Y)\big|_{\varUpsilon=0}-\left(\begin{matrix}1&-\partial_{Y}\\
-\partial_{X}&\partial_{X}\partial_{Y}\end{matrix}\right)(\mathbf{B}(Y)-\mathbf{B}(X))\big|_{\varUpsilon=0}.

Considering the scaling (
6.80
), (
6.81
) we can now compute through the steep descent method, the asymptotic form of the kernel
\mathbf{L}_{\mathrm{ext}}
, as done for the case
\varUpsilon>0
. We obtain

(6.91)

\displaystyle\mathbf{k}(X(u),Y(v))\big|_{\varUpsilon=0}=\mathpzc{k}^{(\infty)}(u,v)(1+O(N^{-1/3})),

(6.92)

\displaystyle\mathbf{B}(X(u))=\mathpzc{B}(u)(1+O(N^{-1/3})),

where the terms
O(N^{-1/3})
can be shown to be absolutely bounded in
u,v>s
. This shows that

(6.93)

\left(\begin{matrix}1&0\\
0&\sigma_{\mathrm{hs}}N^{1/3}\end{matrix}\right)\cdot\mathbf{L}_{\mathrm{ext}}(X(u),Y(v))\big|_{\varUpsilon=0}\cdot\left(\begin{matrix}1&0\\
0&\sigma_{\mathrm{hs}}N^{1/3}\end{matrix}\right)=\mathcal{K}_{\mathrm{GOE}}(u,v)(1+O(N^{-1/3})).

By arguments developed in the proof of the
\gamma=1
case of
theorem

6.7
, the Fredholm pfaffian of the matrix kernel
\mathbf{L}_{\mathrm{ext}}
always defines an absolutely convergent expansion, even though it does not define a bounded operator on
\mathbb{L}^{2}(\varsigma,+\infty)\times\mathbb{L}^{2}(\varsigma,+\infty)
. This along with the convergence (
6.93
) imples that

(6.94)

\lim_{N\to\infty}\mathrm{Pf}\left[J-\mathbf{L}_{\mathrm{ext}}\big|_{\varUpsilon=0}\right]_{\mathbb{L}^{2}(\varsigma,+\infty)}=\mathrm{Pf}\left[J-\mathcal{K}_{\mathrm{GOE}}\right]_{\mathbb{L}^{2}(s,+\infty)},

proving (
6.78
) for
\varUpsilon=0
.

\varUpsilon<0
: convergence to gaussian distribution.
We will follow the same ideas as in the case
\gamma>1
of
theorem

6.7
. Nevertheless, computations here become technically more involved due to the nontrivial pole structure of the kernel
\mathbf{L}
, compared to that of
\bar{L}
. The main issue we will overcome below is to control that, during the asymptotic limit, our pfaffian expression remains absolutely convergent. We consider the equality (
5.58
) and the scaling

(6.95)

-\varsigma=f_{\varUpsilon}^{\mathrm{hs}}N-\varsigma^{\mathrm{hs}}_{\varUpsilon}N^{1/2}s.

With this choice it is clear that, in the left hand side of (
5.58
),

(6.96)

\lim_{N\to\infty}\mathbb{E}\left[e^{-e^{-\varsigma+\log Z^{\mathrm{hs}}(N,N)}}\right]=\lim_{N\to\infty}\mathbb{P}\left[\frac{\log Z^{\mathrm{hs}}(N,N)-f^{\mathrm{hs}}_{\varUpsilon}N}{\sigma^{\mathrm{hs}}_{\varUpsilon}N^{1/2}}\leq s\right]

and therefore it remains to consider the scaling limit of the right hand side of (
5.58
),

(6.97)

\mathrm{Pf}\left[J-\widehat{\mathbf{L}}\right]_{\mathbb{L}^{2}(\varsigma,+\infty)}\times\bigg(1-\langle(\mathbf{B},-\mathbf{B}^{\prime})|\left(1-J^{T}\widehat{\mathbf{L}}\right)^{-1}|(\mathbf{A}^{\prime},\mathbf{A})\rangle\bigg).

The scaling (
6.95
) of parameter
\varsigma
induces on the functions
\widehat{\mathbf{L}},\mathbf{B},\mathbf{A}
the change of variable

(6.98)

X=\varsigma+\sigma^{\mathrm{hs}}_{\varUpsilon}N^{1/2}u,\qquad Y=\varsigma+\sigma^{\mathrm{hs}}_{\varUpsilon}N^{1/2}v.

Defining the function

(6.99)

h_{\varUpsilon}(Z)=\log\Gamma(A+Z)-\log\Gamma(A-Z)-f_{\varUpsilon}^{\mathrm{hs}}Z,

we rewrite
\mathbf{A}_{j}
(which compose
\mathbf{A}
) and
\mathbf{B}
appearing in terms in the right hand side of (
6.97
) as

(6.100)

\mathbf{A}_{j}(X(u))=\frac{(-1)^{j}}{j!}e^{Nh_{\varUpsilon}(-\varUpsilon-j)-(j+\varUpsilon)\sigma_{\varUpsilon}^{\mathrm{hs}}N^{1/2}u}\frac{\Gamma(2\varUpsilon+2j)}{\Gamma(2\varUpsilon+j)},

(6.101)

\mathbf{B}(Y(v))=\int_{\mathrm{i}\mathbb{R}-d}\frac{\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}W}{2\pi\mathrm{i}}e^{Nh_{\varUpsilon}(W)+\sigma_{\varUpsilon}^{\mathrm{hs}}N^{1/2}vW}\Gamma(-2W)\frac{\Gamma(1-\varUpsilon+W)}{\Gamma(1-\varUpsilon-W)}.

For the matrix kernel
\widehat{\mathbf{L}}
we are going to use the expression

(6.102)

\left(\begin{matrix}1&-\partial_{Y}\\
-\partial_{X}&\partial_{X}\partial_{Y}\end{matrix}\right)\widehat{\mathbf{L}}_{1,1}(X,Y),

where
\widehat{\mathbf{L}}_{1,1}(X,Y)
is given by (
5.72
). We write

(6.103)

\begin{split}S_{j}(X(u),Y(v))=-\int_{\mathrm{i}\mathbb{R}+R}\frac{\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}W}{2\pi\mathrm{i}}&e^{N\left(h_{\varUpsilon}(W)+h_{\varUpsilon}(-W-j)\right)+\sigma_{\varUpsilon}^{\mathrm{hs}}N^{1/2}(vW-u(W+j))}\\
&\times\frac{\Gamma(\varUpsilon+W)}{\Gamma(\varUpsilon-W)}\frac{\Gamma(\varUpsilon-W-j)}{\Gamma(\varUpsilon+W+j)}\frac{\Gamma(2W+2j)}{\Gamma(2W+1)},\end{split}

where
R\in(\varUpsilon-1,-\varUpsilon-j+1)
and

(6.104)

\begin{split}\mathbf{k}_{\varUpsilon-\delta}(X(u),Y(v))=\int_{\mathrm{i}\mathbb{R}+\varUpsilon-\delta}\frac{\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}Z}{2\pi\mathrm{i}}\int_{\mathrm{i}\mathbb{R}+\varUpsilon-\delta}\frac{\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}W}{2\pi\mathrm{i}}&e^{N(h_{\varUpsilon}(Z)+h_{\varUpsilon}(W))+\sigma_{\varUpsilon}^{\mathrm{hs}}N^{1/2}(uZ+vW)}\\
&\times\frac{\Gamma(\varUpsilon+Z)}{\Gamma(\varUpsilon-Z)}\frac{\Gamma(\varUpsilon+W)}{\Gamma(\varUpsilon-W)}\mathsf{k}^{\mathrm{hs}}(Z,W).\end{split}

Simple computations show that
h_{\varUpsilon}
has the following properties,

\displaystyle h_{\varUpsilon}(Z)<0\quad\text{for }Z\in[\varUpsilon,0),\qquad h_{\varUpsilon}^{\prime}(Z)>0\quad\text{for }Z\in(\varUpsilon,-\varUpsilon),

\displaystyle h_{\varUpsilon}(-Z)=-h_{\varUpsilon}(Z),\qquad h_{\varUpsilon}^{\prime}(\varUpsilon)=0,\qquad h_{\varUpsilon}^{\prime\prime}(\varUpsilon)=(\sigma_{\varUpsilon}^{\mathrm{hs}})^{2}>0.

We evaluate the function
\mathbf{k}_{\varUpsilon-\delta}
through saddle point method setting
\delta=0
, noticing that the integration contour passes through the saddle point
W=\varUpsilon
of
h_{\varUpsilon}
. After a change of variables,

(6.105)

Z=\varUpsilon+\mathrm{i}\frac{\alpha}{\sigma_{\varUpsilon}^{\mathrm{hs}}N^{1/2}},\qquad W=\varUpsilon+\mathrm{i}\frac{\beta}{\sigma_{\varUpsilon}^{\mathrm{hs}}N^{1/2}},

we have

(6.106)

\begin{split}\mathbf{k}_{\varUpsilon}(X(u),Y(v))=&e^{2Nh_{\varUpsilon}(\varUpsilon)+\sigma_{\varUpsilon}^{\mathrm{hs}}N^{1/2}\varUpsilon(u+v)}\frac{\Gamma(2\varUpsilon)^{2}\Gamma(-2\varUpsilon)^{2}}{\sin(2\pi\varUpsilon)}\left(\frac{1}{\sigma_{\varUpsilon}^{\mathrm{hs}}N^{1/2}}\right)^{5}\\
&\times\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\frac{\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\alpha}{2\pi}\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\frac{\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\beta}{2\pi}e^{-(\alpha^{2}+\beta^{2})/2+\mathrm{i}(u\alpha+v\beta)}\alpha\beta(\alpha-\beta)\\
&=O\left(N^{-5/2}e^{2Nh_{\varUpsilon}(\varUpsilon)+\sigma_{\varUpsilon}^{\mathrm{hs}}N^{1/2}\varUpsilon(u+v)-c(u^{2}+v^{2})}\right),\end{split}

where
c\in(0,1/2)
. The previous expression is apparently divergent when
\varUpsilon=-1,-2,\dots
, but one can easily see that this is not the case as such singularities can be avoided shifting integration contours by
O(N^{-1/2})
to the left of
\varUpsilon
at the cost of adding a factor
N^{3/2}
. A similar estimate can be obtained for the functions
S_{j}
, although in that case we opt for a less sharp bound keeping the integration contour to be
\mathrm{i}\mathbb{R}+R
, which is not necessarily steep descent for
h_{\varUpsilon}(W)+h_{\varUpsilon}(-W-j)
. We have

(6.107)

S_{j}(X(u),Y(v))=O\left(N^{-1}e^{N(h_{\varUpsilon}(R)+h_{\varUpsilon}(-R-j))+\sigma_{\varUpsilon}^{\mathrm{hs}}N^{1/2}(vR-u(R+j))}\right).

Notice that whenever
R\in(\varUpsilon-\tilde{\delta},\tilde{\delta})
, for
\tilde{\delta}>0
small enough and
j<-R
, then
h_{\varUpsilon}(R)+h_{\varUpsilon}(-R-j)<0
and hence the right hand side of (
6.107
) is exponentially decaying in
N
. Combining (
6.106
) and (
6.107
) we obtain, recalling (
5.72
),

(6.108)

\widehat{\mathbf{L}}(X(u),Y(v))=O\left(N^{-1}e^{-CN}e^{\sigma_{\varUpsilon}^{\mathrm{hs}}N^{1/2}[(-R-1)u+Rv]}\right),

for some
C>0
fixed. Choosing a suitable
R\in(-1,0)
, such bound clearly implies the estimate

(6.109)

\mathrm{Pf}[J-\widehat{\mathbf{L}}]_{\mathbb{L}(\varsigma,+\infty)}=1+O(e^{-C^{\prime}N}),

where
0<C^{\prime}<C
, settling the convergence of the first factor in (
6.97
).

We now come to estimate the scalar product appearing in the second factor of (
6.97
). In order to estimate the function
\mathbf{B}
we move the integration contour to the line
\mathrm{i}\mathbb{R}+\varUpsilon
and by saddle point method we obtain

(6.110)

\begin{split}\mathbf{B}(Y(v))&=e^{Nh_{\varUpsilon}(\varUpsilon)+\sigma_{\varUpsilon}^{\mathrm{hs}}N^{1/2}v\varUpsilon}\frac{\Gamma(-2\varUpsilon)}{\Gamma(1-2\varUpsilon)}\int_{-\infty}^{+\infty}\frac{\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\beta}{2\pi}e^{-\beta^{2}/2+\mathrm{i}v\beta}(1+O(N^{-1/2}))\\
&=e^{Nh_{\varUpsilon}(\varUpsilon)+\sigma_{\varUpsilon}^{\mathrm{hs}}N^{1/2}v\varUpsilon}\frac{1}{-2\varUpsilon}\frac{1}{\sqrt{2\pi}}e^{-v^{2}/2}(1+O(N^{-1/2})).\end{split}

On the other hand for the function
\mathbf{A}
, we observe that

(6.111)

\mathbf{A}(X(u))=\mathbf{A}_{0}(X(u))(1+o(1))=e^{-Nh_{\varUpsilon}(\varUpsilon)-\varUpsilon\sigma_{\varUpsilon}^{\mathrm{hs}}N^{1/2}u}(1+o(1)),

where the term
o(1)
is bounded in
u
and uniformly exponentially decaying in
N
. By virtue of estimate (
6.108
) the operator
J^{T}\widehat{\mathbf{L}}
has norm arbitrarily small, for
N
large and hence the resolvent
(1-J^{T}\widehat{\mathbf{L}})^{-1}
can be expanded in Neumann series and we have

(6.112)

\langle(\mathbf{B},-\mathbf{B}^{\prime})|\left(1-J^{T}\widehat{\mathbf{L}}\right)^{-1}|(\mathbf{A}^{\prime},\mathbf{A})\rangle=\sum_{\ell\geq 0}\langle(\mathbf{B},-\mathbf{B}^{\prime})|\left(J^{T}\widehat{\mathbf{L}}\right)^{\ell}|(\mathbf{A}^{\prime},\mathbf{A})\rangle.

In the proof of
theorem

5.12
, using estimates (
5.66
), (
5.75
) and producing the iterated bound (
5.77
), we established the exponential decay of terms
\langle(\mathbf{B},-\mathbf{B}^{\prime})|\left(J^{T}\widehat{\mathbf{L}}\right)^{\ell}|(\mathbf{A}^{\prime},\mathbf{A})\rangle
. Here we can refine such arguments using the improved bounds (
6.108
), (
6.110
), (
6.111
) and we obtain, for any
\ell\geq 1
,

(6.113)

\left|\langle(\mathbf{B},-\mathbf{B}^{\prime})|\left(J^{T}\widehat{\mathbf{L}}\right)^{\ell}|(\mathbf{A}^{\prime},\mathbf{A})\rangle\right|=O\left(e^{-C^{\prime}N\ell}\right).

For
\ell=0
we have

(6.114)

\begin{split}\langle(\mathbf{B},-\mathbf{B}^{\prime})|(\mathbf{A}^{\prime},\mathbf{A})\rangle=\int_{s}^{\infty}\frac{\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}u}{\sqrt{2\pi}}e^{-u^{2}/2}(1+O(N^{-1/2})),\end{split}

which proves that

\lim_{N\to\infty}\mathrm{Pf}\left[J-\widehat{\mathbf{L}}\right]_{\mathbb{L}^{2}(\varsigma,+\infty)}\times\bigg(1-\langle(\mathbf{B},-\mathbf{B}^{\prime})|\left(1-J^{T}\widehat{\mathbf{L}}\right)^{-1}|(\mathbf{A}^{\prime},\mathbf{A})\rangle\bigg)=\int^{s}_{-\infty}\frac{\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}u}{\sqrt{2\pi}}e^{-u^{2}/2}.

This shows (
6.79
) and completes the proof of the theorem.

∎

Remark 6.10
.

The one presented above is the first instance of a proof of the Baik-Rains phase transition for a “positive temperature” KPZ model. Recently,
[
BW21
]
proved a similar “depinning transition” for the point-to-half-line partition function of the half space Log Gamma polymer using a matching in distribution with the point-to-point polymer partition function in full space.

We also present asymptotic results for the
q
-PushTASEP in half space. The proof follows the same ideas as those presented in
theorem

6.9
and therefore we are only going to sketch it.

For the next theorem we recall the
q
-digamma and
q
-polygamma functions

(6.115)

\psi_{q}(z)=-\log(1-q)+\log q\sum_{\ell\geq 0}\frac{q^{z+\ell}}{1-q^{z+\ell}}\qquad\text{and}\qquad\psi^{(n)}_{q}(z)=\frac{\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}^{n}}{\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}z^{n}}\psi_{q}(z)

which are well defined for
q\in(0,1)
and
z\in\mathbb{C}\setminus\mathbb{Z}_{\leq 0}
. Instead of the series representation given above, the
q
-digamma function can be defined in terms of the
q
-Gamma function
\psi_{q}(z)=\frac{\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}}{\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}z}\log\Gamma_{q}(z)
, where
\Gamma_{q}(z)
is defined in (
5.23
).

Theorem 6.11
.

Let
\mathsf{X}^{\mathrm{hs}}(T)
be the geometric
q
-PushTASEP with particle creation and assume that parameters are
a_{1}=a_{2}=\cdots=a\in(0,1)
and
\gamma\in(0,1/a)
. Then, the following limits hold:

•

if
\gamma\in(0,1]
, rescaling
\gamma=1-\frac{\xi}{\sigma^{\mathrm{hs}}N^{1/3}}
we have

(6.116)

\lim_{N\to\infty}\mathbb{P}\left[\frac{\mathsf{x}^{\mathrm{hs}}_{N}(N)-(p^{\mathrm{hs}}+1)N}{\sigma^{\mathrm{hs}}N^{1/3}}\leq s^{\prime}\right]=F_{\mathrm{cross}}(s^{\prime};\xi),

where
p^{\mathrm{hs}}=\frac{2}{\log q}\left[\log(1-q)+\psi_{q}\left(\log_{q}a\right)\right]
and
\sigma^{\mathrm{hs}}=\frac{1}{\log q}\sqrt[3]{\psi_{q}^{(2)}\left(\log_{q}a\right)}
;

•

if
\gamma\in(1,1/a)
,

(6.117)

\lim_{N\to\infty}\mathbb{P}\left[\frac{\mathsf{x}^{\mathrm{hs}}_{N}(N)-(p^{\mathrm{hs}}_{\gamma}+1)N}{\sigma^{\mathrm{hs}}_{\varUpsilon}N^{1/2}}\leq s^{\prime}\right]=\int_{-\infty}^{s^{\prime}}\frac{e^{-u^{2}/2}}{\sqrt{2\pi}}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}u,

where

p^{\mathrm{hs}}_{\gamma}=\frac{1}{\log q}\left[2\log(1-q)+\psi_{q}\left(\log_{q}(a/\gamma)\right)+\psi_{q}\left(\log_{q}(a\gamma)\right)\right]

and

\sigma^{\mathrm{hs}}_{\gamma}=\frac{\gamma}{\log q}\sqrt{\psi_{q}^{(1)}\left(\log_{q}(a\gamma)\right)-\psi_{q}^{(1)}\left(\log_{q}(a/\gamma)\right)}.

Sketch of proof.

The starting point is the Fredholm pfaffian formula (
5.32
). We consider different scalings of the parameter
s
depending on the value of
\gamma
.

\gamma<1
: convergence to crossover distribution.
Under the scaling

(6.118)

s=(p^{\mathrm{hs}}-1)N+\sigma^{\mathrm{hs}}N^{1/3}s^{\prime},\qquad x=s+\sigma^{\mathrm{hs}}N^{1/3}u,\qquad y=s+\sigma^{\mathrm{hs}}N^{1/3}v,

the function
k(x,y)
, which defines the kernel
L_{\mathrm{push}}
can be written as

(6.119)

\begin{split}k(x(u),y(v))=\oint_{|z|=r}&\frac{\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}z}{2\pi\mathrm{i}z^{3/2}}\oint_{|w|=r^{\prime}}\frac{\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}w}{2\pi\mathrm{i}w^{5/2}}\frac{(\gamma/z,\gamma/w;q)_{\infty}}{(\gamma z,\gamma w;q)_{\infty}}\kappa^{\mathrm{hs}}(z,w)\\
&\times e^{Nh_{q}(z)-\sigma^{\mathrm{hs}}N^{1/3}(u+s^{\prime})\log z}e^{Nh_{q}(w)-\sigma^{\mathrm{hs}}N^{1/3}(u+s^{\prime})\log w}.\end{split}

Here we assume that
u,v>s^{\prime}
and the function
h_{q}
in the exponents is defined as

(6.120)

h_{q}(z)=\log(a/z;q)_{\infty}-\log(az;q)_{\infty}-p^{\mathrm{hs}}\log z.

A calculation shows that
h_{q}(1)=h_{q}^{\prime}(1)=h_{q}^{\prime\prime}(1)=0
and
h_{q}^{\prime\prime\prime}(1)=2(\sigma^{\mathrm{hs}})^{3}>0
, so that, employing a saddle point analysis, around critical points
z=1,w=1
we can prove that

(6.121)

\lim_{N\to\infty}k(x(u),y(v))=\mathpzc{k}^{(\xi)}(u,v)\qquad\text{and}\qquad\lim_{N\to\infty}L_{\mathrm{push}}(x(u),y(v))=\mathcal{K}_{\mathrm{cross}}^{(\xi)}(u,v).

Considering the scaling of
s
adopted in the left hand side of (
5.32
), this shows (
6.116
) for
\gamma<1
.

\gamma=0
: convergence to GOE distribution.

For the rest of the proof we will express the residues of the integrand of (
6.119
) at poles
z,w=\gamma^{-1}q^{-j}
, for
j=0,1,2,\dots
, as

\mathrm{Res}_{z=\frac{1}{\gamma q^{j}}}\left\{\parbox{59.75095pt}{\centering$z$-integrand in \eqref{eq:k_q_rescaled}\@add@centering}\right\}=A^{(q)}_{j}(x)B^{(q)}(y),\qquad\mathrm{Res}_{w=\frac{1}{\gamma q^{j}}}\left\{\parbox{59.75095pt}{\centering$w$-integrand in \eqref{eq:k_q_rescaled}\@add@centering}\right\}=-A^{(q)}_{j}(y)B^{(q)}(x),

where

(6.122)

A^{(q)}_{j}(x)=(\gamma q^{j})^{x+1/2}(-1)^{j}\frac{q^{\binom{j+1}{2}}}{(q;q)_{j}}\frac{(\gamma^{2}q^{j};q)_{\infty}}{(\gamma^{2}q^{2j};q)_{\infty}}\left(\frac{(a\gamma q^{j};q)_{\infty}}{(a/\gamma q^{j};q)_{\infty}}\right)^{N},

(6.123)

B^{(q)}(y)=\frac{1}{2\pi\mathrm{i}}\oint_{|w|=r^{\prime}}\frac{\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}w}{w^{y+5/2}}F(w)\frac{(q,w\gamma q^{j},1/\gamma q^{j-1}w;q)_{\infty}}{(1/w^{2},q^{j}\gamma/w,z/\gamma q^{j-1};q)_{\infty}}\frac{\vartheta_{3}(\zeta^{2}w^{2}/\gamma^{2}q^{2j};q^{2})}{\vartheta_{3}(\zeta^{2};q^{2})}.

These should be seen as
q
-deformations of functions (
5.52
), (
5.53
). Then, we can proceed with the analytic continuation of formula (
5.32
) in the variable
\gamma
. In particular as
\gamma
approaches
1
from the left we see that it is no longer possible to select radii
r,r^{\prime}
of the integration contours of the function
k(x,y)
such that
1<r,r^{\prime}<1/\gamma
and as the singularities at
z,w=1/\gamma
cross the contours the analytic extension of the operator
L_{\mathrm{push}}
reads

(6.124)

L_{\mathrm{push,ext}}\big|_{\gamma=1}(x,y)=L_{\mathrm{push}}\big|_{\gamma=1}(x,y)-\left(\begin{matrix}1&-\nabla_{y}\\
-\nabla_{x}&\nabla_{x}\nabla_{y}\end{matrix}\right)\left[B^{(q)}(y)-B^{(q)}(x)\right]\bigg|_{\gamma=1},

since
A^{(q)}_{0}(x)\big|_{\gamma=1}=1
. Using ideas elaborated in the case
\varUpsilon=0
of
theorem

6.9
we can now prove that the Fredholm pfaffian of the operator
L_{\mathrm{push,ext}}\big|_{\gamma=1}
defines an absolutely convergent series and its limit for
N\to\infty
can be computed using saddle point method. The result is

(6.125)

\lim_{N\to\infty}\left(\begin{matrix}1&0\\
0&\sigma^{\mathrm{hs}}N^{1/3}\end{matrix}\right)\cdot L_{\mathrm{push,ext}}\big|_{\gamma=1}\cdot\left(\begin{matrix}1&0\\
0&\sigma^{\mathrm{hs}}N^{1/3}\end{matrix}\right)=\mathcal{K}_{\mathrm{GOE}}.

One can now show, as in
theorem

6.9
, that such limit implies the convergence of Fredholm pfaffians, proving the case
\gamma=1
of (
6.116
).

\gamma>1
: convergence to gaussian distribution
We will argue in the same way as in the case
\varUpsilon<0
of
theorem

6.9
. First we need to provide an analytic extension of the Fredholm pfaffian in the right hand side of (
5.32
) valid for
\gamma>1
. Reasoning as in
theorem

5.12
one can show that, assuming that
\gamma\in[q^{-m+\frac{2-\varepsilon}{2}},q^{-m+\frac{1-\varepsilon}{2}})
for some
m\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}
and
\varepsilon\in\{0,1\}
, then

(6.126)

\mathrm{Pf}\left[J-\widehat{L}_{\mathrm{push}}\right]_{\ell^{2}(\mathbb{Z}^{\prime}_{>s})}\left(1-\langle(B^{(q)},-\nabla B^{(q)})|\left(1-\widehat{L}_{\mathrm{push}}\right)^{-1}|(\nabla A^{(q)},A^{(q)})\right),

is the analytic extension of
\mathrm{Pf}\left[J-L_{\mathrm{push}}\right]_{\ell^{2}(\mathbb{Z}^{\prime}_{\geq s})}
, where

(6.127)

\widehat{L}_{\mathrm{push}}(x,y)=L_{\mathrm{push}}(x,y)-\left(\begin{matrix}1&-\nabla_{y}\\
-\nabla_{x}&\nabla_{x}\nabla_{y}\end{matrix}\right)\sum_{j=m}^{2m+\varepsilon-2}\left[A_{j}^{(q)}(x)B^{(q)}(y)-A_{j}^{(q)}(y)B^{(q)}(x)\right],

and
A^{(q)}=\sum_{j=0}^{2m+\varepsilon-2}A_{j}^{(q)}
. Here, we are assuming that
\langle(\,\cdot\,,\,\cdot\,)|(\,\cdot\,,\,\cdot\,)\rangle
is the natural scalar product on
\ell^{2}(\mathbb{Z}^{\prime}_{>s})\times\ell^{2}(\mathbb{Z}^{\prime}_{>s})
. Then, considering the scaling

(6.128)

s=(p^{\mathrm{hs}}_{\gamma}-1)N+\sigma^{\mathrm{hs}}_{\gamma}N^{1/2}s^{\prime}

one can show, following ideas developed in
theorem

6.9
, that

(6.129)

\lim_{N\to\infty}\mathrm{Pf}\left[J-\widehat{L}_{\mathrm{push}}\right]_{\ell^{2}(\mathbb{Z}^{\prime}_{\geq s})}=1

and

(6.130)

\lim_{N\to\infty}\left(1-\langle(B^{(q)},-\nabla B^{(q)})|\left(1-\widehat{L}_{\mathrm{push}}\right)^{-1}|(\nabla A^{(q)},A^{(q)})\right)=\int_{-\infty}^{s^{\prime}}\frac{e^{-u^{2}/2}}{\sqrt{2\pi}}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}u.

Taking the same scaling limit on the left hand side of (
5.32
) completes the proof of (
6.117
).
∎

7.
KPZ equation

Throughout this section we will treat the Cole-Hopf solution
\mathcal{H}
of the KPZ equation, defined as
\mathcal{H}=\log\mathscr{Z}
where
\mathscr{Z}
is the mild solution of the stochastic heat equation with multiplicative noise (
1.3
). For a more details on mild solutions we consider below the reader can consult
[
Qua13
,
Par19b
]
, where proofs of their existence uniqueness and positivity are provided.

7.1.
Convergence of Log Gamma polymers to stochastic heat equation with multiplicative noise

Certain classes of models of directed polymer in random media are known to converge, under a weak noise scaling to interesting limits. In particular, in
[
AKQ14
]
, authors introduced the so-called
intermediate disorder regime
, under which the noise of a space-time uncorrelated random environment tends to zero as the length of a polymer diverge. It was there proved that, under certain assumptions, the point-to-point partition function of the polymer model converges, after proper scaling, to the solution of the stochastic heat equation with multiplicative noise. We recall this result below, adapting their general result to the particular case of the Log Gamma polymer model.

Proposition 7.1

(
[
AKQ14
]
,Theorem 2.2)
.

Let
Z(T,N)
be the partition function of the Log Gamma polymer with parameters
A_{1}=A_{2}=\cdots=B_{1}=B_{2}=\cdots=A
. Consider the scaling
A=\sqrt{N}
, and

(7.1)

\log Z(\frac{tN}{2}+xN^{1/2},\frac{tN}{2})=\log\mathscr{Z}_{N}(x,t)+\frac{t}{8}+\frac{x}{2}-\frac{t}{2}N\log N+\frac{1}{2}(t-x\log N)N^{1/2}.

Then, as
N\to+\infty
, the process
\mathscr{Z}_{N}(x,t)
converges in distribution to
\mathscr{Z}(x,t)
, the mild solution of the stochastic heat equation with multiplicative noise (
1.3
) on
x\in\mathbb{R}
and initial conditions
\mathscr{Z}(x,0)=\delta_{0}(x)
.

The intermediate disorder regime was considered also for polymer models in restricted geometries. Below we recall a result of
[
Wu20
]
, which provides the half space variant of
proposition

7.1
.

Proposition 7.2

(
[
Wu20
]
, Theorem 5.1)
.

Let
Z^{\mathrm{hs}}(T,N)
be the partition function of the homogeneous Log Gamma polymer in half space with parameters
A_{1}=A_{2}=\cdots=A
and boundary strength
\varUpsilon
. Consider the scaling
A=\sqrt{N}
,
\varUpsilon=\omega+\frac{1}{2}\in\mathbb{R}
and

(7.2)

\log Z^{\mathrm{hs}}(\frac{tN}{2}+x\sqrt{N},\frac{tN}{2})=\log\mathscr{Z}_{N}^{\mathrm{hs}}(x,t)+\frac{t}{8}+\frac{x}{2}-\frac{t}{2}N\log N+\frac{1}{2}(t-x\log N)\sqrt{N}.

Then, as
N\to+\infty
the process
\mathscr{Z}_{N}(x,t)
converges in distribution to
\mathscr{Z}(x,t)
the mild solution of the stochastic heat equation with multiplicative noise (
1.10
) on
x\in\mathbb{R}_{+}
and Robin boundary conditions
\left(\partial_{x}-\omega\right)\mathscr{Z}(x,t)\big|_{x=0}=0
and initial conditions
\mathscr{Z}(x,0)=\delta_{0}(x)
.

7.2.
Proofs of
theorem

1.1
and of
theorem

1.3

Taking appropriate limits of Fredholm determinant and pfaffian formulas for partition functions of Log Gamma polymer models given in
theorem

5.4
,
theorem

5.10
and
theorem

5.12
we prove here exact formulas for the Hopf-Cole solution of the KPZ equation.

Proof of
theorem

1.1
.

We are going to consider the intermediate disorder scaling, prescribed by
proposition

7.1
, in both sides of relation (
5.12
). Setting

(7.3)

\varsigma=-\frac{t}{2}N\log N+\frac{t}{2}\sqrt{N}+\frac{t}{12}+s,

by
proposition

7.1
the left hand side of (
5.12
) becomes the left hand side of (
1.6
), that is

(7.4)

\lim_{N\to\infty}\mathbb{E}\left[e^{-e^{-\varsigma+\log Z(N,N)}}\right]=\mathbb{E}\left[e^{-e^{-s+\log\mathscr{Z}(0,t)+t/24}}\right].

We now show that the Fredholm determinant in the right hand side of the kernel
\mathbf{K}
converges to that of
\mathscr{K}_{\mathrm{mSHE}}
. First let us deform the integration contours in the integral expression (
5.13
) of
\mathbf{K}
to be
C_{-1}^{3\pi/4}
and
C_{1}^{\pi/4}
respectively for the
Z
and
W
variable. By the Stirling’s approximation

(7.5)

\log\Gamma(z)=\left(z-\frac{1}{2}\right)\log z-z+\frac{1}{2}\log(2\pi)+\frac{1}{12}\frac{1}{z}+O\left(\frac{1}{z^{3}}\right),

which holds as long as
\arg\{z\}
stays bounded away from
\pm\pi
, we can estimate the function
g_{A,A}
appearing in the integral representation (
5.13
) as

(7.6)

\log g_{A,A}(Z)+Z\varsigma=-\frac{t}{2}\frac{Z^{3}}{3}+Zs+O(ZN^{-1/2}).

Always using the Stirling’s approximation, this time applied to the polygamma function
\psi^{(1)}
, one can show that the function

(7.7)

\Re\left\{\log g_{A,A}(Z)+Z\varsigma\right\}

is concave along the half line
y\mapsto Z=-1+ye^{\mathrm{i}3\pi/4}
for
y>0
and
N
sufficiently large. For this compute

(7.8)

\begin{split}&\frac{\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}^{2}}{\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}y^{2}}\Re\left\{\log g_{A,A}(Z)+Z\varsigma\right\}\big|_{Z=Z=-1+ye^{\mathrm{i}3\pi/4}}\\
&=N\Im\left\{\psi^{(1)}(\sqrt{N}+Z)-\psi^{(1)}(\sqrt{N}-Z)\right\}\big|_{Z=Z=-1+ye^{\mathrm{i}3\pi/4}}\\
&\approx N\Im\left\{\frac{1}{\sqrt{N}+Z}+\frac{1}{2(\sqrt{N}+Z)^{2}}-\frac{1}{\sqrt{N}-Z}-\frac{1}{2(\sqrt{N}-Z)^{2}}\right\}\big|_{Z=Z=-1+ye^{\mathrm{i}3\pi/4}},\end{split}

then one can explictly evaluate the last line and confirm that for
N
large enough it is a strictly negative function for all
y>0
.
The explicit evaluation is not too much appealing and therefore we do not report it. This, along with estimate (
7.6
) implies that there exists an exponentially decaying function
\tilde{g}
, independent of
N
, such that

(7.9)

\left|g_{A,A}(Z)e^{Z\varsigma}\right|\bigg|_{Z=Z=-1+|y|e^{\mathrm{sign}(y)\mathrm{i}3\pi/4}}\leq\tilde{g}(y),

for all
y\in\mathbb{R}
. Similar arguments applied to terms
g_{A,A}(W)
show that

(7.10)

\lim_{N\to\infty}\mathbf{K}(X+\varsigma,Y+\varsigma)=\mathscr{K}_{\mathrm{mSHE}}(X+s,Y+s),

by bounded convergence. Moreover the inequality

(7.11)

|\mathbf{K}(X+\varsigma,Y+\varsigma)|\leq\mathrm{const.}e^{-(X+Y)},

in which the constant is independent of
N
, combined with the limit (
7.10
) implies that

(7.12)

\lim_{N\to\infty}\det[1-\mathbf{K}]_{\mathbb{L}^{2}(\varsigma,+\infty)}=\det\left[1-\mathscr{K}_{\mathrm{mSHE}}\right]_{\mathbb{L}^{2}(s,+\infty)},

again by bounded convergence. Notice that, so far we carried out the limit assuming that integration contours in the integral expression of
\mathscr{K}_{\mathrm{mSHE}}
were
C_{-1}^{3\pi/4}
and
C_{1}^{\pi/4}
respectively for the
Z
and
W
variable. Once the convergence result is established we can deform such contours to be
\mathrm{i}\mathbb{R}-d
and
\mathrm{i}\mathbb{R}+d
as prescribed by (
1.7
). This concludes the proof.
∎

Next we prove the Fredholm pfaffian formula (
1.14
).

Proof of
theorem

1.3
.

We follow the same arguments presented in the proof of
theorem

1.1
above. Consider the Fredholm pfaffian formula for the half space polymer partition function (
5.36
). Under the scaling prescribed by
proposition

7.2
we have

(7.13)

\lim_{N\to\infty}\mathbb{E}\left[e^{-e^{-\varsigma+\log Z^{\mathrm{hs}}(N,N)}}\right]=\mathbb{E}\left[e^{-e^{-s+\log\mathscr{Z}^{\mathrm{hs}}(N,N)+t/24}}\right],

where
\varsigma
is taken as in (
7.3
). Moving to the right hand side of (
5.36
), using the Stirling’s approximation we evaluate the function
g_{\varUpsilon;A}
appearing in the integrand of function
\mathbf{k}
as

(7.14)

g_{\varUpsilon;A}(Z)e^{Z\varsigma}=\frac{\Gamma(\frac{1}{2}+v+Z)}{\Gamma(\frac{1}{2}+v-Z)}e^{-\frac{t}{2}\frac{Z^{3}}{3}+Zs+O(ZN^{-1/2})}.

Uniform bounds in
N
on the absolute value of
g_{\varUpsilon;A}(Z)e^{Z\varsigma}
, for
Z\in C_{d}^{\pi/4}
can be also derived as described around (
7.7
). This shows that

(7.15)

\lim_{N\to\infty}\mathbf{k}(X+\varsigma,Y+\varsigma)=\mathscr{K}^{\mathrm{hs}}(X+s,Y+s)

and hence

(7.16)

\lim_{N\to\infty}\mathbf{L}(X+\varsigma,Y+\varsigma)=\mathscr{L}_{\mathrm{mSHE}}(X+s,Y+s).

Exponential bounds
\mathbf{k}(X+\varsigma,Y+\varsigma)=O\left(e^{-d(X+Y)}\right)
now allow to prove the convergence of the Fredholm pfaffian

(7.17)

\lim_{N\to\infty}\mathrm{Pf}[J-\mathbf{L}]_{\mathbb{L}^{2}(\varsigma,+\infty)}=\mathrm{Pf}[J-\mathscr{L}_{\mathrm{mSHE}}]_{\mathbb{L}^{2}(s,+\infty)}.

∎

Next we provide an explicit expression for the probability distribution of the solution of the half space stochastic heat equation
\mathscr{Z}^{\mathrm{hs}}(0,t)
for the boundary parameter
\omega\leq-1/2
. To derive such formula we are simply going to take the scaling limit of results of (
5.12
). Alternatively we could produce an analytic continuation, in the parameter
\omega
, of relation (
1.14
). The two procedure, as one can observe from the statement of
theorem

7.3
, yield the same result. We define the functions

(7.18)

\mathdutchcal{A}_{j}(X)=\frac{(-1)^{j}}{j!}e^{\frac{t}{2}\frac{(1/2+\omega+j)^{3}}{3}-(1/2+\omega+j)X}\frac{\Gamma(1+2\omega+2j)}{\Gamma(1+2\omega+j)},

(7.19)

\mathdutchcal{B}(Y)=\int_{\mathrm{i}\mathbb{R}+d}\frac{\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}W}{2\pi\mathrm{i}}e^{\frac{t}{2}\frac{W^{3}}{3}-YW}\Gamma(2W)\frac{\Gamma(\frac{1}{2}-\omega-W)}{\Gamma(\frac{1}{2}-\omega+W)}.

Theorem 7.3
.

Consider
\mathscr{Z}^{\mathrm{hs}}(x,t)
the solution of the stochastic heat equation (
1.10
) in half space with initial conditions
\mathscr{Z}^{\mathrm{hs}}(x,0)=\delta_{x}
and boundary parameter
\omega\leq-1/2
. Let
m\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}
and
\varepsilon\in\{0,1\}
be such that
\frac{1}{2}+\omega\in(-m+\frac{1-\varepsilon}{2},-m+\frac{2-\varepsilon}{2}]
and take a number
d
satisfying

(7.20)

0<d<\min\left\{\frac{1}{2},\frac{1}{2}+\omega+m\right\}.

Then, there exists
s^{*}
such that, for all
s>s^{*}
we have

(7.21)

\begin{split}\mathbb{E}\left[e^{-e^{-s+\log\mathscr{Z}^{\mathrm{hs}}(0,t)+t/24}}\right]=&\mathrm{Pf}\left[J-\widehat{\mathscr{L}}_{\mathrm{mSHE}}\right]_{\mathbb{L}^{2}(s,+\infty)}\\
&\qquad\times\bigg(1-\langle(\mathdutchcal{B},-\mathdutchcal{B}^{\prime})|\left(1-J^{T}\widehat{\mathscr{L}}_{\mathrm{mSHE}}\right)^{-1}|(\mathdutchcal{A}^{\prime},\mathdutchcal{A})\rangle\bigg),\end{split}

where

(7.22)

\widehat{\mathscr{L}}_{\mathrm{mSHE}}(X,Y)=\mathscr{L}_{\mathrm{mSHE}}(X,Y)-\left(\begin{matrix}1&-\partial_{Y}\\
-\partial_{X}&\partial_{X}\partial_{Y}\end{matrix}\right)\sum_{j=m}^{2m+\varepsilon-2}\left[\mathdutchcal{A}_{j}(X)\mathdutchcal{B}(Y)-\mathdutchcal{A}_{j}(Y)\mathdutchcal{B}(X)\right],

(7.23)

\mathdutchcal{A}(X)=\sum_{j=0}^{2m+\varepsilon-2}\mathdutchcal{A}_{j}(X).

Proof.

As in the proof of
theorem

1.3
we consider the scaling indicated in
proposition

7.2
and compute the limit of relation (
5.58
). Setting the parameter
\varsigma
as in (
7.3
), relation (
7.4
), stating the convergence of the Laplace transform of
Z^{\mathrm{hs}}(N,N)
to that of
\mathscr{Z}^{\mathrm{hs}}(0,t)
, still holds, as this is independent of the value of the boundary parameter
v
. To evaluate the limit of the Fredholm pfaffian in the right hand side of (
5.58
), we recall that such expression is well defined only when
\|\widehat{\mathbf{L}}\|<1
, condition that in
theorem

5.12
can be achieved taking
N
sufficiently large, while leaving
\varsigma
fixed. Given the scaling of the parameter
\varsigma
we have adopted, we can no longer control the norm of
\widehat{\mathbf{L}}
in such way and for this reason we are now forced to take
s
to be sufficiently large, but still fixed. As already explained in the proof of
theorem

1.1
and
theorem

1.3
above in the subsection the function

(7.24)

g_{\varUpsilon;A}(Z)e^{Z\varsigma}

is absolutely bounded for
Z\in C_{-d}^{3\pi/4}
by a summable function on the same curve and it converges, for
N
large, to
e^{-\frac{t}{2}\frac{Z^{3}}{3}+sZ}
. We can refine the estimate (
7.9
) as

(7.25)

\left|g_{\varUpsilon,A}(Z)e^{Z\varsigma}\right|\bigg|_{Z=Z=-d+|y|e^{\mathrm{sign}(y)\mathrm{i}3\pi/4}}\leq e^{-sd}\tilde{g}(y),

where
\tilde{g}\in\mathbb{L}^{1}(\mathbb{R})
and produce the bound

(7.26)

\widehat{\mathbf{L}}(X,Y)=O(e^{-sd}e^{-d(X+Y)}),

which holds independently of
N
. It is now clear that we can fix an
s^{*}
large enough so that, for any
s>s^{*}
the norm of
\widehat{\mathbf{L}}
can be made arbitrarily small uniformly in
N
.
Similar arguments allow to produce bounds

(7.27)

|\mathbf{A}(X+\varsigma)|,|\mathbf{A}^{\prime}(X+\varsigma)|\leq\mathrm{const.}e^{-(1/2+\omega)X},\qquad|\mathbf{B}(X+\varsigma)|,|\mathbf{B}^{\prime}(X+\varsigma)|<\mathrm{const.}e^{-dX},

which also hold uniformly in
N
. Thanks to this observation and to arguments detailed in the proof of
theorem

5.12
, the expression in the right hand side of (
5.58
) is well defined for any
N
. We can now proceed to evaluate the limit of functions
\mathbf{A}_{j},\mathbf{B}
and we obtain

(7.28)

\lim_{N\to\infty}\mathbf{A}_{j}(X+\varsigma)=\mathdutchcal{A}_{j}(X+s),\qquad\lim_{N\to\infty}\mathbf{B}(X+\varsigma)=\mathdutchcal{B}(X+s),

which combined with (
7.16
) imply, by bounded convergence, that

\lim_{N\to\infty}\mathrm{Pf}\left[J-\widehat{\mathbf{L}}\right]_{\mathbb{L}^{2}(\varsigma,+\infty)}\bigg(1-\langle(\mathbf{B},-\mathbf{B}^{\prime})|\left(1-J^{T}\widehat{\mathbf{L}}\right)^{-1}|(\mathbf{A}^{\prime},\mathbf{A})\rangle\bigg),\\
=\mathrm{Pf}\left[J-\widehat{\mathscr{L}}_{\mathrm{mSHE}}\right]_{\mathbb{L}^{2}(s,+\infty)}\bigg(1-\langle(\mathdutchcal{B},-\mathdutchcal{B}^{\prime})|\left(1-J^{T}\widehat{\mathscr{L}}_{\mathrm{mSHE}}\right)^{-1}|(\mathdutchcal{A}^{\prime},\mathdutchcal{A})\rangle\bigg),

completing the proof.
∎

7.3.
Alternative representation of kernel and matching with Krejenbrink-Le Doussal kernel

Here we recover, through simple manipulations of the matrix kernel
\mathscr{L}_{\mathrm{mSHE}}
, a Fredholm pfaffian formula for the solution of the mSHE in half space
\mathscr{Z}^{\mathrm{hs}}(0,t)
, previously found by Krejenbrink and Le Doussal in
[
KLD20
]
. In this last work, authors derived their expression for the Laplace transform
\mathbb{E}\left[e^{-e^{-s+\log\mathscr{Z}^{\mathrm{hs}}(0,t)+t/24}}\right]
via Bethe ansatz, although the procedure involved the summation over moments of
\mathscr{Z}^{\mathrm{hs}}(0,t)
, only a finite number of which are finite. Nevertheless, the solution proposed in
[
KLD20
]
, although not mathematically justified, turns out to be correct as we confirm in
corollary

7.4
below.

We introduce the function

(7.29)

\mathscr{F}_{s}(\theta)=\frac{1}{1+e^{-\theta+s}}

and the
2\times 2
matrix kernel

(7.30)

\mathscr{M}(\theta,\varphi)=\left(\begin{matrix}\mathscr{M}_{1,1}(\theta,\varphi)&\mathscr{M}_{1,2}(\theta,\varphi)\\
-\mathscr{M}_{1,2}(\varphi,\theta)&\mathscr{M}_{2,2}(\theta,\varphi)\end{matrix}\right).

Its coefficients are given by

(7.31)

\begin{split}\mathscr{M}_{1,1}(\theta,\varphi)=\int_{\mathrm{i}\mathbb{R}+d}\frac{\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}Z}{2\pi\mathrm{i}}\int_{\mathrm{i}\mathbb{R}+d}\frac{\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}W}{2\pi\mathrm{i}}&e^{\frac{t}{2}\left(\frac{Z^{3}}{3}+\frac{W^{3}}{3}\right)-\theta Z-\varphi W}\frac{Z-W}{Z+W}\Gamma(2Z)\Gamma(2W)\\
&\times\frac{\Gamma(\frac{1}{2}+\omega-Z)}{\Gamma(\frac{1}{2}+\omega+Z)}\frac{\Gamma(\frac{1}{2}+\omega-W)}{\Gamma(\frac{1}{2}+\omega+W)}\frac{\sin(\pi Z)}{\pi}\frac{\sin(\pi W)}{\pi},\end{split}

(7.32)

\begin{split}\mathscr{M}_{1,2}(\theta,\varphi)=\int_{\mathrm{i}\mathbb{R}+d}\frac{\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}Z}{2\pi\mathrm{i}}\int_{\mathrm{i}\mathbb{R}+d}\frac{\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}W}{2\pi\mathrm{i}}&e^{\frac{t}{2}\left(\frac{Z^{3}}{3}+\frac{W^{3}}{3}\right)-\theta Z-\varphi W}\frac{Z-W}{Z+W}\Gamma(2Z)\Gamma(2W)\\
&\times\frac{\Gamma(\frac{1}{2}+\omega-Z)}{\Gamma(\frac{1}{2}+\omega+Z)}\frac{\Gamma(\frac{1}{2}+\omega-W)}{\Gamma(\frac{1}{2}+\omega+W)}\frac{\sin(\pi Z)}{\pi}\cos(\pi W),\end{split}

(7.33)

\begin{split}\mathscr{M}_{2,2}(\theta,\varphi)=\int_{\mathrm{i}\mathbb{R}+d}\frac{\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}Z}{2\pi\mathrm{i}}\int_{\mathrm{i}\mathbb{R}+d}\frac{\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}W}{2\pi\mathrm{i}}&e^{\frac{t}{2}\left(\frac{Z^{3}}{3}+\frac{W^{3}}{3}\right)-\theta Z-\varphi W}\frac{Z-W}{Z+W}\Gamma(2Z)\Gamma(2W)\\
&\times\frac{\Gamma(\frac{1}{2}+\omega-Z)}{\Gamma(\frac{1}{2}+\omega+Z)}\frac{\Gamma(\frac{1}{2}+\omega-W)}{\Gamma(\frac{1}{2}+\omega+W)}\cos(\pi Z)\cos(\pi W).\end{split}

We are now ready to match our result of
theorem

1.3
with Krejenbrink and Le Doussal’s formulas
[
KLD20
, eq. (6), (7)]
.

Corollary 7.4
.

Under the hypothesis of
theorem

1.3
, we have

(7.34)

\mathbb{E}\left[e^{-e^{-s+\log\mathscr{Z}^{\mathrm{hs}}(0,t)+t/24}}\right]=\mathrm{Pf}\left[J-\mathscr{F}_{s}\cdot\mathscr{M}\right]_{\mathbb{L}^{2}(\mathbb{R})}.

Proof.

One can adapt the proof of
proposition

4.8
and write

(7.35)

J\mathscr{L}_{\mathrm{mSHE}}(X+s,Y+s)=\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\mathscr{F}_{s}(\theta)A(X,\theta)B(\theta,Y)\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\theta,

for some explicit operators
A(X,\theta),B(\theta,Y)
, which for convenience we do not report. To do so, rather than (
4.9
), (
4.49
) one needs to use the more canonical notable formulas

(7.36)

\sin[\pi(Z-W)]=\sin(\pi Z)\cos(\pi W)-\cos(\pi Z)\sin(\pi W)

and

(7.37)

\frac{e^{-s(Z+W)}}{\sin[\pi(Z+W)]}=\frac{1}{\pi}\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\frac{e^{-(Z+W)\theta}}{1+e^{-\theta+s}}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\theta,

where the second one is well posed only for
\Re\{Z+W\}\in(0,1)
. Then (
7.34
) follows from the symmetry
\mathrm{Pf}[J-J^{T}A\mathscr{F}_{s}B]=\mathrm{Pf}[J-\mathscr{F}_{s}BAJ^{T}]
of Fredholm pfaffians. We do not report the details of the argument any further.

∎

An immediate corollary of relation (
7.34
) is that the Laplace transform of the density function of
\mathscr{Z}^{\mathrm{hs}}(0,t)
is equal to the expectation of a multiplicative function of a pfaffian point process with correlation kernel
\mathscr{M}
. This had been observed in
[
KLD20
, eq. (8)]
and we report the same result below for completeness.

Corollary 7.5
.

Under the hypothesis of
theorem

1.3
we have

(7.38)

\mathbb{E}\left[e^{-e^{-s+\log\mathscr{Z}^{\mathrm{hs}}(0,t)+t/24}}\right]=\mathbb{E}_{\mathscr{M}}\left[\prod_{i=1}^{\infty}\frac{1}{1+e^{-s+\mathfrak{a}_{i}}}\right],

where in the right hand side
\{\mathfrak{a}_{i}:i\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 1}\}
is a pfaffian point process with correlation kernel
\mathscr{M}
.

Proof.

This is a straightforward consequence of (
7.34
) from the basic property (
4.65
) of pfaffian point processes.
∎

In
[
KLD20
]
authors discovered how to rewrite the Fredholm pfaffian of the
2\times 2
matrix kernel
\mathscr{M}
as a Fredholm determinant of a scalar kernel. We define

(7.39)

\mathscr{D}_{s}(x,y)=2\partial_{x}\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\theta\,\mathscr{F}_{s}(\theta)\left[\mathsf{f}_{\mathrm{even}}(x+\theta)\mathsf{f}_{\mathrm{odd}}(y+\theta)-\mathsf{f}_{\mathrm{odd}}(x+\theta)\mathsf{f}_{\mathrm{even}}(y+\theta)\right],

where

(7.40)

\mathsf{f}_{\mathrm{odd}}(\theta)=\int_{\mathrm{i}\mathbb{R}+d}\frac{\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}Z}{2\pi\mathrm{i}}e^{\frac{t}{2}\frac{Z^{3}}{3}-\theta Z}\frac{\Gamma(\frac{1}{2}+\omega-Z)}{\Gamma(\frac{1}{2}+\omega+Z)}\Gamma(2Z)\frac{\sin(\pi Z)}{\pi},

(7.41)

\mathsf{f}_{\mathrm{even}}(\theta)=\int_{\mathrm{i}\mathbb{R}+d}\frac{\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}Z}{2\pi\mathrm{i}}e^{\frac{t}{2}\frac{Z^{3}}{3}-\theta Z}\frac{\Gamma(\frac{1}{2}+\omega-Z)}{\Gamma(\frac{1}{2}+\omega+Z)}\Gamma(2Z)\cos(\pi Z).

Corollary 7.6
.

Under the assumptions of
theorem

1.3
we have

(7.42)

\mathbb{E}\left[e^{-e^{-s+\log\mathscr{Z}^{\mathrm{hs}}(0,t)+t/24}}\right]=\sqrt{\det[1-\mathscr{D}_{s}]_{\mathbb{L}^{2}(\mathbb{R})}}.

Proof.

It was shown in
[
KLD20
, Proposition B.2]
, that

(7.43)

\mathrm{Pf}[J-\mathscr{F}_{s}\cdot\mathscr{M}]_{\mathbb{L}^{2}(\mathbb{R})}=\sqrt{\det[1-\mathscr{D}_{s}]_{\mathbb{L}^{2}(\mathbb{R})}},

so that
corollary

7.4
yields the proof.
∎

7.4.
Asymptotic fluctuations of
\mathscr{Z}^{\mathrm{hs}}(0,t)
and Baik-Rains phase transition: proof of
theorem

1.7

In this section we consider large
t
asymptotics of our Fredholm pfaffian expressions (
1.14
), (
7.21
) for the distribution of
\mathscr{Z}^{\mathrm{hs}}(0,t)
. Such asymptotic analysis is fundamentally equivalent to those considered in
section

6.4
and therefore in the discussion below we will at times omit the details of our arguments.

Proof of
theorem

1.7
.

As usual we will proceed case by case, depending on the value of
\omega
.

\omega>-\frac{1}{2}
: convergence to crossover distribution.
Consider the exact formula (
1.14
) and set

(7.44)

s=r\left(\frac{t}{2}\right)^{1/3}.

Then, using relation (
5.15
) we have

(7.45)

\lim_{t\to\infty}\mathbb{E}\left[e^{-e^{-s+\log\mathscr{Z}^{\mathrm{hs}}(0,t)+t/24}}\right]=\lim_{t\to+\infty}\mathbb{P}\left[\frac{\log\mathscr{Z}^{\mathrm{hs}}(0,t)+t/24}{2^{-1/3}t^{1/3}}\leq r\right].

We now move to the right hand side of (
1.14
). After a change of variables

(7.46)

X=u\left(\frac{t}{2}\right)^{1/3},\qquad Y=v\left(\frac{t}{2}\right)^{1/3},\qquad Z=\alpha\left(\frac{2}{t}\right)^{1/3},\qquad W=\beta\left(\frac{2}{t}\right)^{1/3}

and a repeated use of approximations
\Gamma(z)\approx\frac{1}{z}
,
\sin z\approx z

we compute the scaling limit of function
\mathscr{K}^{\mathrm{hs}}
as

(7.47)

\begin{split}\lim_{t\to\infty}\mathscr{K}^{\mathrm{hs}}(X(u),Y(v))=\mathpzc{k}^{(\xi)}(u,v),\end{split}

where
\mathpzc{k}^{(\xi)}
is defined in (
6.7
). Notice that the integration contours were rescaled too after the change of variables and they have been set to
C^{\pi/3}_{\delta}
. Analogous limits for derivatives of
\mathscr{K}^{\mathrm{hs}}
can be established and we have

(7.48)

\lim_{t\to\infty}\left(\begin{matrix}1&0\\
0&\left(\frac{2}{t}\right)^{1/3}\end{matrix}\right)\mathscr{L}_{\mathrm{mSHE}}(X(u),Y(v))\left(\begin{matrix}1&0\\
0&\left(\frac{2}{t}\right)^{1/3}\end{matrix}\right)=\mathcal{K}^{(\xi)}_{\mathrm{cross}}(u,v).

Exponential bounds, in the variables
u,v
, for the rescaled kernel
\mathscr{L}_{\mathrm{mSHE}}(X(u),Y(v))
now allow to conclude that

(7.49)

\lim_{t\to\infty}\mathrm{Pf}\left[J-\mathscr{L}_{\mathrm{mSHE}}\right]_{\mathbb{L}^{2}(s,+\infty)}=\mathrm{Pf}\left[J-\mathcal{K}_{\mathrm{cross}}^{(\xi)}\right]_{\mathbb{L}^{2}(r,+\infty)}.

\omega=-\frac{1}{2}
: convergence to GOE Tracy-Widom distribution.
When
\omega
tends to
-1/2
we can no longer choose, in the integral expression of
\mathscr{K}^{\mathrm{hs}}(X,Y)
, contours
C^{\pi/3}_{d}
with
0<d<\omega+1/2
. Let
\mathscr{K}^{\mathrm{hs}}_{\mathrm{ext}}(X,Y)
be the analytic extension of
\mathscr{K}^{\mathrm{hs}}(X,Y)
in the parameter
\omega
. Then it is clear that

(7.50)

\mathscr{K}^{\mathrm{hs}}_{\mathrm{ext}}(X,Y)\big|_{\omega=-\frac{1}{2}}=\mathscr{K}^{\mathrm{hs}}(X,Y)\big|_{\omega=-\frac{1}{2}}-(\mathdutchcal{B}(Y)-\mathdutchcal{B}(X))\big|_{\omega=-\frac{1}{2}}

and hence

(7.51)

\left(\mathscr{L}_{\mathrm{mSHE}}\right)_{\mathrm{ext}}(X,Y)\big|_{\omega=-\frac{1}{2}}=\mathscr{L}_{\mathrm{mSHE}}(X,Y)\big|_{\omega=-\frac{1}{2}}-\left(\begin{matrix}1&-\partial_{Y}\\
-\partial_{X}&\partial_{X}\partial_{Y}\end{matrix}\right)(\mathdutchcal{B}(Y)-\mathdutchcal{B}(X))\big|_{\omega=-\frac{1}{2}},

where, naturally
\left(\mathscr{L}_{\mathrm{mSHE}}\right)_{\mathrm{ext}}
denotes the analytic extension of
\mathscr{L}_{\mathrm{mSHE}}
. After the change of variables (
7.46
) it is simple to compute the limit

(7.52)

\lim_{t\to\infty}\mathscr{K}^{\mathrm{hs}}_{\mathrm{ext}}(X(u),Y(v))\big|_{\omega=-\frac{1}{2}}=\mathpzc{k}^{(\infty)}(u,v)-\mathpzc{B}(v)+\mathpzc{B}(u)

and similarly

(7.53)

\lim_{t\to\infty}\left(\begin{matrix}1&0\\
0&\left(\frac{2}{t}\right)^{1/3}\end{matrix}\right)\left(\mathscr{L}_{\mathrm{mSHE}}\right)_{\mathrm{ext}}(X(u),Y(v))\big|_{\omega=-\frac{1}{2}}\left(\begin{matrix}1&0\\
0&\left(\frac{2}{t}\right)^{1/3}\end{matrix}\right)=\mathcal{K}_{\mathrm{GOE}}(u,v).

Recall that
\mathcal{K}_{\mathrm{GOE}},\mathpzc{k}^{(\infty)}
and
\mathpzc{B}
were given in
definition

6.5
, (
6.8
) and (
6.11
). Although the kernel
\left(\mathscr{L}_{\mathrm{mSHE}}\right)_{\mathrm{ext}}(X(u),Y(v))\big|_{\omega=-\frac{1}{2}}
does not define a bounded operator on
\mathbb{L}^{2}(s,+\infty)\times\mathbb{L}^{2}(s,+\infty)
, its Fredholm pfaffian defines an absolutely convergent series, uniformly in
u,v>r
. This can be verified as in the proof of the case
\gamma=1
of
theorem

6.7
. Due to the exponential decay of terms of functions
\mathscr{K}^{\mathrm{hs}}(X(u),Y(v))\big|_{\omega=-\frac{1}{2}},\mathdutchcal{B}(X(u))\big|_{\omega=-\frac{1}{2}}
we are able to pass the limit symbol inside the Fredholm pfaffian and establish the limit

(7.54)

\lim_{t\to\infty}\mathrm{Pf}\left[J-\left(\mathscr{L}_{\mathrm{mSHE}}\right)_{\mathrm{ext}}\big|_{\omega=-\frac{1}{2}}\right]_{\mathbb{L}^{2}(s,+\infty)}=\mathrm{Pf}\left[J-\mathcal{K}_{\mathrm{GOE}}\right]_{\mathbb{L}^{2}(r,+\infty)},

which proves (
1.21
) for
\omega=-\frac{1}{2}
.

\omega<-\frac{1}{2}
: convergence to gaussian distribution.
In this case we consider the exact formula (
7.21
) and we set

(7.55)

s=\frac{t}{2}\left(\frac{1}{2}+\omega\right)^{2}+\sigma_{\omega}t^{1/2}r.

Notice that
tf_{\omega}=\frac{t}{24}+\frac{t}{2}\left(\frac{1}{2}+\omega\right)^{2}
. Taking the limit of the Laplace transform we get

(7.56)

\lim_{t\to+\infty}\mathbb{E}\left[e^{-e^{-s+\log\mathscr{Z}^{\mathrm{hs}}(0,t)+t/24}}\right]=\lim_{t\to+\infty}\mathbb{P}\left[\frac{\log\mathscr{Z}^{\mathrm{hs}}(0,t)+tf_{\omega}}{\sigma_{\omega}t^{1/2}}\leq r\right].

We move to the asymptotic limit of the right hand side of (
7.21
). For the analysis that follows we define the function

(7.57)

h_{\omega}(Z)=\frac{Z^{3}}{3}-\left(\frac{1}{2}+\omega\right)^{2}Z.

Let us first evaluate the limit of the matrix kernel
\widehat{\mathscr{L}}_{\mathrm{mSHE}}
which we write as

(7.58)

\widehat{\mathscr{L}}_{\mathrm{mSHE}}(X,Y)=\left(\begin{matrix}1&-\partial_{Y}\\
-\partial_{X}&\partial_{X}\partial_{Y}\end{matrix}\right)\left(\widehat{\mathscr{L}}_{\mathrm{mSHE}}\right)_{1,1}(X,Y),

where

(7.59)

\left(\widehat{\mathscr{L}}_{\mathrm{mSHE}}\right)_{1,1}(X,Y)=\mathscr{K}^{\mathrm{hs}}_{\delta-\frac{1}{2}-\omega}(X,Y)+\sum_{j=1}^{2m+\varepsilon-2}\mathscr{S}_{j}(X,Y).

In the previous expression
\mathscr{K}^{\mathrm{hs}}_{\delta-\frac{1}{2}-\omega}
denotes the function
\mathscr{K}^{\mathrm{hs}}
of (
1.16
) where the integration contours for the
Z
and
W
variables are taken to be
\mathrm{i}\mathbb{R}+\delta-\frac{1}{2}-\omega
, while the
\mathscr{S}_{j}
are the residues at the
Z
pole at
-W+j
of the
Z
-integrand of the
\mathscr{K}^{\mathrm{hs}}
. Here we are assuming that
\delta>0
is sufficiently small, but fixed. For more details on this identity see the passage around (
5.72
), where an analogous formula was derived in the study of the half space Log Gamma polymer partition function. After a change of variables

(7.60)

X=\frac{t}{2}\left(\frac{1}{2}+\omega\right)^{2}+\sigma_{\omega}t^{1/2}u,\qquad Y=\frac{t}{2}\left(\frac{1}{2}+\omega\right)^{2}+\sigma_{\omega}t^{1/2}v,

where we take
u,v>r
, we can write the expression for functions
\mathscr{S}_{j}

(7.61)

\begin{split}\mathscr{S}_{j}(X(u),Y(v))=(-1)^{j+1}\int_{\mathrm{i}\mathbb{R}+R}&\frac{\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}W}{2\pi\mathrm{i}}e^{\frac{t}{2}\left(h_{\omega}(W)+h_{\omega}(j-W)\right)-\sigma_{\omega}t^{1/2}(Wv+(j-W)u)}\\
&\times\frac{\Gamma(\frac{1}{2}+\omega+W-j)}{\Gamma(\frac{1}{2}+\omega-W+j)}\frac{\Gamma(\frac{1}{2}+\omega-W)}{\Gamma(\frac{1}{2}+\omega+W)}\frac{\Gamma(2j-2W)}{\Gamma(1-2W)},\end{split}

where
R
in the integration contours can be taken in the interval
(\frac{1}{2}+\omega-1,-\frac{1}{2}-\omega-j+1)
. We obtain the estitmate

(7.62)

\left|\mathscr{S}_{j}(X(u),Y(v))\right|\leq\mathrm{const.}e^{\frac{t}{2}\left[h_{\omega}(R)+h_{\omega}(j-R)\right]-\sigma_{\omega}t^{1/2}\left[Rv+(j-R)u\right]},

where the constant can be made independent of
t,R
. Similarly we have

(7.63)

\begin{split}\mathscr{K}^{\mathrm{hs}}_{-\frac{1}{2}-\omega+\delta}(X(u),Y(v))=&\int_{\mathrm{i}\mathbb{R}+\delta-\frac{1}{2}-\omega}\frac{\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}Z}{2\pi\mathrm{i}}\int_{\mathrm{i}\mathbb{R}+\delta-\frac{1}{2}-\omega}\frac{\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}W}{2\pi\mathrm{i}}e^{\frac{t}{2}\left[h_{\omega}(Z)+h_{\omega}(W)\right]-\sigma_{\omega}t^{1/2}(Zu-Wv)}\\
&\times\frac{\Gamma(\frac{1}{2}+\omega-Z)}{\Gamma(\frac{1}{2}+\omega+Z)}\frac{\Gamma(\frac{1}{2}+\omega-W)}{\Gamma(\frac{1}{2}+\omega+W)}\Gamma(2Z)\Gamma(2W)\frac{\sin[\pi(Z-W)]}{\sin[\pi(Z+W)]},\end{split}

from where we get

(7.64)

\left|\mathscr{K}^{\mathrm{hs}}_{-\frac{1}{2}-\omega+\delta}(X(u),Y(v))\right|\leq\mathrm{const.}e^{t\,h_{\omega}\left(\delta-\frac{1}{2}-\omega\right)-\sigma_{\omega}t^{1/2}\left(\delta-\frac{1}{2}-\omega\right)(u+v)}.

Combining estimates (
7.62
), where we might set
R=\frac{j}{2}
, (
7.64
) with (
7.58
), (
7.59
) we see that

(7.65)

\lim_{t\to\infty}\mathrm{Pf}\left[J-\widehat{\mathscr{L}}_{\mathrm{mSHE}}\right]_{\mathbb{L}(s,+\infty)}=1,

since
h_{\omega}(\delta-\frac{1}{2}-\omega),h_{\omega}(\frac{j}{2})<0
, respectively for
\delta>0
small and
1\leq j\leq 2\left(\frac{1}{2}+\omega\right)^{2}
and all terms of kernel
\widehat{\mathscr{L}}_{\mathrm{mSHE}}
are exponentially decaying in
t
.

We also write the scaling form of functions
\mathdutchcal{A}_{j}
as

(7.66)

\mathdutchcal{A}_{j}(X(u))=\frac{(-1)^{j}}{j!}e^{\frac{t}{2}h(\frac{1}{2}+\omega+j)-\sigma_{v}t^{1/2}(1/2+\omega+j)u}\frac{\Gamma(1+2\omega+2j)}{\Gamma(1+2\omega+j)}

and using saddle point method we compute the large
t
limit of function
\mathdutchcal{B}
, to find

(7.67)

\begin{split}\mathdutchcal{B}(X(u))&=\int_{\mathrm{i}\mathbb{R}-\frac{1}{2}-\omega}\frac{\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}W}{2\pi\mathrm{i}}e^{\frac{t}{2}h_{\omega}(W)-\sigma_{\omega}t^{1/2}uW}\Gamma(2W)\frac{\Gamma(\frac{1}{2}-\omega-W)}{\Gamma(\frac{1}{2}-\omega+W)}\\
&=\frac{1}{\sigma_{\omega}t^{1/2}}e^{\frac{t}{2}h_{\omega}(-\frac{1}{2}-\omega)-\sigma_{\omega}t^{1/2}(-\frac{1}{2}-\omega)u}\frac{1}{-1-2\omega}\frac{e^{-u^{2}/2}}{\sqrt{2\pi}}(1+o(1)).\end{split}

Since the operator
\widehat{\mathscr{L}}_{\mathrm{mSHE}}
is arbitrarily small in norm we can expand the resolvent
(1-J^{T}\widehat{\mathscr{L}}_{\mathrm{mSHE}})
in Neumann series and the scalar product in the right hand side of (
7.21
) becomes

(7.68)

\begin{split}\langle(\mathdutchcal{B},-\mathdutchcal{B}^{\prime})|\left(1-J^{T}\widehat{\mathscr{L}}_{\mathrm{mSHE}}\right)^{-1}|(\mathdutchcal{A}^{\prime},\mathdutchcal{A})\rangle&=\sum_{\ell\geq 0}\langle(\mathdutchcal{B},-\mathdutchcal{B}^{\prime})|\left(J^{T}\widehat{\mathscr{L}}_{\mathrm{mSHE}}\right)^{\ell}|(\mathdutchcal{A}^{\prime},\mathdutchcal{A})\rangle.\end{split}

As observed several times in different places in the text, functions
\mathdutchcal{A}_{j}
are divergent, yet by means of estimates (
7.62
), (
7.64
) we can show that all scalar products
\langle(\mathdutchcal{B},-\mathdutchcal{B}^{\prime})|\left(J^{T}\widehat{\mathscr{L}}_{\mathrm{mSHE}}\right)^{\ell}|(\mathdutchcal{A}^{\prime},\mathdutchcal{A})\rangle
are numerically convergent and they are of order
O(e^{-ct\ell})
, for some constant
c>0
. More details on this argument can be found in the proof of the case
\varUpsilon<0
of
theorem

6.9
above. We obtain

(7.69)

\sum_{\ell\geq 0}\langle(\mathdutchcal{B},-\mathdutchcal{B}^{\prime})|\left(J^{T}\widehat{\mathscr{L}}_{\mathrm{mSHE}}\right)^{\ell}|(\mathdutchcal{A}^{\prime},\mathdutchcal{A})\rangle=\langle(\mathdutchcal{B},-\mathdutchcal{B}^{\prime})|(\mathdutchcal{A}^{\prime},\mathdutchcal{A})\rangle+o(1)=\int_{r}^{\infty}\frac{e^{-u^{2}/2}}{\sqrt{2\pi}}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}u+o(1).

Finally we evaluate the right hand side of (
7.21
) to find

\lim_{t\to\infty}\mathrm{Pf}\left[J-\widehat{\mathscr{L}}_{\mathrm{mSHE}}\right]_{\mathbb{L}^{2}(s,+\infty)}\bigg(1-\langle(\mathdutchcal{B},-\mathdutchcal{B}^{\prime})|\left(1-J^{T}\widehat{\mathscr{L}}_{\mathrm{mSHE}}\right)^{-1}|(\mathdutchcal{A}^{\prime},\mathdutchcal{A})\rangle\bigg)\\
=\int^{r}_{-\infty}\frac{e^{-u^{2}/2}}{\sqrt{2\pi}}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}u,

which concludes the proof.
∎

Appendix A
Fredholm determinant and Fredholm pfaffian

Given a kernel
K(x,y)\in\mathbb{L}^{2}(\Omega\times\Omega,\nu\otimes\nu)
we define its
Fredholm determinant
as

(A.1)

\det(1-K)_{\mathbb{L}^{2}(\Omega)}=1+\sum_{\ell\geq 1}\frac{(-1)^{\ell}}{\ell!}\int_{\Omega^{\ell}}\det[K(x_{i},x_{j})]_{i,j=1}^{\ell}\nu(\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}x_{1})\cdots\nu(\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}x_{\ell}),

whenever the right hand side is convergent.

Consider a
2\times 2
matrix kernel

(A.2)

k(x,y)=\left(\begin{matrix}k_{1,1}(x,y)&k_{1,2}(x,y)\\
-k_{1,2}(y,x)&k_{2,2}(x,y)\end{matrix}\right),

where
k_{i,j}\in\mathbb{L}^{2}(\Omega\times\Omega,\nu\otimes\nu)
and moreover
k_{i,i}(y,x)=-k_{i,i}(x,y)
for all
x,y\in\Omega
and
i=1,2
. The
Fredholm pfaffian
of
k
is defined as

(A.3)

\mathrm{Pf}(J-k)_{\mathbb{L}^{2}(\Omega)}=1+\sum_{\ell\geq 1}\frac{(-1)^{\ell}}{\ell!}\int_{\Omega^{\ell}}\mathrm{Pf}[k(x_{i},x_{j})]_{i,j=1}^{\ell}\nu(\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}x_{1})\cdots\nu(\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}x_{\ell}).

For instance the first few terms of the expansion above are

1-\int_{\Omega}\mathrm{Pf}\left(\begin{matrix}0&k_{1,2}(x,x)\\
-k_{1,2}(x,x)&0\end{matrix}\right)\nu(\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}x)\\
+\frac{1}{2}\int_{\Omega^{2}}\mathrm{Pf}\left(\begin{matrix}0&k_{1,2}(x,x)&k_{1,1}(x,y)&k_{1,2}(x,y)\\
-k_{1,2}(x,x)&0&-k_{1,2}(y,x)&k_{2,2}(x,y)\\
k_{1,1}(y,x)&k_{1,2}(y,x)&0&k_{1,2}(y,y)\\
-k_{1,2}(x,y)&k_{2,2}(y,x)&-k_{1,2}(y,y)&0\end{matrix}\right)\nu(\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}x)\nu(\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}y)+\cdots.

The Fredholm pfaffian of a skew-symmetric matrix kernel
k
is related to its Fredholm determinants via the relation
[
Rai00
, Lemma 8.1]

(A.4)

\mathrm{Pf}[J-k]_{\mathbb{L}^{2}(\Omega)}^{2}=\det[1-J^{T}k]_{\mathbb{L}^{2}(\Omega)}

For any non vanishing function
f:\Omega\to\mathbb{R}
we can define a rescaled kernel

(A.5)

\tilde{k}(x,y)=\left(\begin{matrix}f(x)f(y)k_{1,1}(x,y)&\frac{f(x)}{f(y)}k_{1,2}(x,y)\\
-\frac{f(y)}{f(x)}k_{1,2}(y,x)&\frac{1}{f(x)f(y)}k_{2,2}(x,y)\end{matrix}\right),

so that by notable properties of determinants we have

(A.6)

\mathrm{Pf}(J-k)_{\mathbb{L}^{2}(\Omega)}=\mathrm{Pf}(J-\tilde{k})_{\mathbb{L}^{2}(\Omega)}

The pfaffian of a skew-symmetric matrix
M
of size
2n\times 2n
can be expressed through the expansion

(A.7)

\mathrm{Pf}[M]=\sum_{\alpha=\{(i_{1},j_{1}),\dots,(i_{n},j_{n})\}}\epsilon(\alpha)M_{i_{1},j_{1}}\cdots M_{i_{n},j_{n}},

where the summation runs over choices of
\alpha
such that
i_{1},j_{1},\dots,i_{n},j_{n}
is a permutation of
\{1,\dots,2n\}
with
i_{1}<\cdots<i_{n}
and
i_{r}<j_{r}
for
r=1,\dots,n
. Finally
\epsilon(\alpha)
is the sign of the permutation
\left(\begin{smallmatrix}1&2&\cdots&2n-1&2n\\
i_{1}&j_{1}&\cdots&i_{n}&j_{n}\end{smallmatrix}\right)
.

A useful tool to establish convergence of Fredholm determinants and pfaffians is the Hadamard’s bound. Assume that a matrix
M=(M_{i,j})_{i,j=1}^{\ell}
has coeffients bounded as
|M_{i,j}|\leq a_{i}b_{j}
for some numbers
a_{i},b_{i}>0,i=1,\dots,\ell
. Then we have

(A.8)

\left|\det(M)\right|\leq\ell^{\ell/2}\prod_{i=1}^{\ell}a_{i}b_{i}.

Such bound is easily adapded to pfaffians of anti-symmetric matrices
M
using the relation
\mathrm{Pf}[M]=\sqrt{\det(M)}
.

Proposition A.1
.

Let
a,b\in\mathbb{L}^{2}(\Omega,\nu)
and
T:\mathbb{L}^{2}(\Omega,\nu)\to\mathbb{L}^{2}(\Omega,\nu)
be a linear operator.
Define the
2\times 2
matrix kernel

(A.9)

R(x,y)=\left(\begin{matrix}a(x)b(y)-a(y)b(x)&-a(x)b^{\prime}(y)+a^{\prime}(y)b(x)\\
-a^{\prime}(x)b(y)+a(y)b^{\prime}(x)&a^{\prime}(x)b^{\prime}(y)-a^{\prime}(y)b^{\prime}(x)\end{matrix}\right),

where
a^{\prime}=T(a),b^{\prime}=T(b)
. Then the matrix
(R(x_{i},x_{j}))_{i,j=1}^{n}
has rank at most 2 for any
n
.

Proof.

Let
u,v\in\mathbb{L}^{2}(\Omega,\nu)
. Then we have

(A.10)

\int_{\Omega}R(x,y)\cdot\binom{u(y)}{v(y)}\nu(dy)=(\langle b|u\rangle-\langle b^{\prime}|v\rangle)\binom{a(x)}{-a^{\prime}(x)}+(\langle a|u\rangle-\langle a^{\prime}|v\rangle)\binom{b(x)}{b^{\prime}(x)},

which shows that the image of the operator defined by
R(x,y)
is a subspace of
\mathbb{L}^{2}(\Omega,\nu)\times\mathbb{L}^{2}(\Omega,\nu)
of dimension at most 2.
∎

Proposition A.2
.

Let
L
be a
2\times 2
matrix kernel of trace class, with norm
\|L\|<1
and let
R
be a rank 2 perturbation as in (
A.9
). Then we have

(A.11)

\mathrm{Pf}[J-(L+R)]=\mathrm{Pf}[J-L](1-\langle B|\widetilde{A}\rangle),

where
B=(b,-b^{\prime})
and
\widetilde{A}=(1-J^{T}L)^{-1}\binom{a^{\prime}}{a}
.

Proof.

Define
G=(1-J^{T}L)^{-1}
. Using relation (
A.4
) and the multiplicative property of determinants we have

(A.12)

\mathrm{Pf}[J-(L+R)]=\sqrt{\det[1-J^{T}(L+R)]}=\sqrt{\det[1-J^{T}L]}\sqrt{\det[1-GJ^{T}R]}.

We rewrite the matrix
J^{T}R
in the more convenient notation

(A.13)

J^{T}R=\left(\begin{matrix}a^{\prime}\\
a\end{matrix}\right)\cdot(b,-b^{\prime})-\left(\begin{matrix}b^{\prime}\\
b\end{matrix}\right)\cdot(a,-a^{\prime}).

Denoting
\widetilde{A}
as in the statement of the proposition and
\widetilde{B}=G\binom{q^{\prime}}{q}
we have

(A.14)

GJ^{T}R=\widetilde{A}\cdot(b,-b^{\prime})-\widetilde{B}\cdot(a,-a^{\prime})

so that

(A.15)

\det[1-GJ^{T}R]=\det\left[1-\left(\begin{matrix}\langle B|\widetilde{A}\rangle&-\langle B|\widetilde{B}\rangle\\
\langle A|\widetilde{A}\rangle&-\langle A|\widetilde{B}\rangle\end{matrix}\right)\right],

where
A=(a,-a^{\prime})
. We will deduce, by the skew-symmetric property of the kernel
L
, that
\langle B|\widetilde{B}\rangle=\langle A|\widetilde{A}\rangle=0
and
\langle B|\widetilde{A}\rangle=-\langle A|\widetilde{B}\rangle
. We only show that
\langle A|\widetilde{A}\rangle=0
as the identity
\langle B|\widetilde{B}\rangle=0
can be proven in the same way.
We consider the representation of
G
as a Neumann series of the operator
J^{T}L
and we write

(A.16)

\begin{split}\langle A|\widetilde{A}\rangle&=(a,-a^{\prime})\cdot\sum_{\ell\geq 0}\left(J^{T}L\right)^{\ell}\cdot\left(\begin{matrix}a^{\prime}\\
a\end{matrix}\right)\\
&=-\sum_{\ell\geq 0}(a^{\prime},a)\cdot J\cdot\left(J^{T}L\right)^{\ell}\cdot\left(\begin{matrix}a^{\prime}\\
a\end{matrix}\right).\end{split}

In the right hand side we recognize that for each
\ell
the operator
J\cdot(J^{T}L)^{\ell}
is skew symmetric and hence each term in the summation is identically zero. Let us now apply the same idea to show that
\langle B|\widetilde{A}\rangle=-\langle A|\widetilde{B}\rangle
. We have

(A.17)

\begin{split}\langle B|\widetilde{A}\rangle&=-\sum_{\ell\geq 0}(b^{\prime},b)\cdot J\cdot(J^{T}L)^{\ell}\cdot\left(\begin{matrix}a^{\prime}\\
a\end{matrix}\right)\\
&=-\sum_{\ell\geq 0}(a^{\prime},a)\cdot(L^{T}J)^{\ell}\cdot J^{T}\cdot\left(\begin{matrix}b^{\prime}\\
b\end{matrix}\right)\\
&=\sum_{\ell\geq 0}(a^{\prime},a)\cdot J\cdot(J^{T}L)^{\ell}\cdot\left(\begin{matrix}b^{\prime}\\
b\end{matrix}\right)=-\langle A|\widetilde{B}\rangle.\end{split}

These computations prove that

(A.18)

\det[1-GJ^{T}R]=(1-\langle B|\widetilde{A}\rangle)^{2}

and along with (
A.12
) complete the proof.
∎

Appendix B
Estimates on
q
-Pochhammer symbols

In this section we will prove a number of bounds on
q
-Pochhammer symbols necessary to establish Fredholm determinant and pfaffian formulas for the distribution of the Log Gamma polymer reported in
theorem

5.4
and
theorem

5.10
. Together with the classical and well known results of
lemma

B.1
and
lemma

B.4
, we will present additional refined estimates as those of
lemma

B.6
,
lemma

B.7
, which appear to be new. In deriving these new bounds we will only use elementary techniques.

B.1.
Useful bounds for the proof of
theorem

5.4

We will start stating a number of preliminary lemmas concerning the convergence of
q
-Pochhammer symbols and Theta functions.

Lemma B.1
.

Let
\chi\sim q
-
\mathrm{Geo}(q)
and consider the scaling
q=e^{-\varepsilon}
. Then, we have the following asymptotic equivalence in distribution

(B.1)

\chi=\varepsilon^{-1}\log\varepsilon^{-1}+\varepsilon^{-1}\mathcal{G}+O(1),

where
\mathcal{G}
is a Gumbel random variable.

Proof.

The unnormalized density function of the random variable
\chi
is
k\mapsto q^{k}/(q;q)_{k}
for
k\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
. After scaling and change of variables
k=\varepsilon^{-1}\log\varepsilon^{-1}+\varepsilon^{-1}y
, it becomes, up to constant terms in
y

(B.2)

e^{-y-e^{-y}+o(1)},

where the error
o(1)
is uniformly bounded in
y
and tends to zero, for
\varepsilon
small, uniformly in
y
ranging in any half line
[M,\infty)
. This follows from the asymptotic expansion (see
[
BC14
, Proposition 4.1.9]
)

(B.3)

\log(q;q)_{\varepsilon^{-1}\log\varepsilon^{-1}+\varepsilon^{-1}y}=-\varepsilon^{-1}\frac{\pi^{2}}{6}-\frac{1}{2}\log\frac{\varepsilon}{2\pi}+e^{-y}+o(1).

From the expression of the unnormalized density function after rescaling we conclude that, in distribution
\varepsilon\chi-\log\varepsilon^{-1}
converges to a Gumbel random variable.
∎

Lemma B.2
.

Let
\theta\sim\mathrm{Theta}(\zeta;q)
and consider the scaling
\zeta=(1-q)^{n}e^{-\varsigma}

q=e^{-\varepsilon}
. Then, we have the following asymptotic equivalence in distribution

(B.4)

\theta=-\varepsilon^{-1}n\log\varepsilon^{-1}-\varepsilon^{-1}\varsigma+\varepsilon^{-1/2}\mathcal{N}(0,1)+O(1),

where
\mathcal{N}(0,1)
is a centered gaussian random variable of variance 1.

Proof.

The unnormalized density function of the random variable
\theta
is, after the scaling,

(B.5)

e^{-\frac{\varepsilon k^{2}}{2}+k(-\varsigma+n\log(1-e^{-\varepsilon}))}

with
k\in\mathbb{Z}
. After a change of variable
k=-\varepsilon^{-1}\varsigma+\varepsilon^{-1}n\log(1-e^{-\varepsilon})+\varepsilon^{-1/2}x
it becomes, modulo normalization, equal to
e^{-x^{2}/2}
, completing the proof.
∎

Lemma B.3
.

Set
q=e^{-\varepsilon}
, with
\varepsilon>0
. Let
\varsigma\in\mathbb{R},n\in\mathbb{R}_{+}
and
\alpha=-d+\mathrm{i}y
with
d\in(0,1)
and
y\in[-\varepsilon^{-1}\pi,\varepsilon^{-1}\pi]
. Then, we have

(B.6)

\lim_{\varepsilon\downarrow 0}(1-q)^{\alpha n}\frac{\vartheta_{3}(q^{\alpha}e^{-\varsigma}(1-q)^{n};q)}{\vartheta_{3}(e^{-\varsigma}(1-q)^{n};q)}=e^{-\alpha\varsigma},

where the convergence holds locally uniformly in
y
.
Moreover, there exists a constant
C
depending on
\varsigma,d,n
such that

(B.7)

\left|(1-q)^{\alpha n}\frac{\vartheta_{3}(q^{\alpha}e^{-\varsigma}(1-q)^{n};q)}{\vartheta_{3}(e^{-\varsigma}(1-q)^{n};q)}\right|\leq C,

for all
\varepsilon>0,y\in[-\varepsilon^{-1}\pi,\varepsilon^{-1}\pi]
.

Proof.

We will first prove the convergence result (
B.6
) and later we will establish the bound (
B.7
).
From the Jacobi triple product identity we have

(B.8)

\vartheta_{3}(e^{\eta},e^{-\varepsilon})=\sum_{k\in\mathbb{Z}}e^{-\frac{\varepsilon k^{2}}{2}+\eta k},

which, after completing the square in the exponent and operating the change of variable
x=\sqrt{\varepsilon}k-\frac{\eta}{\sqrt{\varepsilon}}
, becomes

(B.9)

\frac{e^{\frac{\eta^{2}}{2\varepsilon}}}{\sqrt{\varepsilon}}\sum_{x\in\sqrt{\varepsilon}\mathbb{Z}+\frac{\eta}{\sqrt{\varepsilon}}}e^{-\frac{x^{2}}{2}}\sqrt{\varepsilon}.

This proves the asymptotic expansions

(B.10)

\vartheta_{3}(e^{\eta};e^{-\varepsilon})=\sqrt{\frac{2\pi}{\varepsilon}}e^{\frac{\eta^{2}}{2\varepsilon}}\left(1+O(\sqrt{\varepsilon})\right),

which holds when
\Im(\eta)=o(\sqrt{\varepsilon})
. Applying (
B.10
) to the cases
\eta=-\varsigma+n\log(1-e^{-\varepsilon})
and
\eta=-\varsigma+n\log(1-e^{-\varepsilon})-\varepsilon\alpha
we can easily verify (
B.6
).

In order to show the bound (
B.7
), we will prove that the absolute value of the theta function
\vartheta_{3}(e^{a+\mathrm{i}y},e^{-\varepsilon})
, with
a\in\mathbb{R}
and
y\in[-\varepsilon^{-1}\pi,\varepsilon^{-1}\pi]
has a maximum at
y=0
. To prove this we show that
|(-qe^{a+\mathrm{i}y};q)_{\infty}|
is a decreasing function for
y\in[0,\varepsilon^{-1}\pi)
. For this we compute the derivative

(B.11)

\begin{split}\frac{\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}}{\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}y}\Re\left\{\log(-qe^{a+\mathrm{i}y};q)_{\infty}\right\}&=\frac{\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}}{\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}y}\sum_{k\geq 0}\Re\left\{\log(1+q^{k}e^{a+\mathrm{i}y})\right\}\\
&=\frac{\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}}{\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}y}\sum_{k\geq 0}\frac{1}{2}\log(1+q^{2k}e^{2a}+2q^{k}e^{a}\cos y)\\
&=\frac{1}{2}\sum_{k\geq 0}\frac{-q^{k}e^{a}\sin y}{1+q^{2k}e^{2a}+2q^{k}e^{a}\cos y},\end{split}

which clearly is negative if
y\in[0,\varepsilon^{-1}\pi)
. Using the product expansion of the Jacobi theta function we can prove that

(B.12)

\left|(1-q)^{-n(d-\mathrm{i}y)}\frac{\vartheta_{3}(q^{-d+\mathrm{i}y}e^{-\varsigma}(1-q)^{n};q)}{\vartheta_{3}(e^{-\varsigma}(1-q)^{n};q)}\right|\leq(1-q)^{-dn}\frac{\vartheta_{3}(q^{-d}e^{-\varsigma}(1-q)^{n};q)}{\vartheta_{3}(e^{-\varsigma}(1-q)^{n};q)}

and since the right hand side converges to
e^{-d\varsigma}
as
\varepsilon\to 0
, it can certainly be bounded by a constant independent of
\varepsilon
.
∎

The following convergence result is classical.

Lemma B.4

(
[
And86
]
, Appendix A)
.

The
q
-Gamma function
\Gamma_{q}(X)
converges, as
q\to 1
, to
\Gamma(X)
locally uniformly in
X\in\mathbb{C}\setminus\mathbb{Z}_{\leq 0}
.

We recall that, similarly to the classical Gamma function, the
q
-Gamma function
\Gamma_{q}(X)
, introduced in (
5.23
) satisfies the functional equation

(B.13)

[X]_{q}\Gamma_{q}(X)=\Gamma_{q}(X+1),

where
[X]_{q}=\frac{1-q^{X}}{1-q}
are the
q
-numbers.

Below we will establish some bounds for the absolute value of
q
-Gamma function
\Gamma_{q}(z)
, which are uniform in
q\in(1/2,1)
and
z
with bounded real part. Techniques we will employ are elementary. The following lemma will be useful.

Lemma B.5
.

Consider real numbers
a<b
and continuous functions
f,g:[a-1,b+1]\to\mathbb{R}
such that
f
is increasing, while
g
is decreasing. Then, we have

(B.14)

\int_{a-1}^{b}f(k)\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}k\leq\sum_{k\in\mathbb{Z}\cap[a,b]}f(k)\leq\int_{a}^{b+1}f(k)\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}k

and

(B.15)

\int_{a}^{b-1}g(k)\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}k\leq\sum_{k\in\mathbb{Z}\cap[a,b]}g(k)\leq\int_{a-1}^{b}g(k)\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}k.

Lemma B.6
.

Fix a real number
d\notin\mathbb{Z}_{\leq 0}
and let
q=e^{-\varepsilon}
. Then, there exists constants
C_{1},C_{2}
depending only on
d
, such that

(B.16)

\left|\Gamma_{q}(d+\mathrm{i}y)\right|\leq C_{1}e^{-C_{2}|y|},

for all
\varepsilon>0
and
y\in[-\varepsilon^{-1}\pi,\varepsilon^{-1}\pi]
.

Proof.

We prove the result for
d>1
as this also implies the general case by means of the functional relation for the
q
-Gamma function (
B.13
). We also assume that
y\geq 0
with no loss of generality, since
\overline{\Gamma_{q}(z)}=\Gamma_{q}(\overline{z})
.

Define the function

(B.17)

\begin{split}\phi_{\varepsilon}(d,y)&\coloneqq-\frac{\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}}{\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}y}\log\left|1-e^{-\varepsilon(d+\mathrm{i}y)}\right|\\
&=-\frac{\varepsilon e^{-\varepsilon d}\sin(\varepsilon y)}{1+e^{-2\varepsilon d}-2e^{-\varepsilon d}\cos(\varepsilon y)},\end{split}

so that

(B.18)

\frac{\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}}{\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}y}\log\left|\Gamma_{q}(d+\mathrm{i}y)\right|=\sum_{k\geq 0}\phi_{\varepsilon}(d+k,y).

A simple inspection of the derivative of
\phi_{\varepsilon}(d,y)
shows that it is a strictly decreasing function in the
d
variable for
d>0
, whenever
y\in(0,\varepsilon^{-1}\pi)
. We can therefore compare the summation in the right hand side of (
B.18
) with the integral of
\phi_{\varepsilon}(d+k,y)
over
k
using
lemma

B.5
, obtaining inequalities

(B.19)

G_{\varepsilon}(d-1,y)\leq\sum_{k\geq 0}\phi_{\varepsilon}(d+k,y)\leq G_{\varepsilon}(d,y),

where

(B.20)

\begin{split}G_{\varepsilon}(d,y)&\coloneqq\int_{0}^{\infty}\phi_{\varepsilon}(d+k,y)\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}k\\
&=\arctan\left(\cot(\varepsilon y)-\frac{e^{-\varepsilon d}}{\sin(\varepsilon y)}\right)-\arctan(\cot(\varepsilon y)).\end{split}

Here the equality in the second line follows from an exact evaluation of the integral. A display of inequalities (
B.19
) is given in
fig.

6
(b), where we set
d=1/2
. When
d>0
, the function
y\mapsto G_{\varepsilon}(d,y)
is convex in the region
y\in[0,\varepsilon^{-1}\pi]
, it reaches the value 0 only at
y=0
and
y=\varepsilon^{-1}\pi
and it has a unique minimum at
y_{*}=\varepsilon^{-1}\arccos(e^{-\varepsilon d})
, where it attains the value
-\arctan\left(e^{-\varepsilon d}(1-e^{-2\varepsilon d})^{1/2}\right)\sim-\pi/2
. Moreover, always from the explicit expression, we see that, at
y=0
,
G_{\varepsilon}
possesses the Taylor expansion
G_{\varepsilon}(d,y)=-y\frac{\varepsilon}{1-e^{-\varepsilon d}}+o(y)
. All these properties imply that, when
d>0
, there exist constants
c_{1},c_{2}
, possibly dependent on
d
but independent of
\varepsilon
, such that

(B.21)
